PREFACE 

The Latter-day Saints' Welfare Plan, divinely inspired, is now under way-in the wards, stakes and missions of the Church. The rate of its progress will depend to a large degree upon the actual support given it by the Quorums of the Priesthood. It seemed wise, therefore, to devote the quorum class periods for one year to a consideration of the meaning, problems and opportunities of the Plan.

The chapters of this study course have been prepared by three capable men, bearers of the Priesthood: 

Chapters 1 to 5 and 29 to 33, inclusive, by E. Cecil

McGavin; Chapters 6 to 16, inclusive, by Dr. Dilworth 

Walker; Chapters 17 to 28, inclusive, by Dr. George 

Stewart, under the supervision of the Quorum of the Twelve and with the final editorial assistance of the 

General Church Welfare Committee. 

It will be noted under Part IV that the illustrations have been drawn mainly from one section, the intermountain West. This has been done partly because the majority of the members of the Church live in this section, but chiefly because a nation-wide or world-wide survey would require space beyond the limits set for this book. However, every quorum, wherever located, may extend the facts and discussions to cover their own conditions.

The fact that several authors, with different styles of treatment, have presented the main phases of the subject, should make this study course more interesting. 

The Priesthood should be acquainted with the material contained in this study course. 

It has permanent value, and the volume could profitably be found in every Latter-day Saint library. It is earnestly hoped that the labors here presented may be acceptable to the Lord and useful to the body of the Priesthood. 

May the blessings of the Lord rest upon the 

Priesthood of Latter-day Israel.

RUDGER CLAWSON, 

President of the Council of the Twelve.

Chapter 

"But if any provide not for his own, and especially for those of his own house, he hath denied the faith, and is worse than an infidel.' (I Timothy, 5:8.) ?? "Now, I, the Lord, am not well pleased with the inhabitants of Zion, for there are idlers among them; and their children are also growing up in wickedness; they also seek not earnestly the riches of eternity, but their eyes are full of greediness. "These things ought not to be, and must be done away from among them.” (D. and C. 68:31-32.) "I, the Lord, stretched out the heavens, and built the earth, my very handiwork, and all things therein are mine; "And it is my purpose to provide for my saints, for all things are mine. “But it must needs be done in mine own way; and behold this is the way that I, the Lord, have decreed to provide for my saints, that the poor shall be exalted, in that the rich are made low; "For the earth is full, and there is enough and to spare; yea, I prepared all things, and have given unto the children of men to be agents unto themselves." (D. & C. 104:14-17.)

Guided by the revelation of the Lord, the Church since its organization has been admonished to be mindful of the needy and the unfortunate: "And let every man esteem his brother as himself, and practice virtue and holiness before me. "And again I say unto you, let every man esteem his brother as himself. "For what man among you having twelve sons, and is no respector of them, and they serve him obediently, and he saith unto the one; Be thou clothed in rags and sit thou there-and looketh upon his sons and saith I am just? "Behold, this I have given unto you as a parable, and it is even as I am. I say unto you, be one; and if ye are not one ye are not mine." (D. & C. 38:24-27.) "And also, ye yourselves will succor those that stand in need of your succor; ye will administer of your substance unto him that standeth in need; and ye will not suffer that the beggar putteth up his petition to you in vain, and turn him out to perish. "Perhaps thou shalt say: The man has brought upon himself the misery; therefore I will stay my hand and will not give unto

him of my food, nor impart unto him of my substance that he may not suffer, for his punishments are just- "But I say unto you, O man, whosoever doeth this the same hath great cause to repent; and except he repenteth of that which he hath done he perisheth forever, and hath no interest in the kingdom of God. "For behold, are we not all beggars? Do we not all depend upon the same Being, even God, for all the substance which we have, for both food and raiment, and for gold, and for silver, and for all the riches which we have of every kind?" (Book of Mormon, Mosiah 4:16-19.) 

At the same time the Lord has given a warning to those who are in unfortunate circumstances lest they be overcome by greediness, selfishness and idleness. "Wo unto you poor men, whose hearts are not broken, whose spirits are not contrite, and whose bellies are not satisfied, and whose hands are not stayed from laying hold upon other men's goods, whose eyes are full of greediness, and who will not labor with your own hands!" (D. & C. 56:17.)

All during the early gathering of the saints into the communities of Ohio, Missouri and Illinois, and throughout the pioneering period that resulted in the great western migration to and the colonization of the intermountain valleys, each community was made selfsustaining by applying the simple principles of brotherhood, thrift, honesty, and industry as enunciated in the teachings of the Gospel, such as those just referred to The rugged virtues of these early pioneers are well attested by the institutions they founded through cooperative effort, and stand today as monuments to their integrity. Charity "drives" were unknown, infirmaries did not exist, irrigation systems were developed as community enterprises, public roads came into being largely as the result of the "poll tax" each man was required to work out on the roads each year, and as a result, in these thriving and industrious communities where each was concerned about the welfare of all, penal institutions were little needed.

The Women's Relief Society organizations and the brethren of the Priesthood Quorums directed by the bishop of the ward ministered comfort to the sick, aided the unfortunate to provide a living and assisted in laying away the dead. The institution of the Fast Day and the resulting contributions provided the means for supporting the indigent.

With the advance of civilization, life among the Latter-day Saints became more complex; and while the methods of dealing with economic problems changed to meet the new conditions, the same early principles remained fixed and unchanged.

A great economic depression has engulfed the world since 1929. Many men who had been employed for years were thrown out of work as the social order changed. The savings and resources of those thrown out of employment vanished and there were widespread conditions of actual want and distress. 

The Federal government undertook to give relief to the unemployed and the needy. It has spent millions to finance make-work projects, which were often. only temporary and threatened with curtailment and suspension.

In 1935, a survey was made throughout the Church to ascertain the relief conditions of the members. Many of the stakes of the Church had provided, within their own organziations, experimental plans designed to assist the destitute and the unemployed to provide as far as possible their own sustenance through their own efforts. These activities and plans were carefully studied by the First Presidency and the General Authorities, who formulated a plan which was intended when put into operation to meet these conditions that existed among Church members.

Announcement of the Plan 

The plan was to be known as the Church Welfare Plan. It was instituted to enable the members of the 

Church to "help themselves." The setting for the historical announcement of the plan was the Annual Conference of the Church held in April, 1936, before a great audience assembled in the Tabernacle on Temple 

Square in Salt Lake City.

The official announcement from the First Presi dency follows: 

"An Important Message on Relief”

To the Presidents of Stakes and Bishoprics of the Church: 

Dear Brethren:

Pursuant to the statements made at the special Priesthood meeting of the Semi-Annual Conference last October, the First Presidency, through the Presiding Bishopric, made a survey of relief conditions in the Church as of last September. (Then follows statistics from September, 1935, survey.)

This makes clear the size of the problem which is involved in meeting the relief needs of Church members. The curtailment of 

Federal aid which is now forecast, makes it imperative that the Church shall, so far as it is able, meet this emergency. 

To enable the Church to do this, the following principles are laid down as guides: 

1. Fast offerings must be increased to an amount equaling one dollar per Church member per year. This is an amount within the reach of every head of family and every single person in the Church. Those who can give more should do so. 

2. Tithing should be fully paid, where possible, in cash, and where cash payment is not possible, then payment is to be made in kind. 

3. The Ward authorities, the Relief Society, and the Priesthood quorum organizations must exert the greatest possible effort to see that fast offerings and tithing are fully paid.

4. Upon Ward teachers, personal welfare committees of Priesthood quorums, and the Relief Society, must rest the prime responsibility for discovering and appraising the wants of the needy of the Ward. These wants must be administered to under and in accordance with the regular rules and through the regular organizations of the Church. 

5. Every Bishop should aim to have accumulated by next 

October Conference sufficient food and clothes to provide for every needy family in his Ward during the coming winter. The Relief 

Society must cooperate in this work by directing and assisting the needy sisters of the Ward in drying and preserving fruits and vegetables, providing clothing and bedding, etc. 

6. Every Bishop and every President of a Stake must keep constantly before himself, that other Wards and Stakes may be more needy than his own, and therefore that even though his own Ward or Stake may not need the whole he is to collect on fast offerings, nevertheless he must collect the full sum in order that the sum not needed for the needs of his own Ward or Stake may be passed on to places where it is needed. 

The problem of Church relief is Church-wide; it is to be accomplished through local unit organizations and operations. It will be the business and responsibility of the Bishop and his Ward organizations-Priesthood, quorum, auxiliaries, Relief Society-to see that the full relief collections of the Ward are made. 

7. Relief is not to be normally given as charity; it is to be distributed for work or service rendered. All members of the Church must cooperate to this end.

The Church itself will be prepared to assist to the utmost extent possible in providing work on its own properties for its unemployed members, and also in providing other work in wisely rehabilitating ranches, farms, gardens, and orchards that may be used to furnish foodstuffs for those in need. 

No pains must be spared to wipe out all feeling of diffidence, embarrassment, or shame on the part of those receiving relief; the 

Ward must be one great family of equals. The spiritual welfare of those on relief must receive especial care and be earnestly and prayerfully fostered. A system which gives relief for work or service will go far to reaching these ends. 

8. The work of directing and coordinating all this work will be in the hands of the Presiding Bishopric of the Church. The 

First Presidency will appoint a Church Relief Committee to assist the Presiding Bishopric in their work. 

9. It will be observed that the foregoing general principles call only for the operation of regular Ward and Stake organizations. Some supplemental, coordinating and grouping organization

may be necessary as the plan is more fully developed. The regular 

Church organization, set up under revelations from the Lord, was planned by Him to meet every emergency coming to human beings. 

The Church organization will meet the present grave economic crisis if the members of the Church will but live fully and conscientiously the Gospel. 

10. For the present and pending further developments in the working out of the Church plan, all persons engaged in W. P. A. projects should endeavor to retain their positions, being scrupulously careful to do an honest day's work for a day's pay. 

11. Whether we shall now take care of our own Church members in need and how fully, depends wholly and solely upon the faith and works of the individual Church members. If each Church member meets his full duty and grasps his full opportunity for blessing, full necessary relief will be extended to all needy Church members; in so far as individual members fail in their duty and opportunity, by that much will the relief fall short. 

Speaking to the Saints in the early days of the 

Church, the Lord said: (Doctrine and Covenants, Sec. 56:14-20.) "Behold, thus saith the Lord unto my people, you have many things to do and to repent of; for behold, your sins have come up unto me, and are not pardoned, because you seek to counsel in your own ways. "And your hearts are not satisfied. And ye obey not the truth but have pleasure in unrighteousness. "Wo unto you rich men, that will not give your substance to the poor, for your riches will canker your souls; and this shall be your lamentation in the day of visitation, and of judgment, and of indignations-The harvest is past, the summer is ended, and my soul is not saved! "Wo unto you poor men, whose hearts are not broken, whose spirits are not contrite, and whose bellies are not satisfied, and whose hands are not stayed from laying hold upon other men's goods, whose eyes are full of greediness, who will not labor with your own hands! "But blessed are the poor who are pure in heart, whose hearts are broken, and whose spirits are contrite, for they shall see the kingdom of God coming in power and great glory unto their deliverance; for the fatness of the earth shall be theirs. "For, behold, the Lord shall come, and his recompense shall be with him, and he shall reward every man, and the poor shall rejoice;

"And their generations shall inherit the earth from generation to generation, for ever and ever. And now I make an end of speaking unto you. Even so. Amen." 

Faithfully your Brethren,

HEBER J. GRANT, J. REUBÉN CLARK, JR., DAVID O. McKAY. 

AN INSPIRED PLAN: "And this is the ensample unto them, that they shall speak as they are moved upon by the Holy Ghost. "And whatsoever they shall speak when moved upon by the Holy Ghost, shall be scripture, shall be the will of the Lord, shall be the mind of the Lord, shall be the word of the Lord, shall be the voice of the Lord and the power of God unto salvation." (D. & 

C. 68:3-4)

Throughout the Church faithful and thoughtful people hailed the plan as a worthy program, founded upon the wisdom of God, while the people of the world looked on with amazement. "It is my testimony to you," declared President J. Reuben 

Clark, Jr., in a general conference of the Church, "That President

Grant was inspired to begin this work and this plan, and it stands on an equality with any of our Church organizations." (Special 

Meeting Stake Presidents, October, 1936.) 

President David O. McKay declared in the Salt 

Lake Regional meeting, February, 1937: "The Church Security Plan has not come up as a mushroom over night. It is the result of inspiration, and that inspiration has come from the Lord... Those who have selffishness in their hearts would like to see it fail, but it is not going to fail.” *

Meanwhile it embodied principles which have guided the Church from its beginning. 

Plan: 

Managing Director, Harold B. Lee, said of the "The Church Security plan is not something new to the Church, neither does it contemplate a new organization within the Church *The name "Church Security Plan" was given to this movement in the early developments but was changed to "Church Wel fare Plan" at the General Conference, April, 1938. 2

to carry out its purposes, but rather it is the expression of a philosophy that is as old as the Church itself, incorporated into a program of stimulation and cooperation to meet the demands of Church members in the solution of present day economic problems. The words "Church Security" describe the underlying hope of our leaders in this program, rather than name a movement separate and distinct from the already established organizations of the Church." (Improvement Era, June, 1937.)

Church Organization and the Plan. Under divine revelation the administrative responsibility for the welfare of the Church rests upon the First Presidency for the Church as a whole; upon the Stake Presidency for each group of Wards organized as a Stake; and upon the Ward Bishopric for each Ward. Indeed upon the 

Ward Bishoprics, fathers of their flocks, having intimate knowledge of the conditions of the group for which they are responsible, rests the primary burden of putting the Welfare Plan into operation. In turn these men have the right to claim full and active cooperation from the Priesthood quorums and Relief Societies, both of which are charged with the responsibility of helping their fellow men.

The burden of actually carrying the plan into effective operation is upon the quorums of the Priesthood -associations of brethren-expected by the Lord to care for one another's welfare, both temporal and spiritual.

More will be said in a later chapter as to the relationship to and the responsibility of Priesthood quorums and Relief Society organizations in the Church Welfare Plan. 

Publicity and Responsibility. From the pulpit and the press of the world have come glowing tributes to the integrity, independence, and ingenuity of our people as evidenced by the stated purposes and objectives of the program. The L. D. S. Church Welfare Plan has

been received favorably by the world. The New York 

Times devoted much valuable space to a discussion of the novel project, and carried to the world pictures of the appointed leaders of the Plan. The March of Time secured an authentic newsreel story of the much discussed plan, which was shown in hundreds of theatres throughout the land.

Typical of the publicity the movement received, the Catholic Worker, in the issue of November, 1936, said: "The Church of the Latter-day Saints has set an example worthy of imitation by their Catholic fellow country-men. It has set up a system under which the curse of idleness would be done away with, the evils of a dole abolished, and independence, industry, thrift, and self-respect once more be established among our people. "It has accomplished this great task by calling upon every man, woman, and child in their communities to consider the we fare of others about them as their own, and to be willing to work for others not related by ties other than Christian fellowship. It has called upon every man, woman, and child to be personally responsible for the amelioration of the present crisis."

When the eyes of the world were turned upon the Church and its brotherly methods of solving the economic ills of the time, the responsibility for success was increased. The obligation was placed doubly upon every member and quorum to rally to the cause and show the world the spirit of the "Mormon” pioneersa spirit which the changing social and economic order had pushed into the background. 

The Church authorities have continued to explain, encourage and inspire the members of the Church to give their full support to the deserving plan. The 

Christmas Message from The First Presidency was per meated with the spirit of the welfare plan. One paragraph may be reproduced here:

"As Christmas approaches let us double our efforts to bring faith, happiness, and cheer into the lives of our fellow-men. Let those who are blessed with comforts and luxuries share with those who are less fortunate, not with an air of superiority, but with an attitude of appreciation, expressive of the feeling that it is a privilege to invite another out of the shade and cold of penury and discouragement into the sunshine of kindness and brotherly love." (Improvement Era, December, 1936.)

President Grant expressed his approval of the accomplishments of the program during the first year of its operation, as follows: "It gives me very great pleasure indeed to announce that we have had a remarkable and splendid increase in the tithes of the people since this Security Plan was first announced. We have also had, as I remember it, fully one hundred per cent increase in the amount of Fast Day offerings paid. The Church is growing in power and ability and strength along all lines. The growth in tithing and donations tells its own story. "I rejoice in this and I have rejoiced in the very many splendid and remarkable meetinghouses we are building. The Church has furnished a great deal of employment by increasing the amount they are giving to the people when they erect meetinghouses." (Improvement Era, March, 1937.)

PROBLEMS AND QUESTIONS 

1. Consult the Improvement Era for April, 1937, for a sample of the favorable publicity the project has received. From the same publication read what the March of Time said of it. 

2. What advantages does the Church have in launching such a welfare program, compared with other societies or groups? 

3. Read the Christmas Message of the First Presidency (Im provement Era, December, 1936) and see what is prescribed for the social, economic, and spiritual ills of the day. 

4. Contrast the instructions given in the principles number six and number seven of the official message of the First Presidency, with the methods of public relief agencies. 

5. How is it possible for the Church through its organization under this plan to care for its people with far less outlay of money from its general funds than would be possible for a public charity organization? 

6. Has a family fulfilled the law of fasting when $1.00 per capita per year has been paid? (See number one, First Presidency's 

April, 1936, Message.)

Chapter 

According to the First Presidency in their statement of October, 1936: "The announced objective set for the Church under this program was to provide by October 1, 1936 by a wholly voluntary system of gifts in cash or kind, sufficient food, clothing, and bedding to supply through the coming winter every needy and worthy Church family unable to furnish these for itself, in order that no member of the Church should suffer in these times of stress and emergency. 'Our primary purpose was to set up, in so far as it might be possible, a system under which the curse of idleness would be done away with, the evils of a dole abolished, and independence, industry, thrift and self-respect be once more established amongst our people. The aim of the Church is to help the people to help themselves. Work is to be re-enthroned as the ruling principle of the lives of our Church membership. "Our great leader, Brigham Young, under similar conditions, said: 'Set the poor to work-setting out orchards, splitting rails, digging ditches, making fences, or anything useful, and so enable them to buy meal and flour and the necessities of life.' 

The Threefold Objective. The threefold objectives of the Church Welfare Plan are: 

1. To help feed, clothe, and house the needy members of the Church. 

2. To help the unemployed who are capable of labor, to find employment. 

3. To help raise the standard of living and of those near the margin of relief, the ones who are barely subsisting.

These objectives may be summarized as unemployment relief, self-support, and progressive improvement of existing conditions.

Concerning this subject Dr. Henry C. Link wrote in his popular book, The Return to Religion: "The depression made many independent people temporarily helpless. But to convert such difficulties into a long drawn-out or permanent program of dependence, whether done by a psychologist or by the state, is a crime against the individual and against those who must support him. It is comparable to encouraging the person to sell his most precious birthright for a mess of pottage, and it robs society of the kind of people who make a self-sustaining social order possible."¹

Specific Objectives in the Plan. In the foregoing statement three broad general objectives of the Church Welfare Plan are given. Attainment of these objectives requires the combined efforts of individuals, families, auxiliary organizations, Priesthood groups, and Church units.

In the following article written by Dr. John A. 

Widtsoe for the Improvement Era for June, 1938, there will be found specific objectives that may be immediately applied in every home and community of the Church. "Eternal principles must guide us in our efforts, else the results will be temporary. That is the first and most important consideration. "WORK, or industry, is the basis of economic safety. True wealth is produced by the intelligent application of human labor to the resources offered by earth, and in no other way. All that we have to sustain our lives and to give us comfort is the product of human labor. The first step in any program for economic welfare must be to put every person to work to produce wealth from existing natural resources. It is the beginning and the end of any sound economic system. "None should be exempt from work, if able-bodied. Naturally, the kind of labor should be fitted to the strength and ability of the individual. Idleness at any period of life invites uselessness, unhappiness, and evil. Productive labor, with opportunity for ample leisure and rest, should be the desire of all-men and women, young "The Return to Religion p. 135. The Macmillian Company.

and old. The conservation or use or human energy lies at the bottom of spiritual as well as material welfare. "THRIFT is a companion principle to WORK. Wealth, won by intelligent industry, whether much or little, should be used with discriminating care, with economy in view. It must be cherished as the product of life's best possessions, which are: Opportunity for industry, strength to labor, and time in which to work. Wealth must not be wasted, or spent in useless pursuits. Waste is an evil second only to idleness. "A third principle completes the supporting pillars of a safe ecenomic structure. Men must live within the means obtained from their productive labor. That is, DEBT must be avoided. It should be abhorred. The burden of repayment is always heavier than that of production. He who contracts a debt enters into partnership with an enemy. Economic prosperity requires that men go without things rather than to go into debt for them. "Add to these principles FAITH and trust in the Lord, OBEDI ENCE to His commands, and PRAYER to Him for help, and we may be certain that success will smile upon our efforts to raise all Church members into positions of economic prosperity. Let it ever be remembered that only the Lord can give security to human kind. Man can only advance one another's welfare. Therefore, the name Church Welfare Plan is preferable to Church Security 

Program. "Undoubtedly, if all men feared God, used their energies in productive labor, were thrifty, and lived within their incomes, there would and could be no economic depressions. Any other procedure of living causes financial panics, breeds poverty, and makes charity necessary. "These principles of economic security and progress must be applied by each individual for himself in his life's endeavors. 

SELF-EFFORT is the key to self-development and personal progress. There must be no needless leaning on others for support. 

The only dependence should be self-dependence, under God's will. 

Self-help brings greatest happiness. "To prevent an economic depression or to rise out of one is the personal concern of every individual, not of a central agency, however rich and powerful. The long history of the race shows that individual self-effort, coupled with self-reliance, is always victorious. 

If it does not yield great wealth, it makes big men. 

The Order of Responsibility for Man's Economic 

Welfare. "Should the earnestly striving individual fail in his quest for a living, he may properly turn to others for assistance. His first

appeal should be to those who are connected with him by the intimate ties of family relationship. Indeed, the welfare of an individual must be, in a well-ordered society, the responsibility of the family group. Families should take pride in caring for their own. A family capable of taking care of its own should be ashamed to receive help from an outside agency. "Should the family be unable to give the necessary help, then it would be proper to request aid from the immediate community. 

Appeal to the State should be a last resort. "The order of responsibility for man's economic welfare is then, as follows: the individual; the family, the community; the State. "Nothing can be more disastrous than to have every failing individual look for help toward some far-distant central agencya great white Father, so to speak. The individual problems of life are best solved individually-for the safety of the man and the group.

A Welfare Program-An Opportunity Program. 

"In all our thinking on these matters, the duty of the strong must be kept in mind. He who has, must give to him who has not. 

He who is blessed with power or wealth should delight to help those who cannot well stand alone. But his help should be directed toward increasing the strength of the weak rather than by constant giving, making of them chronic economic cripples. A welfare program should be an opportunity program. "There is much idle talk about a new day that requires new ways of living. True, our age has marvelous possessions for human comfort, never known before. However, the laws under which these inventions and discoveries may be used are identical with those recognized as vital in ages past. The telephone does not abrogate the law of honesty; the radio does not transcend love. 

In the new day there is but one day-the day of justice and righteousness. Thus, for example, work has been, is, and always will be the only means of producing true wealth; and thrift and economy are as necessary today as in any other age. "New tasks do confront us, but they are accomplished with the old tools. There will always be new frontiers to pass. Now that the desert has been tamed, the problem is to live happily in the desert with our new-found knowledge. Now that we no longer travel with covered wagons, we must learn how to live well with automobiles at our command. In every such changing aspect of life we shall and must be directed by unchanging, timeless guides of conduct. "The call to service in the Church Welfare Plan merely means

that we bring into renewed and more vigorus action external, conquering principles. In no other way can we secure certain progress or enduring happiness. In no other way can we solve the problems of this or any other day. Every Latter-day Saint should understand, respect, and practice these principles, upon which the material happiness of the world depends." (Improvement Era, June, 1938.)

Essential Steps. The essential steps to realize the objectives of the Plan, are: "1. Temporary sustenance in the way of food, fuel, bedding, clothing, shelter, etc., must be supplied to the needy as far as possible through their own efforts. "2. Through Church agencies, Ward Employment Committees, Priesthood Welfare Committees, Stake Work Directors, or Regional Employment Offices, and in cooperation with public employment agencies of the county, state and Federal Government, every effort should be made to find jobs for the unemployed of each community. (Details of the organization are given in Chapter 3.) "3. Ways and means of permanently stabilizing and rehab ilitating needy individuals should be studied and applied in each community. "4. Encourage and foster the establishment of private or cooperative industries that would increase the incomes of individual families or furnish additional employment." (Manuscript Handbook of Instructions.)

The Christmas Message of the First Presidency, 

1936, said of this subject: "To accept this eternal truth, the saviorship of Jesus Christ is the first and most important step along the pathway that leads to the comfort and happiness of mankind. "The second step is victory over weakness and sin. With

Christ as our supreme Exemplar, working for the glory of God, we obtain power to triumph over selfishness; to gain control over hitherto unrestrained passions; to govern appetite, envy, hatred, and worldly ambition-things which arouse not peace and goodwill, but enmity in the hearts of men. At his birth Christ not only heralded goodwill toward men, but also pointed the way later when 

He said: 'He that will lose his life for my sake shall find it.' Goodwill will never exist permanently either among individuals or nations, until men learn to submerge self for the good and happiness of others.

"Such is the objective of the Church Security Plan." (Improvement Era, December, 1936.)

The Christmas Message of the First Presidency, 

1937, continued the thought: "Chief among these (Latter-day Saints' devotion to Christ's principles and outstanding achievements in Church projects) is the Church Security Plan, which calls for unselfish devotion by every member, and for much time, energy, and leadership especially on the part of general, regional, and stake committees. The first and immediate objective has been achieved—that of supplying sufficient food, clothing, and shelter for every needy person during the winter months. In response to the appeal of the leaders of the Church, regional and stake storehouses have been established in which are stored thousands of pounds of foodstuffs, articles of clothing, bedding, etc., ready for proper distribution under the direction of Ward bishoprics and Relief Society sisters. "But giving something for nothing is contrary to the fundamental teachings of the Church. The real purpose of the Church 

Security Plan is to assist each individual to secure independence, to help make him self-supporting, to replace idleness with thrift and productivity. Here, again, Jesus' teachings are applicable. His plan was to make the individual right, and then the group of many such individuals, or society, will be right. Make the individual contented, and social unrest with its attendant evils will be greatly minimized. "To accomplish these things may require many years, but what has already been achieved by cooperative effort of the people is most encouraging. God bless you for your united response, and your willing service, as evidenced in your contribution of time, energy, and means!" (Improvement Era, January, 1938.)

The Major Welfare Objective. A major objective is to make it possible for the Church eventually to take care of all its needy, faithful members and to assist them in becoming financially independent.

A bulletin issued by the Presiding Bishopric states: "Every member of the Church has a place in the plan. The objectives will be reached only when and to the extent that all participate. The Church Welfare program begins in the home, around the fireside, with family prayer, and a common understanding among the members of the family that enables them to cooperate

in the solution of family problems by the application of such proven principles as faith, honesty, thrift, industry, kindness, and consideration for each other. It moves forward through the payment of tithing, the observance of the law of fasting, and the contribution of fast offerings and donations. It contemplates the utmost activity on the part of all who hold the Priesthood." (Improvement Era, June, 1938.)

Dr. Francis W. Kirkham has written on this subject: "First, let us proceed, as our Church Security Plan is now organized, toward its first objective. Encourage all to give according to their ability and distribute these surplus products according to need, each person receiving to compensate by giving in exchange his labor for the good of all. This is a marvelous opportunity and an unusual privilege for both the giver and the one who receives. "Second, create work projects for our members who are in greatest need, and who have the least ability to compete in the labor market. The products of this industry may in part be turned over to the Bishop's Storehouse to be distributed according to need, as many of these workers will be furnished clothing, food, and shelter by order from the bishops. "Third, let each quorum try to place each member at some profitable service, or work for money, or at the making of an article or the growing of farms and range products to be sold on the market for money. Many opportunities will be found for mutual cooperation and help, exchanging work for lease of land, or for the advance of money for seed, and other forms of assistance. "Fourth, make an appeal, under the direction of stake and regional committees, to all of us, to invest money in new industries not only for profit, but primarily for providing work for all. Even at the cost of a few cents more on the price of an article, we must purchase home-made goods, so that all may be employed. 

The Larger Program. This Church includes in its general program for human welfare of which the Welfare Plan is but a part, a vast field of social and cultural processes. Though the economic welfare of the members is a grave concern of the Church, a healthy society is the best means of serving such welfare. 

Our vision of social reform is much the same as that expressed by Dr. Henry C. Link:

"If Jesus were present today, judging by His works while living, He would house the destitute in the empty churches, He would inspire the employed to help the unemployed and the fortunate to share the sufferings of the less fortunate. He would approve the block or neighborhood relief plan. He would persuade the rich and the competent to give their money and experience to plans for wise employment. He would convert suffering into spiritual strength and personal self-reliance. He would help in material things, as 

He often did, but He would promise no security in a material world. 

To rich and poor alike He would preach the eternal values of a higher Kingdom as the key to a more abundant life in our temporal world. Instead of institutionalizing the weak He would humanize the strong." ""1 

The Priesthood Quorums Again. The General 

Priesthood authorities have given to the Church the Welfare Plan, which provides an instrumentality or vehicle, through which the Priesthood, in quorums and in territorial units, may apply its power to the solution of the major problem of modern society. The response and activity on the part of the Priesthood quorums, divinely appointed agencies in aiding God's purposes in bringing to pass the "immortality and the eternal life of man" will increase the success of the Plan.

Each quorum and every member thereof are charged with the duty of pushing this worthy movement forward. The challenge is to each member of the 

Church to catch the vision of the Welfare Plan and to help achieve its aims. As President John Taylor has said: "God feels interested in the welfare of the whole human family, and for this purpose He has established principles upon the earth which exist in the heavens." (J. D. 22:291.)

For all Men. The Welfare Plan is not selfishly designed to aid "Mormons" only. In every community region where the power inherent in Priesthood groups 

¹The Return to Religion, p. 139. The Macmillan Company.

is exercised to fulfill objectives of the Plan, the community at large will benefit. If the purchasing power of Latter-day Saints increases, the business and welfare of the community at large will be promoted. If quorum-motivated projects provide useful goods and services for economical consumption, benefits will be felt beyond Church confines. Others will be aided in their quest for welfare and happiness in direct proportion to our own success in working out the objectives of the 

Welfare Plan.

2. How does a well organized welfare program afford an ideal opportunity for the Priesthood quorums to function? 

3. Why should each person be his brother's keeper?

(b) to aid the unemployed find employment? (c) to help raise the standard of living of individual families now barely subsisting in your community?

ORGANIZATION OF THE WELFARE PLAN 

"So far as I can judge from what I have seen, the organization of the Mormons is the most nearly perfect piece of social mechanism with which I have ever, in any way, come in contact, excepting alone the German army." (Dr. Richard T. Ely, Harpers' Monthly, April, 1903.)

We of the Church do not except even the German army. Brigham Young has said: "Our ecclesiastical government is the government of heaven." 

There can be nothing worked out by man as perfect as that which God has ordained and revealed to his children.

An Integrated Society. The Church is an ideal institution for perfecting the Welfare Program, which is not a separate movement within the Church, but rather a program of activity for the Priesthood of the Church. 

Although the Priesthood must lead and bear responsibility for this great undertaking, all Church organizations and auxiliaries are expected to lend their energetic support as well as to direct their specific functions in terms of broad Church welfare. Likewise each family as a unit must employ the genius at its command in terms of the welfare objectives. 

Elder Henry D. Moyle, of the General Church

Welfare Committee, has written of the relationship existing between the family and the organizations of the Church: "Each head of a family, each president of a Relief Society, each president of a Priesthood quorum, each bishop of a Ward is now called upon to carry on the social security program of the

ORGANIZATION OF THE WELFARE PLAN 29 

Church, each in his or her own sphere of activity. The family, as a unit, through the united wisdom and efforts of its members, seeks first to solve its own problems, seeking assistance, when necessary, through the Priesthood quorum, the Relief Society, and the ward. 

When each of these units has exhausted its ingenuity and resources, the reserves of the stake are utilized. Cooperative efforts between stakes has been made possible through our regional organizations. 

The General Committee finally seeks to apportion such surpluses as some regions are able to accumulate, among stakes and regions less fortunate. Thus the combined work and contributions of the entire Church are conducted from the place of plenty to the place of need, and the security of the humblest individual is guaranteed by the combined efforts of all." (Improvement Era, December, 1937.)

Now let us analyze carefully the organization, divinely given and designed to cope successfully with the economic problems confronting us at the present time. I.

Coordination with the Relief Society. If the Welfare Program is to accomplish all that its leaders have contemplated, there must be perfect cooperation, loyalty, and unity in all departments and organizations of the Church. Only by such united efforts can we wage a successful war against our common enemies-unemployment, want, and despair. However, in this task of co-ordinating all the units of the Church, the Relief Society marches side by side with the Priesthood quorums. This worthy organization, more than any other, is in a position to render valuable service in the realm of welfare work.

This auxiliary organization must give continued support and assistance to the Priesthood. The women of the Church, wives and daughters of Priesthood hold-

ORGANIZATION OF THE WELFARE PLAN 31 

ers, share the obligation with the Priesthood quorums of relieving the distress of the needy. 

On the day this worthy society was organized the Prophet Joseph Smith said in illustrating the object of the society, "that the society of sisters might provoke the brethren to good works, in looking to the wants of the poor, searching after objects of charity and in administering to their wants. . . . This charitable society is according to your natures, it is natural for females to have feelings of charity. You are now placed in a position where you can act according to those sympathies which God has planted in your bosoms. If you live up to these principles, how great and glorious. If you live up to your privileges, the angels cannot be constrained from being your associates. This society is not only to relieve the poor but to save souls"... (Minutes of first meeting of Society held in Nauvoo, March 17, 1842.)

Thus the facilities and experiences of the Relief Society are such as to qualify them to cope successfully with the "relief” phases of the Welfare problem. 

It devolves, therefore, upon the Priesthood to lead out, and with the Relief Society to develop positive aspects of welfare as needed in our changing modern life. 

The Ward Welfare Committee RELATION OF PRIESTHOOD QUORUMS TO THE CHURCH WELFARE ORGANIZATION THE WARD EMPLOYMENT COMMITTEE THE CHAIRMAN

Relief Society Work Director. 

The Ward Welfare organization consists of the bishopric as advisers, with one of the bishopric to be named as chairman, a ward work director to assume active chairmanship-if desired by the bishop-the chairman of the Personal Welfare Committee of High Priests, Seventies, and Elders groups in the Ward, the leader of the Adult Aaronic Priesthood class, the Ward Relief Society president and the Relief Society work director.

This small unit is of utmost importance. It is the hub about which the whole activity of the program revolves. It is a staff to aid the Bishopric and the Priesthood quorums of the Ward. Though the Relief Society is well represented in the Ward Committee, the quorum responsibility remains. This Ward agency is an effective method of co-ordinating the work of the various quorums of the Ward in their mission of mercy and brotherhood. It should meet weekly.

It is the duty of the ward committee to make and keep up-to-date a careful family survey, particularly of those families in need of some assistance in order to have before it constantly the economic status of each individual in the Ward. With this information at hand, it is the business of the committee to provide themselves with every device and ingenuity at their command and in co-ordination with the Stake Welfare committee to reach the obiectives of the Welfare Plan, as far as

ORGANIZATION OF THE WELFARE PLAN 33 

possible in that Ward. Jobs must be solicited for the unemployed, work projects developed to produce commodities and to provide work for those who are unemployed. It should be the endeavor of the Ward Committee to urge that every Priesthood group in the Ward have a productive project of some kind, to assist in providing the necessities required by needy quorum members.

The commodities thus provided and stored in central storehouses established by co-operation with other wards in the stake or with other stakes in the region can be can be efficiently distributed to needy families upon bishop's orders to reach the first objectives of the program.

Should the stake storehouse lack in having a supply or a variety of commodities that have been produced by the Welfare Program sufficient to meet the requirements of the wards in any storehouse district, these additional commodities from the surpluses of other regions may be had by requisition from the office of the General Committee in Salt Lake City.

The Stake Committee. In each stake a supervisory committee is designated as the Stake Welfare Committee to co-ordinate the work of wards, priesthood groups or quorums, and Relief Society organizations, and to stimulate in every such group, activities that will accomplish the utmost in looking after the welfare of the individuals of each group.

This committee is composed of the following: The 

Stake Presidency as advisers, member of stake presidency as chairman, the chairman of the Bishops' Executive Council, President of the Stake Relief Society, Stake Work Director, Stake Clerk or assistant as secretary. If the work of the member of the stake presi-

dency is such as to prevent his giving this work the leadership it requires, the stake Work Director or some other competent person should be delegated the authority to serve as the executive head of the Committee with the member of the stake presidency serving in an ex-officio capacity. To be effective, this committee should meet together at least once each month. "Projects for the economic welfare of its members, an important quorum activity, should be undertaken in harmony with the efforts of the Church Security Program. For that reason, and to prevent unnecessary duplication, the member of the stake presidency who is chairman of the stake security committee should be the chairman of the stake Melchizedek Priesthood committee. The two committees need not have the same membership; but the stake

Melchizedek Priesthood committee could sit in, as desired, with the stake security committee, to discuss projects of mutual interest to the two committees." (Improvement Era, January, 1938, “Instructions from Council of the Twelve.)

Under the Welfare Plan, the Melchizedek quorums are co-ordinated with the stake committee as well as with ward units. (a) The Bishops' Executive Council of each stake is expected to work in the closest harmony with the stake committee and is designed primarily to directly supervise the operation of bishops' storehouses that they may be organized to serve all or a part of the wards of a given territory as a storage and distribution center. Otherwise the bishops are to counsel together frequently to unify the work of the various wards within each stake and to establish uniform policies in dealing with welfare and unemployment problems. 

Regional Organization. To facilitate the work of the 

Church Welfare Plan, stakes within a given geographical area are grouped together into regions, with the stake presidents of the several stakes in the group comprising a regional executive council, with one of their

ORGANIZATION OF THE WELFARE PLAN 

355 number being named as Regional chairman and another as vice chairman.

The Regional Council is to function under the direction and supervision of the General Committee. 

1. To properly store in and distribute from suitable storehouse facilities any surplus commodities received from the stakes comprising the region, or to send the same to designated General Committee Storage centers where such surpluses are not needed by any stake in that region. 

2. To create a regional fast offering fund from surplus stake cash fast offering funds on hand in each stake at the end of the regular periods agreed upon by the Executive council and to disburse the same upon requisition of the stake presidents to stakes within the region in need of additional assistance or to the Presiding Bishop's Office at the end of each quarter, if such funds are not needed by any stake within that region. 

3. To work out cooperatively through the associated stakes a productive program each year that will approximate the probable needs of that region. 

4. To lay plans for efficiently and economically producing, storing and distributing surplus commodities within the region. 

5. To lay plans for improving the economic conditions within the region through the establishment of employment agencies, development of irrigation and reclamation projects, encouragement of exchanges between groups producing cooperatively, organizing and fostering agricultural or industrial projects in an effort to reduce the present relief load. 

6. To render to the office of the Presiding Bishopric and the General Committee a monthly statement of all cash received in and disbursed from the regional

storehouse to individual, stake, or to the General Committee Central Storage center.

The General Welfare Committee. To facilitate putting into effect the plan, a General Committee is organized whose functions are to represent the Presiding Bishopric in the detailed administrative work of coordinating and supervising the labors of the various regularly established Church organizations in their large and important operations in the Welfare Program. 

The work of the General Committee is divided under sub-committees: (a) Agriculture. This committee is expected to survey private and public land projects available with suitable water rights for purchase and settlement by Latter-day Saint families and to report the same to the proper Church officers; to determine the number of L. 

D. S. families desiring farm opportunities and to advise as to ways and means of placing such families on farms; to co-operate with agricultural colleges, county agents, farm organizations, etc., in the general improvement of farm practices and to study new uses and possible outlets for farm produce. (b) Finance. In order to legalize the business transactions necessary in carrying forward the work of the program, the finance committee has been incorporated as a non-profit legal entity known as the Co- Operative Security Corporation and is duly qualified to function in each state as it becomes necessary. (c) Industries and New Projects. The work on this committee consists mainly in originating and in studying the possbility of developing new projects or industries looking to the more permanent phases of the 

Church Welfare Plan. 

All propositions suggestive of this phase of the

ORGANIZATION OF THE WELFARE PLAN 37 

program submitted to the General Committee for consideration are referred to this sub-committee for study and report.

These committee members are called by the First Presidency to this responsibility to render a voluntary service for which they receive no compensation. 

All problems coming within the scope of these subcommittees are referred for study and report to the 

General Committee.

Weekly meetings of this committee are held with the First Presidency, Presiding Bishopric and advisers from the Council of the Twelve, the First Council of Seventy and the General Board of Relief Society for decision on all matters presented by the committee. 

Effects on the Welfare Organization. The Regional 

Welfare organization makes possible the utmost cooperation throughout the Church in the equitable distribution and efficient transportation of produce or other materials through centralized direction. 

The regional organization provides a convenient organization for making the quorum member conscious of the fact that his responsibility in the Welfare Plan is not limited by the narrow confines of his Ward or Stake, but extends to all the Church. 

There are two primary reasons for the regional organization. First, educational; second, administrative and distributional. In a region where one stake is slow in catching a vision of the Welfare Plan the more progressive stakes in that region can give assistance in getting the work started and seeing that it is properly done.

Moreover, the regional organization does not hinder or curtail the progress or initiative of any stake. 

On the other hand, it tends to encourage initiative, so

that one stake may excell in some particular project which may be adapted to the region. 

In pioneer times each ward had its own storehouse because of the limited facilities of transportation. 

But with our present highways and fast trucks one storehouse can serve a vast area. The sixteen Salt Lake stakes share one storehouse, a cooperative project in itself, which is a great saving in time and money over the old system of a storehouse in each ward. Four stakes use the storehouse in Ogden, while four stakes in southern California will share one storehouse.

Thus, in the organization of the Welfare Plan, every Church unit in the remotest part of the Church is made to feel its responsibility as a functional part of the whole Church organization, upon whom the Church must depend for the success of the Welfare Program. 

On the other hand, every Church unit is made to feel that it is supported by every other unit in the Church. Thus the weakest ward through its stake and regional officer may call for assistance from every strong ward in the Church.

When the organization of this plan is understood and the functions of each unit are clearly defined, no quorum, ward or stake will measure its responsibility by the number of needy within its own territorial boundaries, but rather by the opportunities for promoting fraternalism and for rendering service that are to be found therein.

Chapter 

"The Lord is hastening His work. He is at the helm; there is no mortal man at the helm of this work. It is true, the Lord uses such instruments as will be obedient to his commandments and laws to assist in accomplishing His purposes in the earth. "What I mean by the Holy Priesthood is, that authority which God has delegated to man, by which he may speak the will of God as if the angels were here to speak it themselves; by which men are empowered to bind on earth and it shall be bound in heaven, and to loose on earth and it shall be loosed in heaven; by which the words of man, spoken in the exercise of that power, become the word of the Lord, and the law of God unto the people, scripture, and divine commands. "Ours is the Church where law is dominant, but the law is the law of love.' (Joseph F. Smith, Gospel Doctrine, p. 168, 174, 178.)

Meaning of Priesthood. Priesthood is authority given to man to act for God. Since a man may become a partner with the Lord, through the Priesthood, in bringing temporal and spiritual salvation to himself and to his fellows, every member of the Church should be on the alert to secure this power and use it for human needs.

Inseparably connected with the Priesthood are divine powers, gifts, and blessings, which make this agency most effective in sponsoring the Welfare Program of the Church. Quorums may become sacred instruments through which the welfare work can be exalted to the realm of the spiritual. Every quorum member may likewise become a co-partner with the Lord in bringing temporal and spiritual peace, prosperity, and happiness to the human soul. He ceases to be a mere social worker bringing bread to the hungry. Clothed with the Priesthood he becomes a humble messenger, bearing eternal happiness to his fellows.

Majesty of the Priesthood. The holy character of the Priesthood is evident from the following words of the Lord to the Prophet Joseph Smith: "They who receive this Priesthood receive me. 

And he that receiveth me receiveth my Father's kingdom; therefore, all that my Father hath shall be given unto him." (D. & C. 84:35-39.) 

In the words of President Brigham Young: "The Priesthood of the Son of God, which we have in our midst, is a perfect order and system of government, and this alone can deliver the human family from all the evils which now afflict its members, and insure them happiness and felicity hereafter . . . it is the law by which the worlds are, were, and will continue for ever and ever. It is that system which brings worlds into existence and peoples them, gives them their revolutions—their days, weeks, months, years, their seasons and times, and by which they are rolled up as a scroll, as it were, and go into a higher state of existence.' (Discourses, p. 201)

Without the Priesthood there would be no Church. 

The Church is dependent upon this power. Since the opportunities and blessings of the Church are to be made universal, the Priesthood is given to all the worthy men and boys in the Church. There is no place in the 

Church for inactive persons. Therefore the Priesthood is given to all male members who will honor and magnify it. Its power should spread to the homes where the members holding the Priesthood preside over their families. Quorum members are instructed and encouraged to teach the Gospel in their homes and train their children to take an active part in the Church. In this way the quorum becomes a basic, powerful unit in the organization and mission of the Church, unifying the work of the territorial divisions of the Church: Ward and Stakes.

Law of the Priesthood. The Priesthood must be exercised in the spirit of love of the Lord and our fellow

men. It is a welding link uniting the men and boys who are honored and privileged to hold the Priesthood, into a brotherhood where perfect unity may be found. It is an eternal tie that binds its members in the strong bonds of brotherly devotion. Unless this spirit actuates its use, the Priesthood becomes useless to him who holds it, and before he is aware, "The heavens withdraw themselves; the Spirit of the Lord is grieved; and when it is withdrawn, Amen to the Priesthood or the authority of that man." (D. & C. 121:37.)

The Keys of the Priesthood. The fact that there is no Priesthood class in the Church promotes unity among the members who are honored by having it bestowed upon them. The presence of the Priesthood in the Church, with its quorum divisions and responsibilities, offers the key to the solutions of Gospel problems, which are the problems of life. Thorough welfare plans, if used wisely, may thus better succeed in the Church with its Priesthood power than in other so cieties. Said President Brigham Young: 

"The keys of that Priesthood are here. We have them in our possession; we can unlock, we can shut up. We can obtain salvation, and we can administer it. . . . They may actually unlock the treasury of the Lord, and receive to their fullest satisfaction. (Discourses, pp. 202-203.) ...

Having this power, the task of today is to exercise it, not with weak words, but with strong deeds. 

President Joseph F. Smith has said: "We may not enjoy the blessings or key, very much, but the key is in the Priesthood. You who hold the Priesthood have the key or the authority. There is not a minister anywhere else in the world, I repeat, who possesses these keys, or this Priesthood or power, or that right. Why? Because they have not received the gospel, nor have they received that Priesthood by the laying on of hands by those having authority to confer it." (Gospel 

Doctrine, p. 176.)

The Priesthood is conferred upon all worthy male

members above twelve years of age. The united quorums represent practically all adolescent and mature male members of the Church. They become an army for the accomplishment of the purposes of the Lord. 

They have within their grasp the key with which to unlock the portals now closed against the spirit of brotherhood-the essential spirit in solving the problems of earth.

An Old Problem. The Welfare Program of the 

Church aims at the solution of an old problem. We have the experiences of the past as a background, but far more important than that, we possess the energizing and integrating power of the Priesthood. We live at a time when the voice of God has been heard anew. His precious words to this generation are sacred scripture to us. Concerning the welfare work He has said: "The earth is full, and there is enough and to spare; yea, I prepared all things, and have given unto the children of men to be agents unto themselves." (D. & C. 104:17.) "What man among you having twelve sons, and is no respecter of them, and they serve him obediently, and he saith unto the one: 

Be thou clothed in robes and sit thou here; and to the other: Be thou clothed in rags and sit thou there and looketh upon his sons and saith I am just?" (D. & C. 38:26.) "For if you are not equal in earthly things, ye cannot be equal in obtaining heavenly things." (D. & C. 78:6.)

The principal difference between the welfare work of other societies and that of our Church lies in the incentive to action, represented primarily by the Priesthood—a brotherhood in a very literal sense. As long as the spirit of selfishness is present there can be no real success in attempts to equalize economic conditions. 

Selfishness hinders a man from doing as much for the community as he could do for himself. 

Without the influence of religion, faith in the brotherhood of man and the Lord's plan of salvation for all

men, selfishness cannot be checked or eradicated. The restored Church of Christ, with its holy Priesthood, forms a perfect organization, if man will use it for activity in the field of social welfare. 

The Priesthood, when its quorums are properly functioning, would become the most potent agency in the world for promoting brotherly relations among men, which other societies have failed to establish. 

Successful social work is always the outgrowth of revealed religion. The present problem within the 

Church is one of re-emphasizing this fact. Members of the various Priesthood quorums should seek to become each his brother's keeper through voluntary, joyous effort and service.

Cooperative Salvation. The plan of salvation is one of cooperation. The Lord cannot save anyone who is not willing to assist in his own salvation. "If people are not saved," said President Brigham Young, "it is because they are not disposed to be saved." (Discourses, p. 87.) Man assists Deity through labor with his fellow men. The Priesthood quorum provides the ideal agency for each man to become his brother's keeper. No other agency has such an admirable plan for carrying out a social welfare program as has the Church in its integrated Priesthood quorums, women's 

Relief Society and the other auxiliaries.

Though the Priesthood provides a perfect medium for the administration of the Welfare Plan, the obligation rests upon each quorum and each member to make the Plan work. The success of a worthy movement depends upon the interest and activity of each member. 

The ideal machinery is ready for operation; the need has arisen; the quorums are organized. The outcome of the Plan rests entirely with the quorums.

A church which does not bring social, physical, and economic salvation to its members may not be relied upon to offer an ultimate and certain salvation of the spirit. A quorum is judged by the same criteria. It is their explicit duty to visit not only the widows and the fatherless, but all members of the quorum, and make sure that there is no distress whatever which the quorum can relieve. 

President Brigham Young has said: "When you understand the Gospel plan, you will comprehend that it is the most reasonable way of dealing with the human family. You will discern that purity, holiness, justice, perfection, and all that adorns the character of Deity are contributing to the salvation of men.' (Discourses, p. 36.) 

The Lord has said in our day: "This is my work and my glory-to bring to pass the immortaltiy and eternal life of man.' (Moses 1:39)

Priesthood Quorums and the Plan. The program for Priesthood quorums has provided for a personal welfare committee in each quorum. It is the duty of this committee to foster closer personal relations with, and ascertain the needs of every member of the quorum. 

Each member of the committee becomes, as it were, a specialized personnel administrator. It becomes the fraternal duty of the committee to visit the members of the quorum to discover where sickness, unemployment, or other forms of distress are present.

In recent years the radical changes in the social and economical structure of society have created so much leisure time that a greater fraternal responsibility was placed upon the various quorums of the Priesthood. 

In too many cases, able-bodied members were faced with total unemployment. A wealth of leisure time would drive one to distraction unless it could be made

use of in some gainful occupation. Others were working only part time and were willing to devote their spare hours to some worthy cause. It was the duty of the personal welfare committee to learn the degree of distress that existed within their group and to assist in finding employment for their idle but ambitious members.

The present Welfare Plan of the Church, on a 

Church-wide scale, has greatly enhanced the personal welfare committee's work. It envisions a perfected development of the fraternal spirit of the Priesthood quorums throughout the Church. The social-economic crisis of recent years has given the movement spirit and animation. An emergency always tends to unite a people as one vast family. Adversity reminds one that he is his brother's keeper; that the lamentations of the widows and orphans should not fall upon ears of parchment or hearts of stone.

Supremacy of the Priesthood. No public or private agencies are comparable with the Priesthood and the auxiliary organizations of the Church. For a generation these organizations have been in operation. 

Their members spring forward, eager to be of service when an emergency or a crisis threatens, not unmindful that "pure religion and undefiled before God and the Father is this: To visit the fatherless and widows in their affliction, and to keep himself unspotted from the world." (James 1:27.) 

President Brigham Young has said on this theme: "The first year that I came into this valley I had not flour enough to last my family until harvest, and that I had brought with me, and persons were coming to my house every day for bread. I had the blues about it one day; I went down to the old fort, and by the time I got back to my house I was completely cured. I said to my wife, 'Do not let a person come here for food and go away empty-handed, for if you do we shall suffer

before harvest; but if you give to every individual that comes we shall have enough to last us through. "I have proven this many a time, and we have again proven it this year. I have plenty on hand, and shall have plenty, if I keep giving away. More than two hundred persons eat from my provisions every day, besides my own family and those who work for me. "I intend to keep doing so, that my bread may hold out, for if I do not I shall come short. "Do you believe that principle? I know it is true, because I have proven it so many times." (J. D., Vol. 3, 332-333.)

The relation of the Priesthood to the Church Welfare Plan is simple. If the Priesthood functions; if the quorums act; if th eindividual deacon, teacher, priest, elder, seventy, high priest performs his duty in line with the welfare revelations, the Plan will succeed. In so far as the Priesthood, individually and collectively, fail to respond to the needs of this hour, the Plan will fail. 

What will be your place in the scheme one yearten years from today? Where will the plan be then?

The answer lies with the Priesthood of this Church. 

Its leaders have spoken: they are united in the movement. It is for each Priesthood bearer to set the welllaid plans into motion, and to resolve lethargy so that the Plan will not fail because of his indifference or inactivity.

PROBLEMS AND QUESTIONS 

1. Professor Peabody has asserted: "Never in human history were so many people, rich and poor, learned and ignorant, wise and otherwise, concerning themselves with social amelioration, dedicating themselves to philanthropy, organizing for industrial change, or applying the motives of religion to the problems of modern life." 

If this be true, why should the Priesthood lead the way? 

2. What is the special responsibility of the Relief Society in the Church Welfare Plan as related to the Priesthood quorum? 

3. In what way can all the auxiliary organizations of the 

Church cooperate? 

4. Suggest projects in which all may participate. Ask for suggestions from your ward and stake committees.

5. G. K. Chesterton remarked: "The Americans have more organizations and less organization than any people I know." 

Show how this is definitely untrue of the Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints in its ideal mode of operation. 

6. Outline in detail the responsibilities of the Personal Welfare committee. (See Priesthood Manual.) 

7. Why is a quorum project, participated in on a quorum basis, of more value than merely individual contributions to the Church 

Welfare Plan? To the quorum? To individual members? To the unemployed themselves? 

8. Which of the following in your locality are most feasible as quorum projects on a quorum basis? a. Agricultural. b. Manufacturing. c. Building. d. Assisting on premises of individual members. e. Home beautification. f. Church beautification. g. Acting as an employment agency to place unemployed members in industry. h. Raising funds as a quorum for a Church Welfare contribution. i. Organizing cooperative enterprises.
