Chapter 5 

CHURCH-WIDE PRODUCTION AND DISTRIBUTION IN THE CHURCH WELFARE PLAN 

a system under which the curse of idleness is done away and independence, industry, thrift and self-respect be once more established amongst our people." (Statement of First 

Presidency, October Conference, 1936.)

The Lord's Way 

"And it is my purpose that my saints be provided for but it must needs be done in mine own way. (D. & C. 104.) The law of tithing and the law of fast offerings have been instituted by the Lord as the way by which revenue shall be provided to finance the necessary activities in "laying the foundations of Zion," and in providing sustenance for the needy of the Church. 

These are just and equitable laws of the Lord, the keeping of which to the fullest extent is within the reach of every member of the Church, whether rich or poor. An analysis of these laws will explain the "Lord's way" in providing for the needs of His saints: The strong shall help to bear the burdens of the weak, but in the process the weak shall not be made weaker than they already are, by requiring of them self-effort to the extent of their physical and financial possibilities to "help themselves."

Contributions to the Church Welfare Plan, or 

"Security Donations," as they are termed on the official ward donation receipt, are to be accounted for in the same fund, as fast offerings, and are solicited of those who are able to contribute in excess of the fast day

contributions, or from productive projects operated cooperatively by brethren of the Priesthood or by sisters of the Relief Society. It will be seen from the following discussion that the same principle is applied in the Church Welfare Plan as in the payment of tithing and fast offerings. Indeed it might be said that the commodities produced and the contributions obtained through the Welfare Program are in reality but an extension of the revenue laws the Lord has already given by which the financial needs of the Church may be supplied.

The Annual Budget Forecast. It has been the reoccurring objective since the first announcement of this plan to provide sufficient food, fuel, clothing, etc., by 

October 1 of each year to provide for those in need of assistance through the ensuing winter period.

In order to plan this annual production on a business-like basis, statistics are obtained from the annual reports of each stake as to the number of individuals who received assistance for the past year. Using these figures as a base and by using tables scientifically prepared, it is possible to forecast fairly accurately the approximate amounts of various commodities each stake, region, and the Church as a whole may need to meet the requirements of the people.

Inasmuch as there is rarely to be found a stake that of itself is able to produce all the commodities required, the plan provides that each stake shall undertake to produce only those commodities that are adaptable to that locality to the limit of its possibilities, as determined by the regional council of which the stake president is a member. The stake responsbility is then to divide its assignments so that every Priesthood group in the Church is expected to have some part in the production program.

A paragraph from the report of the Church Storehouse Supervisor, which is typical of practically every other sections of the Church, will serve to illustrate the tremendous production throughout the Church in the 

Church-wide budget program. "In the Boise Stake, which is not associated with any region, an outstanding work has been done by every ward unit in the stake, and in all these assignments there has been the utmost cooperation on the part of the Priesthood quorums and the Relief Society. Perhaps in no other stake in the Church has there been the same careful assignments and the same amount of activity on the part of the group as a whole, including every Melchizedek Priesthood quorum in the Stake, as we have had in the Boise Stake this year. Twenty-five thousand cans of fruits and vegetables have been packed, at least three cars of potatoes have been raised, two tons of dried fruit have been prepared, 450 bushels of apples have been gathered, a considerable quantity of root vegetables such as carrots, onions, and so forth have been raised. Boise Stake seems to have pointed out the possibilities of the Program when every ward quorum is properly organized and functioning.” 

Widening the Base of the Production Program. 

To attain the goal of supplying by October 1 of each year sufficient to meet the estimated needs requires careful planning. Any activity or industry to accomplish that immediate result comes within the present scope of the Welfare Program. Beyond that immediate objective, the Church will foster and encourage in all stakes and wards, community projects designed to make for the stabilizing and the permanent security of individual members participating.

Experience has proved beyond question the possibility of producing well beyond the budget requirements in such items are fruits and vegetables, potatoes, flour, etc., but there yet remains the requirement to develop the ability to produce largely through the labors of the unemployed themselves many items that must now be purchased from foreign markets to supply the

budget requirements. Of the items much in demand to supply our needs but being produced only in part, if at all, might be named the following: cereals, dairy products (milk, butter, cheese), poultry products, soap, meat, sugar, fuel, building materials, certain articles of clothing, etc.

Which of these items should and could be produced by the Welfare Program must be a concern of every Priesthood group and Church unit who should not be satisfied until they have carefully investigated the feasibility and practicability of the production of each item in their own locality. 

In one region of the Church a salvage project has been developed to a point that it now is filling a much needed place in providing quarters where household articles, furniture, electrical fixtures, clothing, shoes, etc., can be provided at a price within the reach of low income families or to supply storehouse requirements. 

The possibilities of the establishment of more similar projects should be studied. 

Priesthood Quorum Projects and Budget Assignments. When a Priesthood group or quorum has been assigned a certain quota to meet the stake assignments, it is then urged to produce beyond that assignment to build up a fund which the quorum may use in the rehabilitation of individual members.

The Priesthood committee of the Council of the Twelve has given definite instructions as to the care and expenditure of such funds as might be derived from quorum projects after making its desired contribution to the ward bishop for the Church Welfare Plan. "A word about the handling of quorum funds. Neither the president of the quorum nor his counselors, nor quorum members are authorized to use money belonging to the quorum fund. Every expenditure from quorum funds should be upon the vote of the quo-

rum not necessarily as to details but in general. The quorum should be made aware of every proposed project and expenditure and be party to it by voting for it. Then, the actual draft upon the quorum fund should be made upon an order signed by the quorum president and preferably also by his two counselors. "Since all Melchizedek Priesthood quorums in the Church are stake organizations, the quorum presidency should always be in touch with the stake presidency as to the advisability of making the expenditures approved by the quorum. No expenditure should be made which is not approved by the stake presidency. "Quorums should not speculate, and thereby endanger their resources. They are to keep moneys intact, safe and whole, and expend them under proper authority for the worthy purposes of 

Priesthood activity. "All money paid by a quorum for Church Welfare purposes or for the support of the poor within the quorums, should be receipted for by the bishop. The receipt is an evidence that the quorum has done the work and placed the quorum activity upon the books of the Church. (Priesthood Committee Council of the Twelve.)

Surplus Fast Offerings and Contributions. Acting upon instructions from the General Authorities, ward bishops send in monthly surplus fast offerings to the stake fast offerings fund. Surplus funds in the stake are remitted to the regional fast offering fund. These funds are provided to assist needy wards and stakes in taking care of the needy, it being understood that these contributions from individual Church members are made for the purpose of caring for the needy wherever they may live, within or without the ward where the contribution is made.

Likewise commodities contributed to the Welfare Program are to be applied to meet the local need, but surpluses beyond that need are to be turned over for distribution under the direction of the General Church 

Welfare Committee, through Bishops' storehouses wherever these surpluses are needed.

Storehouse Operation Explained. It has been explained in another chapter how a worker on a project

is issued a work receipt from the project director that entitles him to what he needs in cash from his bishop or to orders on the storehouse for commodities which he in part has helped to provide. The storehouse manager should keep fully informed as to the surplus commodities available in General Committee Storage Centers, and that they may be requisitioned to supplement or be exchanged for produce in any given storehouse district. 

Thus it will be seen that the whole plan of distribution of fast offerings and commodities is done in the "Lord's Way" of the strong helping the weak.

Vocational Training and the Welfare Plan. But production for the purpose of distribution to the needy must not be thought of as an end in itself. The planting, cultivating and harvesting of crops from agricultural projects, the processing of food products in canning centers, and the making of clothing in sewing centers, must be viewed in light of the objectives of the Church Welfare Plan, as training schools for the participants. Those who supervise these activities should be selected with the thought in mind constantly of improving the quality of service being rendered and thereby qualify the participants on the project for the time when they can be independent and self-sustaining on projects of their own. 

So, likewise, the varied activities of well-operated storehouses should provide opportunities for improving the individuals engaged on these projects. In the girl who answers the telephone, and the man who records the withdrawals of ward bishops, to the person who receives the merchandise and labels the canned produce for distribution, can be developed an attitude of learning and advancement that predicts a future where employment opportunities are brighter.

It can be seen that as production activities of the Program become more varied, as explained elsewhere in this chapter, the greater the opportunities for training men and women in vocational activities in preparation for an independent livelihood.

Agriculture and the Welfare Plan. Agricultural projects and activities are basic in the production program, just as a stabilized rural community means advancement toward the goal at which the Program aims.

Dr. John A. Widtsoe writes of this phase of the subject: "The desire of the L. D. S. Church Program is to place all members in permanent pursuits, which will yield not only daily economic support, but a steady approach, however slight, towards economic advancement. To the meeting of this demand, much more important than the first, the Program is now resolutely squaring its shoulders. "The attempt to find worth-while, permanent employment for the unemployed, and to better the conditions of those whose scanty earnings are insufficient for ordinary human comforts, of necessity brings the Program into the field of agriculture. Indeed, from 

Latter-day Saint farms came the vast majority of the food supplies provided for the destitute of the Church during the last two years. The Church has always held that a stable, enduring civilization rests upon a body of men and women who own and till the soil-who make their living by farming; and is proud to know that the majority of its members are tillers of the soil.' 

Dr. Widtsoe outlines the five-fold needs of the farmer as follows: "1. There must be a new spirit of conservation of our resources. "2. More profitable crops must be grown. "3. There must be more cooperation among farmers in the production and marketing of crops. "4. Men must be helped to own farms. "5. Farm life must be made attractive." (Improvement Era, January, 1938)

Genealogical Activities and the Church Welfare

Plan. One of the significant developments in providing productive activities for the so-called "unemployables" is the establishment of projects under the direction and supervision of the Genealogical Society, by which funds are solicited from among those desiring to have Temple work done, and these funds and contributions used to sustain workers at the Temple while working on the names submitted by those making the contributions.

The trend of this movement may be best described in the language of the president of the Parowan Stake and chairman of the Virgin River Region: "The Presidencies of these stakes (of the Virgin River Region) met at the Temple and discussed our problem, and decided that we would build on the plan of a tourist camp-each stake building its own unit, but all being planned together, which, when completed, will all be under one management. The stakes are to build their own units, which consist of a house with four apartments in it. There are seven stakes in the Region, and that will provide twenty-eight apartments to house temple workers from our temple district, when completed. "The stakes are to build their own houses, using their own means of financing. It so happens that the Parowan Stake has been the first to get the building program under way, and I have been asked to explain the method by which we have financed our building. "The question most frequently asked us perhaps is, "Is the Church participating with you and on what basis? I will say that the Church is not participating with us. We haven't asked them to. We thought we would like to see if we could do one thing for ourselves without Church or Federal aid. It costs us, if we hire the work done in the Temple, fifty cents per name. It costs us that much or more if we drive down there to do the work. We told the people of our need for the house and said, "If you will build this house in St. George, the stake will obligate itself to do the work for one person in the Temple for every fifty cents that you will contribute to our house, so that in the end your contributions will cost you nothing. You will get value received on it." The

Stake Presidency secured a list by wards of all heads of families and made a tentative allotment of funds which we would like them to raise. These lists were then submitted to the Bishop for approval or revision, and then we mailed a letter to each family

giving them an allotment and explained our plan to them. Then we assigned a member of the High Council to supervise the collection of these funds in each Ward. This part, I think, perhaps if we do it again, we would do differently, because in any body of men such as a High Council you get some who are good collectors and some who are not. There is an art in the collection of funds. 

I think we would select a special committee for that purpose. 

However, our High Council have done well. Some of the Wards were in 100%, with their funds in cash, in a few weeks, and some have taken longer. Some are not 100% yet. Our house is almost completed and the building committee, the Chairman, told me. yesterday that the Committee have paid out in money, less than $50.00 for labor, except for the plumbing, which will be let by contract. "Up to the present time our people have responded splendidly to this appeal. I know of only two in the stake that have refused to accept their allotment, and many have asked if they couldn't increase it, not because that was a cheaper way of getting temple work done than if they did it themselves. We have had no financial problems. It is easier than anything we have ever attempted. We have had some interesting and unexpected reactions. 

One who opposed our plan was the best genealogical worker we had in the Stake, and she thought we should be spending this money on research rather than for building a house. She saw a crying need for money to be spent in research work and she was opposed very strenuously to our building program. Two weeks ago she came and asked if she could have a talk with me. She said, 'You will be surprised at all I am going to tell you. I have changed my mind about the building program.' I asked, 'What changed your mind?' She said, 'You know So and So. Well, isn't he about the last fellow you would think of being interested in genealogical work?' I said, 'He surely is.' 'He has been coming to my place now every night for the last week, asking me to help him on his genealogical research. Last night I asked him what interested him in genealogy. He said, "Well, I have paid for twenty names to be done in the Temple, and now I have got to rustle the names'." "Now, we will have when our house is completed, something like a $5,000.00 inducement to do genealogical research that we haven't had before. We have been very happy in our project. 

It has worked out splendidly. Our home will soon be ready to occupy, and when it is we will have a place to send the worthy poor who may need some help. We will ask them to go there, spend a week or month, as the case may be, working in the Temple on the names that the stake is obligated to do the work for, and while they are so engaged we will support them by supplying them out of our Welfare fund so that it will work in splendidly with our

Welfare program." (Special Welfare Meeting, October Confer ence, 1938.)

Quorums in large cities where great genealogical libraries are located, in some instances are raising funds from among their members to keep one or two otherwise unemployed members of their quorum or wards constantly employed doing research work. 

A Ward Conservation Project-Example. In a ward of the San Bernardino Stake for the last two years, there has been carried forward an interesting and profitable project that demonstrates the value and possibilities of team-work.

In this particular ward, approximately sixty families were found who were of low-income, and facing an uncertain future because of their inability to prepare against a possible day of unemployment, sickness, or want.

The project began as a high priests' project, intended only for families of that quorum group, but was enlarged to become a ward project after consultation with the ward welfare committee.

These families were not on "relief;" some had employment at low wages, others had gardens but little or no cash income, and others were unemployed but at present not in distress. 

The activity was called the "Ward Conservation

Project." In circular letters to these families the ward committee explained that it was proposed to conduct a project in the ward to give opportunity to all families to improve their conditions through united effort. Each family could participate in the project by contributing either cash, commodities, or labor, on a basis agreed upon, and receive credit therefore on the records of the organization. Peas, beans, corn, tomatoes, fruit, veal,

fish, etc., were processed through this activity and at the conclusion of the summer's work each family was permitted to liquidate its credits by drawing from the variety of commodities on hand.

At the outset, they agreed that a percentage of the commodities produced were to be turned over to the bishop as a contribution to the Church Welfare Program, in taking care of those not able to care for themselves in the ward, the stake or the region. 

The result of this project was that each family participating finished the summer season with several hundred bottles and cans of produce, provided through united effort, that otherwise would not have been possible.

A Ward Building Project. In a ward of the Idaho 

Falls Stake, a group of twelve men counseled together as to how they could improve their housing conditions. 

Each, with his family, was living in a house pitifully inadequate to meet the requirements of the rigorous climate of that section.

They organized themselves into a building society for the purpose of assisting each other to build suitable homes. As an organization, they applied to a loaning agency in the state and obtained a loan of $1500.00 for the purpose of purchasing materials they were not able to produce through their own efforts. 

One of their number was a carpenter and the others were unskilled laborers. They agreed that each man in his turn would be given the opportunity of borrowing from the funds on hand sufficient to buy materials for a type of structure previously agreed upon. As they labored to assist each other in this construction, the time of each man was charged against the one having the home constructed, while he in turn could dis-

charge his obligation to his fellow members by returning the labor when they built their house. By rotating the small loan fund over a period of three years, each one of the twelve has been enabled to greatly improve his housing conditions at a minimum of expenditure, and had received a training in construction work that was invaluable.

Each man borrowed on an average of $250.00 to $300.00, and paid back the money borrowed at so much per month, so that by the time the last of the twelve applied for a loan the first had returned sufficient in payments on their loans to provide the necessary funds to carry on the project.

Conclusion. A careful study of the foregoing chapter should have served to define the place of the Priesthood quorum in the Church Welfare Plan, first in activities to produce sufficient to provide for the requirements of the annual budget forecast, and to provide quorum funds for the rehabilitation of individual quorum members; and, second, in united enterprise as quorum groups, to permanently stabilize those now wholly or partially dependent. 

These suggestions should but serve to inspire alert

Priesthood quorums in a study of their own problems and possibilities. In the language of the chairman of the Northern Utah Region: "I say to you, Presidencies of Stakes and Ward Bishoprics and Relief Society who are engaged in the Welfare Plan very frankly, that its success requires a lot of time, a lot of thought, and a lot of energy. It won't just grow up like Topsy in Uncle Tom's 

Cabin, and the more time and thought and energy put into it the greater it will succeed."

Chapter HOW TO CREATE WEALTH 

"True wealth does not consist in the possession of gold and silver, but in the judicious use made of them.' (Napoleon I.) 

Man's Struggle for a Living. In spite of all our scientific advancement, our countless mechanical inventions, and our unsurpassed industrial development, man's chief economic problem today is what it has ever been since Adam left the Garden of Eden: to acquire an income large enough to satisfy his wants, or, in other words, to make a satisfactory living for himself and his family. Indeed the vast majority of people must still devote the major part of all their activities, from the time they arise in the morning to the time they fall asleep at night, to the process of making a living. If the goods, including commodities and services of all kinds, essential to the satisfaction of our wants, were all as free as the air we breathe when out-of-doors, there would of course be no problems of getting a living. 

From time immemorial people have dreamed about such a world. Like the children of Israel, weary from their wanderings in the Wilderness, they have looked forward to entering the "Promised Land" where milk and honey flowed freely. Many have conjured up Utopia where there would be more opportunity to live and less need for struggle. Others, disappointed with their fortunes in this life, have turned their attention and their hopes to the hereafter, visualizing it as a state in which there would be an abundance of want-satisfying goods, plenty of servants to wait upon them, but free from weary toil on their part.

HOW TO CREATE WEALTH 

63 But, unfortunately, one of the most distinctive and significant characteristics of the world in which we live today, whether we like it or not, is the fact that nature does not supply us gratuitously with all, or even many, of the commodities and services that we desire. We live in a world of scarcity; a world that requires persistent human effort to provide the things we need. It is relative scarcity of food, clothing, shelter and the countless comforts and luxuries of life that make it necessary for men to struggle to get a living. It is scarcity and man's desire to satisfy the needs and wants of his family that has driven men to the uttermost parts of the world in quest of new and larger economic opportunities. 

Wealth Creation, Yesterday and Today. In the days of Abraham, Isaac and Jacob, the struggle for a living consisted largely in caring for their flocks and herds and in producing enough grain to satisfy the restricted wants of their time. Their income was not measured in so many dollars per week or per month, but in the productive increase of their flocks and herds from year to year. Their wealth was likewise measured by the number of cattle, sheep or other livestock they possessed, by the number of servants they had, and by the extensiveness of their lands and holdings. If they had plenty of fat cattle and sufficient grain to carry them over the winter, and perhaps a surplus for a possible crop shortage, due to drought or pestilence, they were considered wealthy or well-to-do. 

Their chief aim was to increase their wealth and well-being by expanding their holdings, increasing their output of grain and enlarging their flocks and herds. 

Their whole program centered around the idea of increased production, on the theory that the more they

produced the more there would be to divide among their families and their servants. Consequently, disease, pestilence or drought were considered as tragedies, because they meant decreased production, less to share among themselves, and therefore a scantier living during such a period.

Their philosophy of improving economic welfare, therefore, was simply to increase the output of their food and clothing supplies so there would be more to go around. Fortunately their money system had not yet become so complicated as to confuse their thinking or to distort their ideas of economic welfare or the fundamental principles of creating wealth in a world of scarcity.

However, in the world we live in, where most people work for wages, fees or commissions, where most things are bought and sold for money-even human services the process of creating wealth often appears quite differently. Many persons nowadays are inclined to visualize a wealthy man as one who has a considerable amount of money, but not necessarily one who has large buildings, flocks or herds. 

Today, the man who has a large bank account, who owns stocks and bonds, or gets a big commission on a deal, or royalty on a patent, or a "cut" in the gate receipts of a movie production, is considered the wealthy person.

Or, in other words, the one who has a large money income, even though he does not own the roof over his head, is considered rich in the eyes of the world of today, because that money income will purchase the goods and services produced by others; it can be exchanged for food, clothing, shelter, entertainment, and luxuries unheard of in Abraham's time.

HOW TO CREATE WEALTH 

65 But the fundamental principles of creating wealth is still the same as in the earlier times. It consists in creating more goods and services in order that there may be more to be divided among the group. That some get more than others is another question to be discussed later. The all-important point is that all economic wealth, all things that satisfy human wants, are created through man's efforts, and only through the application of his energies and intelligence to the bounties of nature; in the wise utilization of the resources available.

Improved Methods of Creating Wealth. Fortu nately for us in this day and age the means of creating wealth and providing goods and services have been greatly improved over Abraham's time. Through scientific knowledge and technological advancement, man can now produce such things as shoes, wheat and automobiles, with such efficiency and perfection that the accomplishments of our progenitors appear trifling, to say the least. In America, the development of power and machine farming has been extended so widely, that less than three farmers in ten are now needed to produce the food and raw materials required by our nation, and still we have large surpluses to ship abroad in exchange for other commodities not produced in this country. 

Such unparalleled efficiency has released the other seven families from the land, permitting them to engage in other productive pursuits, such as education, medicine, transportation, entertainment, and so on, to give us a richer variety of goods and services at a greatly reduced cost. Hence, today, through the inventions of power, steam, and electricity; through the development of the railroad, the automobile, the press, the movies and now the radio, we have raised our standard of

living, enriched our lives and lightened our burdens, so that it has become much easier to provide the common necessities of food, clothing and shelter, and with little added effort we may also enjoy modern comforts, art, music, education and the many cultural things of life. But each one must do his part.

To be sure, we need not all engage in the production of crops and livestock products any more. Nor is it necessary to make our own clothes or build our own homes. But it is essential that we all engage in productive enterprises if we wish to share in the fruits of other's work, and in order that society as a whole might have a maximum of the goods and services they desire. Obviously, if some live off the products of others, without contributing their part, the group as a whole will have less to share among the members of the group. 

And the more there are that are non-productive, the lower will be the standard of living of the group in comparison with what it could be if all members of the group were producers as well as consumers. 

Unfortunately, our system of trade, our production for sale, our working for money income with which to buy the goods and sevices produced by others under a constantly fluctuating price structure, tends to confuse the whole process of creating wealth. The tendency is to think in terms of money rather than goods and services, to measure quality by the price tag, and to gauge success in terms of dollars. Some persons even get so disturbed in their philosophies as to believe that we can have more by producing less; that by destroying certain forms of wealth there will somehow be more income; by working fewer hours and fewer days we can accomplish more; or by spending more, even if we have to go in debt to do so, there will be more business, and consequently more prosperity and so forth.

HOW TO CREATE WEALTH 

67 But the old rule, that "we cannot get something for nothing," still applies. Wherever there is found a high stage of civilization, or great prosperity, or a high average production or consumption of wealth, the explanation must lie in the character of the people or in the character of the environment in which they live. If the people in China have less per capita to consume than the people in France, it is because the Chinese produce less per capita than do the French; and the explanation of this must be found in the lower vigor, or skill, or energy, or intelligence, or scientific attainments of the Chinese, or in the unfavorable conditions under which they live.

Likewise, if Americans are more prosperous and live better than Europeans, it must be that Americans are better producers-more active, more inventive, more enterprising or that the soil and climate and other natural resources of America offer more advantageous opportunities for production. Men do not gather grapes of thorns or figs of thistles. It takes more than a good farmer alone, or a good farm alone, to make a good crop. There must be both farm and farmer. Only opportunity improved is success.

1. Arrange in proper order of sequence: (a) Standard of living, (b) consumption, (c) desire, (d) productive efficiency. 

Which comes first? second? etc. 

2. Which of the following may properly be called wealth? (a) a silver dollar, (b) a healthful climate, (c) a mortgaged farm, (d) a life insurance policy, (e) a deserted mine, mill and smelter.

(c) Will the wealth of the country be affected if the bonds are paid off?

5. How is it that so many people can be spared from the production of wealth to work for the government? 

6. What has been the attitude of the Mormon Church toward the creation of wealth and the economic welfare of its members?

Chapter 

LABOR IS LIFE 

"Is not the upbuilding of the Kingdom of God on earth a temporal labor all the time? It will be built up by physical force and means, by manual labor more than by any particular mental effort of the mind. Everything connected with building up Zion requires actual, severe labor. It is nonsense to talk about building up any kingdom except by labor; it requires the labor of every part of our organization, whether it be mental, physical, or spiritual, and that is the only way to build up the Kingdom of God."1 

Labor Indispensable. Thus did Brigham Young expound the philosophy of Mormonism to the Saints in early days regarding the principle of labor and the necessity of work by every able-bodied person, and from that day to the present time President Young's successors have continued to teach the Saints to be industrious, to do an honest day's work where ever they are employed, that manual work is honorable, and that idleness is the Devil's workshop.

Capital and Labor. In another discourse President 

Young outlined very effectively the relation of capital and labor as follows: "All the capital there is upon the earth is the bone and sinew of working men and women. Were it not for that, the gold and the silver and the precious stones would remain in the mountains, upon the plains, and in the valleys, and never would be gathered or brought into use. The timber would continue to grow, but none of it would be brought into service, and the earth would remain as it is; but it is the activity and the labor of the inhabitants of the earth that bring forth the wealth. Labor builds our meetinghouses, temples, court houses, fine halls for music and fine school houses; it is labor that teaches our children, that makes them 

'Discourses of Brigham Young, by John A. Widtsoe. Chap. 

16, p. 447.

proficient in their own language and in other languages, and in every branch of knowledge understood by the children of men; and all this enhances the wealth and the glory and the comfort of any people on the earth."2

In a similar manner, economists classify labor along with land and capital as the primary agents of production. One writer in emphasizing the importance of labor referred to capital as "labor merely gone to seed," meaning that all machinery and equipment used in the manufacture of economic goods were themselves once created by the application of labor to raw materials provided by nature.

Labor, and its contributions to wealth, is considered so important by one school of thought led by Karl Marx that, according to them, all economic life should be organized and controlled by the workers. So far reaching has this doctrine permeated the thinking of European people during the past century, that all of Russia is now organized according to that principle and the idea is spreading throughout Germany, France and other countries.

To be sure, they have carried the importance of labor too far, and have attributed to the one who does the physical work too much credit for the finished product. They do not recognize the part played by management, capital or natural resources. Consequently, they do not recognize profit, interest or rent-only wages. Accordingly, there can be no such thing as private property, accumulation of wealth by individuals or the lending of money by private capitalists. All property and wealth, all instruments of production, including farms, factories, stores, railroads, etc., thereby belong to the state, and are operated by the state, which 'Ibid. Page 462.

LABOR IS LIFE 

71 in turn is controlled by the laboring class (by the Soviet 

Union in Russia).

In all capitalistic countries, like America, the factors of production are all recognized and given legal status. The industrialist has certain property rights and certain managerial freedom. Likewise, the banker and money lender is permitted to earn a fair return on his investment, sufficient to cover the cost of handling, the risk involved and an adequate return for the service rendered.

In the same way, the laborer is considered worthy of his hire, and is given as large a wage as the market price of the product will permit, considering the other factors of production and the efficiency of the worker himself.

The Worker in Modern Industry. The role of the working-man of today, however, is quite different from the hired man of a few generations ago. At the time our Church was organized approximately three-fourths of the population lived on farms, produced their own food and made most of their own clothing. In the main, people provided their own employment. There were few corporations and few hired men. And accordingly there was little unemployment, and therefore practically no excuse for idleness.

In contrast, we find three-fourths of the people living in cities today, largely dependent upon giant industrial corporations for employment. Most of the so-called working class are dependent upon their weekly wage, living in a rented apartment just around the corner from a grocery store, and not far from a fiveand-ten-cent department store from which most of their needs are supplied from day to day. If the factory shuts down, due to business reverses or unfavorable

economic conditions, the average worker and his family find themselves in a serious predicament. Usually, they have little or no reserves, no garden, chickens or cows to supplement the factory pay check, and so the family is virtually stranded unless the government or some other agency comes to their aid.

As a result of this changing economic world, in a highly commercialized and industrialized civilization, we are becoming accustomed to far-reaching economic disturbances, to wide-spread unemployment and constant shifts in economic fortunes. The changes have come upon us so rapidly that neither the employer, the worker or the Government have been able to adjust to the changing conditions. The advance of technological improvement has been so rapid, the shifts in methods and technique so revolutionary, and the social adjustments so difficult, that most people find themselves bewildered in their attempt to understand the direction and meaning of it all. Many give up in despair, join the first group that offers a way out, sound or otherwise, or turn to the Government or the Church for help. Some, unfortunately, seem to lose all sense of values, except their employers, their union leaders, and their Government to do the impossible. They seem to believe in a Santa Claus with an unlimited bank account, having no connection with wealth, resources, or work, or any of the basic principles of economic production.

To be sure, in such periods of serious economic disturbances, the innocent victims of such revolutionary changes must be aided in their efforts to adjust to the violent shifts that are taking place. Both church and state must accept the challenge in protecting the family from privation and undue suffering, and the morale of

LABOR IS LIFE 

73 

In such the individual members from disintegration. times their faith in the ultimate outcome must be strengthened and reassured from time to time. Agencies of the Church, such as Priesthood quorums, can render great service to their members and their fellow men generally through encouragement, guidance and direct assistance. Frequently such a fraternal organization can do more than any other agency in keeping the boat from rocking during such dangerous storms.

In time we will no doubt learn how to live in a highly industrialized civilization without experiencing such violent disturbances. Perhaps we may come to 

Henry Ford's plan of decentralized industrial villages, where the wage-earner can have a home of his own, a few chickens, a cow and a garden to supplement the family income and to afford greater security in time of economic disturbances. Or, perhaps the Government will devise a permanent program of work projects for the unemployed on a more satisfactory basis than now exists. Or, maybe, an entirely different economic system will evolve out of our difficulties, based on the principles of cooperation, with more emphasis on economic security, and less emphasis on profits, expansion and exploitation.

Chapter 

THRIFT "Thrift is such a simple thing and it means so much. It is the foundation of success in business, of contentment in the home, of standing in society. ."-Russel Sage. Thrift Defined. To some people thrift means parsimony, stinginess; being miserly or unduly sparing in the expenditure of money. To others it means saving merely for the purpose of hoarding. But true thrift is none of these. It is "economical management, frugality, prosperity, success," according to Noah Webster.

Thrift is not only saving; it is in fact mostly spending-wise spending. Spending for life insurance; spending for a home; spending for the education of our children; spending for a reserve fund which may be used later if misfortune should overtake us; or spending for sound investments to take care of the old man or old woman that we are going to be some day.

Thrift is Really Deferred Spending, in order that we may be able to spend more later. Thrift means getting the most for our money over a long period of time. 

In the main it is the systematic and regular saving of a part of our earnings during our productive years for the purpose of having a sure income that will provide our necessities and our comforts when our earning power has decreased or when we wish to slow down a little. 

For example: If one has a desire to spend $100 now for things which are not absolutely necessary (not $100 in one lump sum, but in small amounts, over a period of time), just remember that if one will invest this money for about twelve years, the $100 will have increased to $200. In about 24 years it will have increased to $400, in 56 years to $800, and so on.

Furthermore, the spending of $400 or $800 in 24 or 36 years hence would likely give far more pleasure and satisfaction than the spending of $100 now, especially if the $100 were to be spent for unnecessary things. 

Interest and Thrift. A part of almost every dollar we spend goes to pay interest to some individual or group of individuals. If we rent a home, a part of the rent money is used to pay interest on the landlord's investment. When we ride on a train, a bus, or a street car, the fare includes interest on the company's investment and bonds. Our light and telephone bills include interest; every merchant from whom we buy food and clothing must charge enough for his merchandise to cover interest on his investment.

If one is thrifty in the management of his income, if he can put a little of his earnings aside each week, he may soon have others paying interest to him. If we can manage to save a little-ever so little-each pay day, then we can invest in these interest bearing bonds and thereby put our thrift savings to work. 

We can never get out of the interest paying class, but we can get into the interest receiving class if we have the determination to save and invest wisely and regularly a part of our earnings. To illustrate the importance of the interest factor, let us remember that whenever we waste $5 (or spend it unnecessarily) it is the equivalent of spending the interest on an investment of $100. Or to put it another way, $10 wasted in trivial expenditures this year will deprive us of goods worth $10.50 next year; $11.03 two years from now; $12.76 in five years, and so on indefinitely. (Based on 5% interest.)

Obligations to our Dependents. We should be thrifty for another reason—the obligation we have to our dependents. If during our old age we become dependent

THRIFT upon our children, we may not only prevent them from having some of the comforts which they might have had, but we might also prevent them from accumulating a reserve which would insure their independence. In other words, dependence may be passed from one generation to another.

On the other hand, if we are thrifty and independent, thus enabling our children to accumulate more for their own independence, then we will be passing independence from one generation to another. 

The great majority of people pass through two periods of dependency during their lives. The first is before they begin earning their own living, and the second is after they are too old to work. It should be the desire and determination of all of us to eliminate the latter period of dependency through habits of thrift during the productive years of our life. 

Providing a fund for old age, and in a similar way providing a reserve for emergencies such as sickness, accident, or unemployment, is not our duty as citizens; it is a privilege of free men. Only slaves may expect their masters to care for them under all circumstances, only the indolent, or those who have suffered misfortune through no fault of theirs, should expect society to carry their burdens for them. Those who are truly thrifty will be too proud to be supported by the savings and sacrifices of others. The thrifty have their own definite program of financial support; they will finish on their own power." 

Economic Justification for Thrift. Aside from any selfish motive or feeling of obligation to provide for the future, we owe it to society, we owe it to our community, to consume at the moment less than we produce, to spend less than we earn, to be thrifty, to try to get ahead financially.

Former President Calvin Coolidge once said, “It is not too much to say that almost the whole of what we call civilization is the difference between saving what we make today for use on the morrow, and exhausting it at the time we receive it. And wherever we find a people with sufficient self-control, sufficient balance, sufficient thrift and industry to save their money and increase their capital, there you may be altogether certain that civilization will make progress. 

The economic justification for thrift may be summed up briefly by stating that if no one had ever produced more than he consumed, we would still be in a primitive state. The more we save and invest, the more industries there will be and the larger the industries, the more employment and better the wages. The more employment, the greater our savings and investments should be. And this is how civilization has developed and prospered. It is a great satisfaction to the thrifty person to know that he is contributing to our economic development and prosperity.

Thrift and Happiness. The most satisfied and contented people are those who realize that, as a result of their earnest and conscientious effort, as a result of their wise spending and plans for future spending, they are each year adding to their reserve fund.

These people are also strengthening their character, because no one can put away a part of his earnings for use at some future time, no one can make up his mind to cut out useless things and stick to his resolution, without strengthening his character.

Some men and women who are not making any provision for the future try to console themselves with the thought that when they grow older they will perhaps be earning more money and then it will be easier to save.

THRIFT They do not seem to realize that when people earn more they usually feel so much more prosperous that they are likely to spend their increased earnings to gratify present desires.

Instead of finding it easier to save when they earn more, people frequently find it harder, because their desire for luxuries and extravagances, their desires to gratify present whims, seem to increase more rapidly than their earnings increase. But real contentment and happiness do not always come from gratifying present desires, and surely nothing can bring greater unhappiness than spending our entire income now, with no regard for the future.

Thrift and Time. But thrift applies not only to money and industry; it applies equally to the use of our time and talents. Those who are truly thrifty plan their work and work their plan. They do not allow time to go to waste any more than they allow clothing, food or wealth in any form to be wasted. Neither do they impose upon other people by stealing their time. The thrifty person is always on time at his meetings and appointments, and he expects other people to be likewise. He has no time for idle gossip or questionable story-telling during working hours He has no time for wasteful habits, like tobacco and strong drinks require.

Yet the thrifty person has more time than the average person for the important assignments; more time for intellectual improvement and spiritual development; and more time for true leisure, because he utilizes his time more effectively. For, as someone has said, "If you want a job well done, get a busy person to do it;" he will have more time!

1. Does one have to go without the comforts of life that other people enjoy to be thrifty?

3. Will the government's old age assistance program or the old age pension system make thrift unnecessary in the future? 

4. Will being thrifty interfere with one's church participation, either spiritual or temporal? 

5. What effect do wars, bank failures, and economic depressions have on thrifty habits of the people? 

6. What effect does good government have on the encouragement of thrift?

Chapter THE BONDAGE OF DEBT 

"Once in debt, interest is your companion every minute of the day and night; you can not shun it or slip away from it; you can not dismiss it. Interest never sleeps_nor sickens, nor dies; it never goes to the hospital; it works on Sundays and holidays; it never takes a vacation; it has no love, no sympathy; it is as hard as a granite cliff."-J. Reuben Clark, Jr.

Modern Use of Credit. While it is true that most business is operated on credit today-on "other people's money"-yet the basic principle of debt, which includes the payment of interest, the security by a mortgage or its equivalent and the forfeiture of the property upon failure to meet the payments, have been the same since the beginning of commercial lending. To be sure, the processes of borrowing and lending have been modernized and extended in numerous ways to meet the complex industrial life of today. The methods of repayment, particularly, have been modified to correspond more nearly with the receipt of income, greatly facilitating the ease with which loans may be made and repaid. 

So, today, the amortized loan has replaced the straight mortgage, and the installment contract has largely replaced the open book account for large purchases. 

These developments have really been a great help to the banker and merchant. They have reduced both the cost of furnishing credit and the number of bankruptcies in all types of business. But these very developments that have been such a boon to the business organizations have been a means of luring many persons into debt who probably would have been much better off had they remained on a "pay as you go" basis.

Installment Credit. Articles purchased on credit, and particularly on the installment plan, usually cost more than similar articles purchased for cash. It therefore reduces our purchasing power; it results in our getting less for our money than if we pay cash. Buying on the installment plan also means the mortgaging of our future earnings. If through sickness or death, or through the loss of work, the earnings cease, the property purchased is usually lost, together with what has been put into it. As one British economist has said: "It has done much harm in America; it has been drastically limited in many parts of Europe; it is the enemy of real thrift; it is most tempting to the least provident." 

Schedler, in his little book on "How to Get Ahead 

Financially" says that, "We can afford to buy only a few items on the installment plan and we can afford these only because they increase our assets, because they provide for the future of our families or reduce our expenses. For example, our homes, life insurance and sound investments are forms of saving, not spending. Also, labor-saving devices, provided they reduce expenses sufficiently to warrant buying them on the installment plan.'

As an illustration of where an installment purchase might be profitable, let us consider the following: If a family were paying out $2.00 per week for laundry, and if by buying a washing machine, half of this amount could be saved, the $1.00 per week ($52.00 per year) would not only be a good return on an investment of say $100.00 in a washing machine, but the machine would soon pay for itself out of the saving in laundry bills. 

Obviously, such cases of buying on credit are probably justifiable. But such instances are the exception rather than the rule. Too often the installment plan is used to buy expensive luxuries that the family

THE BONDAGE OF DEBT 

83 cannot afford; they put undue strain on the budget and increase the cost of living unnecessarily. 

Why not save the equivalent of these monthly installments first, then buy, rather than buy and then save to pay the installments-plus the added cost of interest and carrying charge? We would get more for our money. We would be able to take advantage of special sales when they occur, and would not have to patronize special partial payment concerns where prices are usually higher to compensate them for carrying installment accounts and for the losses they incur because some people default in their payments. 

Anyone who buys on the "dollar down-dollar a week" plan is demonstrating the fact that he can save money. If he can spare the easy payments out of his income, he could just as easily save them in advance—in the bank. Eventually, if he saves the necessary amount regularly he will be able to pay cash for what he buys and save the difference between the cash price and the installment price, including interest and carrying charges. 

For example, let us suppose we were buying an automobile. 

The Installment price would be 

The Cash price (not including insurance) Difference .$972 890 $ 82 

The Installment payments- 

One-third of installment price in advance.. 

Twelve monthly payments of $54 each.... $324 648 $972 

But since the installment buyer owes the dealer $648 for only one month, and since this amount is reduced by the payment of subsequent installments of $54

each month, the buyer owes the dealer an average of about $351 for the period of twelve months. For this debt of $351 the dealer adds $82 to the price of the car, which is equivalent to an interest and carrying charge of about 23 percent of the average debt. 

True, some automobile manufacturers have organized their own financing corporations in recent years with deferred payment plans advertised at 6 percent, not including insurance. However, the 6 percent applies on the full amount of the loan for the entire period, which really amounts to 12 or 13 percent on the deferred payments.

Many cases of installment buying have come to the writer's attention where the interest and carrying charges were even higher than the case cited above, some with deferred payment rates as high as 50 percent per annum. 

Hence it usually pays to pay cash, regardless of other factors. Jr.: 

Again, in the words of President J. Reuben Clark, "Until times become more certain, the ordinary family will do well to purchase by installments only the actual necessaries of life, leaving the luxuries to be bought as they can be paid for when purchased. I shall not attempt to draw a line between necessities and luxuries, beyond saying that an artisan or mechanic who can ride to work on the train or street car would hardly be justified in buying an airplane for that purpose on the installment plan; nor would a farmer already owning a cow pony to go to the pasture for the cows be justified in buying an automobile on the installment plan to be used for the same purpose.' 

Long Term Loans. Long term debts may be more perilous than installment purchases. As our civilization becomes more highly industrialized, economic disturbances seem to become more violent. Periods of prosperity and depression are making investment procedure so uncertain that long term commitments, even though based on the best of security, are no longer dependable.

THE BONDAGE OF DEBT 

85 Wars and depressions cause governments to go in debt to meet the emergencies. 

Unbalanced budgets usually result in monetary inflation accompanied by rising prices, over optimism, speculation and false prosperity. During such periods many people lose their sense of values, rush headlong into debt to buy more sheep, more land, more stocks and bonds, more of anything because prices are rising and prospective profits are great. Even the most conservative are often blinded by the confusion, by the trend of the times. Even Bishops and Stake Presidents have been known to mortgage a debt-free farm or debt-free sheep or cattle in order to buy more, because "everybody is doing it." And banks have frequently encouraged them in such speculative ventures, even though the land price or the sheep or cattle prices were double the normal price at the time of the venture.

When the break comes-and it always does come, for what goes up must come down-prices decline, land values shrink, banks close in on the loans and thousands find themselves bankrupt. The one who borrowed $9.00 per head on his debt-free herd of sheep to buy another herd at $18.00 per head, now finds that he still owes $9.00 per head on both herds that he would have difficulty in selling for $4 or $5 per head. So the bank is unwillingly forced into the sheep business, and the heretofore thrifty farmer finds himself bankrupt at a time in life when he should be turning his business over to his sons, so that he could retire, not rich, but financially independent. 

To be sure, there are those who strike it lucky during war time and during periods of prosperity. But they are the few, the exceptions that prove the rule.

They are like the one lucky gambler who wins at the expense of the other players who lose.

The lesson we learn from such experience is, that since periods of prosperity and depression do exist, and come so irregularly that forecasting such events in advance has been so far largely unsuccessful, long term debts are somewhat risky. They may be justified in the purchase of a home or a farm if there is a large enough margin of safety. But even then, one is gambling on future economic trends unknown to anyone.

Long term debts are especially risky and unwarranted for expansion purposes. For in such ventures one may jeopardize his debt-free property and the future economic status of the entire family. 

Of course, if one could accurately guess when prices were going up and know exactly when the break was coming, he would do well to go in debt at the beginning of the upturn, sell at the peak of prices, pay off the debt, and glory in his good luck. But that is a form of speculation no less risky than playing the market. It is a game in which approximately 93 percent lose while 7 percent win.

So until business cycles have been eliminated and prices have become more stabilized, debts should be avoided as far as possible, particularly for luxuries and unnecessary expansion. Installment credit is expensive and hazardous-the method of the unthrifty. Other debt structures are dependent upon a continuous state of prosperity, which seems to have vanished from the earth. Hence they are necessarily speculative and exceedingly risky in such turbulent times.

1. Why is so much business done on a credit basis today? 

2. Why is installment credit so expensive?

THE BONDAGE OF DEBT 

87 3. What would happen to business cycles if everyone paid cash? 

4. When is it safe to go in debt? When is it not safe or wise? 

5. What advice have the Church leaders given in times past about going in debt? About paying one's debts? 

6. Do you know of anyone who has made money by going in debt? Do you know of any who have suffered financially by going in debt too heavily and at the wrong time?
