Improvement Era, June, 1938. Smith, Joseph F., Gospel Doctrine, page 325. Warren and Pearson, Prices, Chapter XI. (John Wiley & Sons.) Jordan, David F., Managing Personal Finances, (Pren tice Hall.)

Chapter 10 DISTRIBUTION OF WEALTH AND INCOME 

"A noble income nobly expended, is no common sight. It is far more easy to acquire a fortune like a knave, than to spend it like a gentleman. If we exhaust our income in schemes of ambition, we shall purchase disappointment; if in law, vexation; if in luxury, disease. What we shall lend, we shall most probably lose; what we spend rationally, we shall enjoy; what we distribute to the deserving, we shall enjoy and retain."-Colton. 

Connection of Wealth and Income Inequalities. In

Chapter 8, the inequalities of wealth and income among the people of the world, particularly in the United States, were briefly discussed. We found that the so-called capitalistic system as it works today in America and in most European countries has been unusually efficient in the creation of wealth. It has encouraged a high degree of specialization, extreme division of labor, and has fostered private initiative.

But since all capitalistic systems are founded upon the institution of private property, inheritance and the profit motive, great inequalities of ownership and income inevitably result.

Since no solution to the problems arising out of such a situation were included in the discussion in Chapter 8, perhaps it might be well for us to consider next, how we might preserve the desirable features of this very efficient super-economic organization and yet bring about a more equitable distribution of the products of the system.

In other words, how can we reduce unemployment, eliminate extreme poverty among the lower classes and secure greater economic welfare for the less fortunate

DISTRIBUTION OF WEALTH AND INCOME 89 

members of society, without destroying the moral fibre of those who receive aid, or the individual initiative of those who create the wealth?

Would it be advisable to divide the wealth equally among all the people, even if it were legally possible? 

Could the economic machinery continue to operate as effectively, divided up into so many units or even into so many ownerships? In the United States the amount of wealth per capita would be worth approximately $3,000.00 and much less in other countries. 

Assuming that such a division of wealth would greatly impair the productive efficiency of our economic organization, then what alternatives are there that might improve the situation?

Progressive Taxation. Among the more plausible suggestions offered to correct existing abuses without adversely affecting the productive system, is to continue the socialization of our service institutions through a system of progressive taxation based upon ability to pay. 

In other words, to let such captains of industry, and financial geniuses as the Mellons, the Morgans, the

Fords and the Rockefellers, continue to produce wealth and provide employment in the future, much as they have in the past, but through a scientifically worked out tax system, taking the bulk of their profits to finance free education, free libraries, free public parks and recreation centers, unemployment insurance, old age benefits, sickness and accident insurance, and perhaps eventually free medical aid and hospital service. 

Such a program, introduced step by step, is in fact nothing less than a panorama of the social reforms that have taken place during the past hundred years and that are on the agenda for legislative consideration during the decades immediately ahead of us.

Diffusion of Benefits. It will be remembered that when the Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints was organized, there were practically no free schools, no state road systems and no community health clinics. 

The Mormon Church established the first school in Utah. They (the Mormons) provided secondary education for their young people through Church supported academies, until the tax-supported (free) high schools came into existence. Following the same policy, the 

L. D. S. Church established the Brigham Young University and junior colleges in Utah, Idaho and Arizona. 

But, with few exceptions, the Church has withdrawn from the secular education field, as fast as the state and local governments have established tax-supported systems to take their place, as they logically should do.

Thus we see how the Mormon Church, through the wisdom and foresight of its leaders, has led the way in this movement toward a greater diffusion of the benefits of our economic progress. They have set the pace for a greater realization of the Christian ideal of the brotherhood of men, through a wider distribution of economic and temporal things as well as in religious and spiritual blessings. 

Steps Towards Brotherhood. The ultimate outcome of such a proposal may not differ greatly from the plan advocated by the Prophet Joseph Smith in the Law of Consecration and Stewardship, where each person is assigned economic responsibility according to his respective ability, and where all surplus production is turned over to the Bishops to be used in providing for the economic, social and spiritual needs of the entire Church membership, including aid to the less fortunate

DISTRIBUTION OF WEALTH AND INCOME 91 

members, be they young or old, able-bodied or crippled, leaders or lay members.¹

Under such an economic system the profit motive for private gain and personal aggrandisement will become less important, necessitating other incentives for achievement and other measures of success. The opportunity for leadership and for service in the betterment of mankind may become more significant as the amassing of wealth becomes more difficult and more futile. Greater economic security will take precedence over exploitation, speculation and "get rich quick" schemes generally. 

The average family may not have much more money, if any, to spend under such a system than now. But if education can be provided free, regardless of the number of children; if police protection, health and sanitation inspection, recreational facilities and other social welfare services, can be had without cost, then the meagre family income can be devoted entirely to the necessities of life, plus some of the comforts now enjoyed by the higher income classes, and perhaps even a few luxuries eventually—if the system is progressively extended. 

For if hospitalization, medical and dental care, music and art education are added to the list of free services; and then if old age benefits, unemployment insurance and similar social reforms are also provided for those in need, out of the tax receipts, surely the average family income will be greatly augmented. True, the income may not appear in the weekly pay check, but the total goods and services available to the family in the form of education, medical care, and social security benefits, if paid for on a cost basis, would amount to a surprisingly large cash income. 

¹See Chapter 15 for a fuller discussion of the law of Consecration.

To finance all of this, of course, will necessitate huge sums of money. It will require an extremely efficient economic system to turn out all of the desired goods and services. And it will also require a carefully worked out tax system so that every one will contribute according to his financial ability. Inheritance and estate taxes will become progressively higher, until the present system of permitting large fortunes to be passed on from generation to generation will become extinct. And incidentally, the so-called idle rich who have been living on the earnings of past generations will be no more. 

However, it will always be possible for anyone who is energetic and thrifty to accumulate a substantial competence to care for his own retirement period, without having to depend upon the Government old age program. 

But he would probably not be permitted to leave an inheritance worth millions of dollars for his children to quarrel over. The major part of his surplus wealth would revert back to society when he died. 

1. How can we reduce inequalities of wealth and income and still retain the qualities of individual initiative? 

2. If we eliminate the profit motive will we destroy individual initiative? 

3. What incentives do Church workers have to spur them on to maximum achievement? 

4. Is it not wrong to take wealth away from people after they have struggled so hard to acquire it? What if the wealth is acquired through inheritance? 

5. Is there a difference between confiscating wealth and taxing the income from the wealth as it is produced? 

References: Edie, L. J., Principles and Problems in Economics, chapter 25. Doctrine and Covenants.

Chapter 11 

THE POWER OF SELF-HELP 

"Though the people support the Government, the Government should not support the people."-Grover Cleveland. 

Man's Inclination to Rely on Others. Most of us are inclined to blame someone else or some outside force when things go wrong. When the crops fail we blame the weather man, the poisoned soil or the shyster that sold us poor seed. If prices are too low, it is the middlemen, the speculators, or the Government interference that is to blame.

In a similar way we frequently justify our actions in buying an automobile, that we cannot afford, by a process of rationalization, by building up an elaborate set of reasons why we needed the new car. Perhaps the old car would cost too much to fix up; we got such a good offer for the old car that we could not afford to turn it down; or we had to have the car to go to work with; or the wife really needed a car herself because the other car was always in use when she needed it. 

We not only indulge in self-justification in defense of our extravagences and blame the government or others for our mismanagement or misfortunes, but we are more and more coming to depend upon the Government, the banks and even the Church to get us out of our troubles. Too many of us are inclined to believe in and rely on a Santa Claus 365 days out of a year. We seem to have lost faith in our own efforts, and hence turn to the Government to do something about it. We want a law passed to stabilize the price of meat, a tariff to keep out the foreign grown grain, or a

subsidy to help the beekeepers, the onion growers or the cotton planters. In short, we want the price of our products kept up on a high plateau, and the price of all other things low so our money will go farther, and we want subsidies, social security benefits and tax exemption, all at the same time.

To be sure, there are many calamities, financial and otherwise, over which man has no control, be he ever so good a manager, and there are, certainly, many functions that our government can perform and many things that our Church can do, that cannot be done by an individual or even a small group of individuals. The 

Government and the Church both are organized to render service to their members in the fields best suited to their purposes and spheres of action. 

Need for More Help. However, too many of us seem to have lost the beam of light that points to the power of self-help. The resourcefulness of the pioneer seems to have become dimmed as the industrialization has, step by step, made us more dependent upon each other; upon the local community, the state and the 

Church. When financial reverses strike us, we turn immediately to the group for help-we seem at a loss, many of us, to know how to adjust to the changed conditions on our own power, our own resourcefulness.

Of course, the highly industrialized setup of our modern economic organization accounts for much of this present day dependency. Men make plans as though their jobs were to continue indefinitely, with a strong probability of a raise in pay each year. No allowance is made for a long siege of unemployment. No reserves are set aside. No alternative method is planned for providing food, or even supplementing the reduced family income with a garden, a cow or a few chickens.

THE POWER OF SELF-HELP 

95 Few men have prepared themselves for an alternative occupation; many are not even adequately trained for their present jobs even sufficiently to hold on during a period of slack employment. They have taken their jobs for granted; lived right up to their income, or beyond; they have made no plans for an emergency, depending upon the blind hope or faith that somehow the Government, the Church or a magic Santa 

Claus will take care of them. "Certainly, someone will take care of us-they will have to."

Ways of Self-Help. There are many things that a farm family can do to help themselves in times of adversity and even in normal times. Most farms have enough waste to feed a few chickens, some pigs, and perhaps one or more cows. They usually have adequate land and water for a garden. Generally such enterprises can be managed on the side with the help of the women folks and the children. And when well cared for, such side-line enterprises can almost feed the family and may even supplement the family income through marketing the surplus. In fact, during periods of severe economic depression, such side lines have frequently grown into major enterprises. But their main function is the added economic security they give through selfhelp.

In normal times most farmers can usually increase the family income by improving the quality of their products, by enlarging the size of their business and by increasing their efficiency through better organization and better management. The quality of the product can be improved by securing better seed, by proper care of the crop, by grading and standardization, and by careful preparation for a shipment or for market. 

If poultry, dairy cows or range livestock are kept,

the boarders can be eliminated through careful testing, culling and breeding, and the production of eggs, milk or wool can be increased by supplementing the breeding program with proper feeding, intelligent care and good management.

On most farms the volume of business done is too small to absorb the overhead costs or permit the optimum use of machines and equipment or to permit the best farm practices. Such deficiencies can be corrected only by increasing the size of business. But most farmers could enlarge their size of business appreciably through their own efforts-without the help of the Government --and could thereby increase the family income. They might arrange to rent more land, add poultry, grow more intensive crops, go in for purebred stock, grade A milk, extra fancy fruit or seed potatoes, etc.

A successful farm will have enough profitable work to keep from two to four men busy the year around, or their equivalent. If the farm business is smaller than that, an enlargement program should be projected to increase the size of business by one or more of the ways suggested above, until the optimum economic unit is reached and the family income raised to a more satisfactory level.

City folks usually find it more difficult to apply the principle of self-help to their problem. They cannot always have a garden or keep a cow, a pig or a hen in town unless they live out some distance. In such instances, individual initiative must be developed along other lines. The children learn to do odd jobs like mowing lawns, delivering newspapers, working in stores, and so on, to supplement the family income. Sometimes the house can be remodeled at a little expense into a duplex, or with extra sleeping rooms, as an aid to balanc-

THE POWER OF SELF-HELP 

97 ing the budget. Occasionally, the family can operate a corner grocery store, a floral shop, or a general repair shop, according to their talents and opportunities. 

One family, whose finances were disrupted during the depression, showed their resourcefulness and power of self-help by digging worms to sell to fishermen. The idea worked so well that, before long, this temporary make-shift side line became a full-time job for the whole family. Eventually, the business expanded to outside markets through mail-order and express shipments, and the family income soon exceeded their best efforts before the depression.

However, cities are pretty tough places to be in during periods of unemployment. A family who lives entirely out of a tin can and a paper sack, and whose rent must be paid the first of every month, is really in a desperate situation when the factory or the mine closes down. Usually there is no savings account and no way of getting along without money. Getting another job is generally quite difficult, if not impossible. 

Perhaps some day our industrial system will be changed so people will not live that way. They should have greater security in their job or be provided with an independent means of subsistence when the job folds up during unprofitable periods. But in the meanwhile, each family should study its own situation carefully, examine its own talents and possibilities, and through the power of self-help, develop some side lines, some means of economic security aside from the regular job. 

If possible, every member of the family should acquire additional skill in some avocation. Schools now provide training in all lines for all ages. It is a day of education for all, a day of training and specialization for specific jobs and occupations. There is always a

market for skilled workers and trained artisans. There is always work and opportunity for the person who is resourceful, who has ideas and who is industrious and ambitious.

The power of self-help must not be lost sight of or allowed to disintegrate. We must not allow ourselves to be led into a false security, dependent upon Government support or Church aid, for the people must support the Government and the Church, but never the reverse. 

1. Approximately one-fifth of the population are now dependent upon the Government for support. What might that lead to? 

2. What can a farmer do to insure his family against financial storms? 

3. What can city folks do to attain greater economic security during periods of economic stress? 

4. How can a family improve its earning power? How can individuals improve their earning capacity? 

5. How can the Church aid such a program?

Chapter 12 THE NEED OF OPPORTUNITY 

"The secret of success in life is for a man to be ready for his opportunity when it comes."-Disraeli. "The crowning fortune of a man is to be born to some pursuit which finds him in employment and happiness, whether it be to make baskets, or broadswords, or canals, or statues, or songs."- Emerson. 

Are There No More Opportunities in the World? "There are no longer any good chances for young men," complained a youthful law student to Daniel

Webster. "There is always room at the top," replied the great statesman and jurist. "No chance, no opportunities, in a land where thousands of poor boys become rich men, where newsboys go to Congress, where those born in the lowest stations attain the highest positions? The world is all gates, all opportunities to him who will use them," says 

Orison Swett Marden in Pushing to the Front.

Yet the law of chance has played an important role in the lives of most men. Some have been more fortunate than others in locating where the basic resources were more abundant, where even a second-class man had good chances to succeed. While others, far more capable, have wasted a life time trying to eke out an existence where the odds were against them, where they were rowing upstream all the time, but getting nowhere. 

All of the principles of self-help and all of the qualities of persistence, energy and enthusiasm cannot make a silk purse out of a sow's ear. And so it is with many persevering, self-sacrificing souls. They are unwittingly wasting their talents on worthless mining

claims, poor farming land, or in the employ of organizations of no promise.

Of course there are opportunities left today-millions of opportunities-far greater than existed at any other period in the world's history. There are abundant opportunities for both men and women, for young and old, for rich and poor. But we must learn to differentiate between the genuine and the imitation, between the job with a future and the job in a blind alley, and between the land of promise and the land of desolation. 

Function of Church Welfare Program. No small part of the Church Welfare Program is to aid its unfortunate members who are marooned on an island of despair, or who have allowed themselves to get into a hopeless rut of misfortune. The Church, through its 

Welfare organization and through the Priesthood quorums, hopes to aid such families to move into greener pastures, where they will have a better chance to make a go of it, a chance to really exercise the principles of self-help in improving the economic and social welfare of the whole family.

Responsibility of the Priesthood Quorums. The 

Priesthood quorums, as such, can do much to improve the opportunities of their fellow-members, or in helping them to get prepared for better opportunities when they become available. If each quorum would develop an attitude of fellowship, they would never rest content or let up until each member was located in as favorable an environment for him and his family as is humanly possible for them to achieve. Some fraternities keep such close check on their members that whenever an opening of promise shows up, they immediately endeavor to place one of their less fortunate members in the posi-

THE NEED OF OPPORTUNITY 

101 tion, in order that he may improve his opportunity, his chances for success.

Some Priesthood quorums have followed similar methods with remarkable success. They have not only improved the economic welfare of many of their members, but they have lifted the morale and fellowship status of the entire quorum into an envious position. 

Opportunities At Home. Frequently our greatest opportunities are right where we are, but we cannot see them. They have to be pointed out to us by someone else, perhaps a fellow quorum member. The old adage that "a rolling stone gathers no moss" is surely a truism. 

Men have been known to travel around the world in search of diamonds, oil fields, or other unusual opportunities, only to find them later-after it is too late-on their own farm that was sold for a pittance for funds to travel on.

The big problem is to recognize opportunities when we see them, or to be prepared for them when they appear. And there, again, is where Priesthood quorums can render great service, in aiding their members who are in a rut or in a blind alley, to improve their talents and skill through education and other ways, so that they can get out of the rut or blind alley into something more promising. With all of the part time schools, special training courses and vocational education classes that are being offered today, there is little excuse for anyone not having a trade or vocation where they can work into something with more promising opportunities, when an opening comes.

Of course, there are situations so hopeless and so desperate that only a change of location can correct the difficulties. In such cases, great care must be taken in selecting the new locality, the new farm or the new

company where one is to start anew. There are many factors to be taken into consideration in selecting a new job, a new community or a new farm, particularly where a growing family is involved.

Material opportunities are not all that should be considered. Proper health conditions, social standards and religious opportunities are far more important than the kind of house available for rent, the number of movies in the town, or the comparative wages offered. 

Usually, the opportunity with the greatest financial promise is the most hazardous, and may also have other serious drawbacks. Now that the frontier is no longer with us, the speculative and venturesome opportunities should be examined with great caution. They should be checked and double-checked. Ordinarily, a little greater security is better than the promise of high earnings, if the latter is irregular or undependable.

It was this same principle that led President Brigham Young to advise the Saints against mining in the early days, when the food supply was all important and the building of an empire, their major economic and social objective.

It was the same philosophy that led a great teacher to advise his students always to accept the job with the lowest pay. Strange advice, perhaps, but when considering opportunity in its broadest sense, and over a long period of time, the occupation, the job or the enterprise that offers only a moderate financial reward is usually accompanied by greater stability, more permanent security and a richer opportunity for social and spiritual development.

Need of Opportunity for Young People. Perhaps the most far-reaching problem in many of the Church communities today is the lack of economic opportunities

THE NEED OF OPPORTUNITY 

103 for the young people, who are ready to launch out on their own. Already farms have been divided and subdivided until they are too numerous and too small to be operated efficiently in competition with other areas. 

Hundreds of our high school and college graduates are leaving their State to find jobs elsewhere, right at a time when the community that financed their education should begin to benefit from the years of training and expense-when the educational investment should begin to pay dividends. Of course, it is laudable to educate these young people and then turn them over to other communities where there are growing branches of the ship in a larger circle, but it is expensive for the folks at home paying the bill. It is a continual drain on the talents of the community, and it sometimes works out unfavorably for the ones who have to migrate. They frequently get located in some strange community, with no social or church connections, and are soon lost track of. As a result, their social and religious life becomes warped; their family grows up indifferent to the principles of the Gospel, and all that it signifies. 

However, if these young people can be guided into communities where there are growing branches of the Church, where they can associate with their friends and with other Church members, then the outcome may not be so bad, so far as the ones who migrate are concerned. 

Also, if the resources at home are limited, and if their leaving helps to relieve the pressure of population in such areas, then a constant flow of young people into other areas where opportunities are more abundant and still expanding, is not only inevitable but will undoubtedly work out for the best interests of all. 

In any event, a comprehensive program of economic and social welfare must provide opportunities for each

new crop of young folks, to find profitable employment for them, a reasonable amount of economic security, and ample opportunity for social and spiritual development. Such a program should also provide adequate training and guidance for these young people, so they will recognize the opportunities around them or that may be offered to them, and be properly prepared to take advantage of all such opportunities when they appear. 

1. As we come to the end of the frontier, will economic opportunities diminish? 

2. For a young man just starting out for himself, how do the opportunities of today compare with earlier periods? 

3. How much chance is there in opportunity? How does advance preparation and training affect the chances? 

4. How can the Church Welfare program improve the opportunities of Church members? 

5. Where do the Priesthood quorums fit into this phase of the 

Welfare program? 

6. What should we do about the relatively large migration of our young people from the rural communities to the urban communities; from Utah to other states? etc.

Chapter 13 THE ROLE OF GOVERNMENT 

"We are more heavily taxed by our idleness, pride, and folly than we are taxed by the government."-Franklin. 

Government and Economic Welfare. Any plan for improving our economic security or social welfare must take into consideration the increasing role of government in human affairs. There was a time when governmental functions were very few and simple. 

They offered protection from enemies, looked after the legal affairs of state, and settled the major disputes among their constituents. But, today, there is scarcely a phase of our life with all of its complexities that government does not touch in one way or another. And the trend seems to be toward even more government; more regulation; more supervision; more operation. 

Government, an Economic Burden or Expense?

Some persons have peculiar notions about our Government, its activities, services and costs. They look upon the cost of government as a burden upon society. Taxes are considered an evil and something to be avoided (or evaded) if possible.

In reality, the expense of government is no more of a burden than the cost of telephone, transportation or postal service. For example, if a business concern should purchase a truck for delivery service, that would be a legitimate expense. But if the same business is asked to pay a gasoline tax to help construct and maintain a hard surface road over which the truck is to operate, that would probably be regarded as a burden, because it is a governmental function.

Likewise the typical taxpayer considers his payments for fire insurance an ordinary expense. But he "knows" that his payments in the form of taxes to keep a trained and well-equipped fire department to protect his home and property are not an expense, but a burden. Similarly, the cost of his summer cottage or his country club is not a burden, though the cost of public parks is a burden; the education of his daughter at a private musical conservatory is merely an expense, but payments through taxes for their education in public schools is a burden. 

Demand for Increased Governmental Services. In spite of this inexplainable attitude toward taxes and government, people continue to press for more governmental services. The question is not one of expense, if we really want the service; it is merely a question of who can render the service best at the least cost. If the Government can build our roads, provide public parks and educate our children more effectively and more efficiently than we could do it ourselves, then the people are justified in shifting such functions from private service to public or governmental service. 

Some of the more recent fields of governmental intervention are along the lines of economic and social welfare. Considerable labor legislation, including minimum wage laws, compensation and insurance laws, eight-hour day laws, child labor laws, and laws requiring factory and mine sanitation have been enacted in recent years.

Public health is being promoted through clinics, hospitals, publicity and medical supervision. Community recreation is provided through public parks and supervised play grounds. Pure food laws regulate what we eat and drink.

THE ROLE OF GOVERNMENT 

107 In many respects the government has become an agency of social reform. Censorship decrees the clothes we wear, the literature that we may read, and the theatrical entertainments that we may see. Of course, not all of its policies are wisely formulated. But in general, the weight of government influence has been cast decidedly on the side of social betterment. 

We must recognize, too, the manifold productive functions of government in our complex economic world. By preserving the rights of private property, by maintaining freedom of contract, by defining and enforcing the terms of free competition, by extending the sphere of government ownership and operations where expediency demands, and by conserving the natural resources, the government exercises important economic functions that could not be performed so effectively by private agencies.

Apportioning the Cost of Government-Taxation. 

But, of course, all of these added functions of government cost money, and they must be paid for by the people in the form of taxation. The big problem, therefore, is to apportion this governmental burden as fairly and as equitably as is humanly possible among the beneficiaries who have ability to pay.

Thus taxation has come to be an important element in every family budget; in every business venture. 

And as governmental functions expand, governmental expenses will increase. To pay for these added services will require a larger and larger share of the family's income to be turned over to the government in the form of taxes, of one kind or another.

Originally the general property tax, with a very moderate levy, provided all the revenue required for the simple functions of government. At that time, most

people lived on farms, and their ability to support the government could be measured fairly well by the amount of property a man owned. For example, if one man owned 100 acres, he might be assessed $10 per year as his share of the governmental expense; whereas, his neighbor, with 200 acres, would be asked to pay $20 for governmental services.

The amounts were small, and the measure of taxable ability seemed fair enough then, but as more and more people moved into cities and earned their living from wages, fees, and commissions, or from interest, rents and dividends, then the amount of tangible property owned was no longer an accurate measure of ability to support government. 

Consequently, new forms of taxation had to be devised to supplement the general property tax, and to bring in the additional revenue required from year to year to meet the expanding needs of government. 

Thus we now have the income tax, the sales tax, the tobacco tax, the beer tax, the gasoline tax, the excess profits tax, the gift tax, the inheritance tax, and many others. 

They are so many and varied that no matter what one's occupation is, in what form his wealth exists or in what state he lives, or dies, he must contribute his share to the government treasury-according to his financial ability, as measured by these varied and numerous pipe lines into his income and expenditures. 

To be sure, our present tax system has many defects.

Assessment practices are far from perfect. Some governmental agencies are woefully inefficient. As a result, many persons and businesses become proficient at shifting the tax on to some one else, or in evading it entirely. Students of taxation, legislators and tax officials are constantly studying the problems and con-

THE ROLE OF GOVERNMENT 

109 stantly modifying the laws and methods of assessment and collection.

Some taxes are too expensive to collect for the revenue they yield. Some tax the poor proportionately too much. Others hit the home owner, the wage-earner or the farmer too hard. Still others tend to retard business enterprise unduly, and thus reduce economic output.

But if we want governmental services, they must be paid for; and in a capitalistic system, where individual initiative and freedom of enterprise is preserved, taxation in one form or another is the only way these expenses can be met. The only alternative is a socialistic system, where the government owns and operates all forms of productive wealth.

So if we want to preserve our present economic system, and still have the many governmental services it is trying to perform, we shall have to get used to taxation, and plenty of it. Of course, we shall try to perfect these devious ways of collecting revenue for the government, but it must be collected one way or another. 

Increased Governmental Services Mean Increased

Taxation. However, we must also become more conscious of the fact that each new governmental service will require additional funds, which means heavier taxation. Consequently, if we are getting concerned over the amount of our tax "burden," we shall have to curtail our demands for increased governmental service. We simply cannot continue to add new forms of education, public health service, unemployment insurance, old age pensions and work relief projects, without taxation to pay the bills. If they are necessary, and if we can afford such services, if that is the wisest way

to use our surplus income, then the new program is justified and we should not complain at high taxes, if we are paying no more than our just share. 

But if the tax load is really becoming a burden, perhaps we are becoming a little governmentally extravagant, just as any other luxury expenditure, beyond our financial ability, would be considered extravagant. 

The problem is one for each citizen to ponder over, to study carefully, and to vote on intelligently when the opportunity presents itself.

1. How does the governmental sphere of activity of one hundred years ago compare with the numerous services of today? 

2. In what fields of activity has the Government expanded most. 

3. How do the governments of European countries compare with ours in scope and effectiveness? 

4. One writer maintains that our tax system is antiquated. 

What are its major defects? 

5. What reforms in taxation would be desirable? 

6. What difficulties arise in devising a more perfect tax system? 

7. What principles should guide our efforts?

Essays in Taxation. Edie, L. D., Economics, Principles and Problems... Plehn, C. C., Introduction to Public Finance.

Chapter 14 

"While the primary object of a cooperative marketing association is to improve the farm incomes of its members, it should function, also, to enrich the social phases of life of the community in which it operates. Cooperation is essentially a spiritual movement, and organizations operate in accord with the members of a community, uniting them in support of educational, religious, and civic agencies generally."-Wilson Gee. 

Beginnings of Cooperative Movement. Nowhere can a clearer picture be secured of the meaning of cooperation and its debt in this country to Old World influences than is found in the vivid experiences of the 

Rochdale pioneers in their immortal experiment in Toad 

Lane, Rochdale, England (1844).

These Rochdale weavers are generally looked upon as the pioneers of the standardized cooperative movement. They formulated not only the philosophy, but the technique for applying and working out their philosophy. After painstaking development of their ideas, they put them into operation. They opened their store. They carried out their plans. They succeeded, and from that day on there has never been a recession in the onward progress of the methods they inaugurated.

Since that early beginning the cooperative movement has spread throughout the world. It is especially strong in Denmark, Sweden and the northern European countries. It has also developed to a high degree in 

America, in certain agricultural marketing fields, such as dairy, poultry, and citrus fruit cooperatives. More recently, cooperative buying organizations appear to be developing quite rapidly, and some consumer coop-

eratives are successfully challenging the chain store domain in retail merchandising. 

Meaning of Cooperation. The term "cooperation" means, of course, "working together." But in recent years the term has taken on a new significance to many people in the United States. It means more than just working together. It carries the inference of an institution which may be used to lighten the burden of high prices and low wages through the elimination of the middleman and his profit, and through the management of the affairs of business enterprise by the individuals to whom the services are rendered. Then, it eliminates not only unnecessary costs, but also profits. Its distinguishing feature is that it exists for the common good of all its members. All property acquired becomes common to all members.

Basic Principles of Cooperative Organization. The basic principles of cooperation initiated by the Rochdale pioneers and accepted in this country, with some modifications, might be summarized as follows: (1) Unrestricted membership, with capital shares of low denomination, which may be paid for in installments. This is an important feature. Since the cooperative movement is above all a movement of farmers or of the working classes, it is essential that the financial undertaking may be made easy and within the range of their possibilities. (2) Capital invested in the society, if it receives interest, shall receive not more than a fixed percentage which must not be more than the prevalent rate. A cooperative organization is not in business to make profits for its stockholders, but to render service to and effect savings for its members. (3) Limitation of the number of shares to be held

by any one member. Members of means are not excluded, but in order that democracy may prevail, it is well that there should be no wide inequality in the members' financial standing in the society. (4) Democracy in government; with officers elected by and responsible to the members, and each member entitled to one vote only, irrespective of the number of shares he holds or the amount of business he does. This feature immediately eliminates any tendency toward control of the society by the more well-to-do members, as in a stock company. (5) If a surplus-saving (profit) accrues, by virtue of the difference between the net cost and the net selling price of the commodities and services, after meeting expenses, paying interest ("wages to capital"), and setting aside adequate depreciation reserves and other funds, the net surplus saving shall be used for the good of the members, for beneficient social purposes, or shall be returned to the patrons as savings-returns (dividends) in proportion to their patronage.

Types of Cooperative Enterprises. Cooperative enterprises have taken a number of different forms in 

America, which may be classified roughly as follows: (1) Cooperative credit and banking associations. (2) Cooperative associations for productionraw materials or finished products. (3) Association of producers to sell cooperatively either raw materials or finished products. (4) Consumer cooperatives or associations organized for buying supplies of either raw materials or finished products. 

Need for Cooperative Organizations. The American citizen is traditionally an intense individualist. This is particularly true of the American farmer who lives

amid the acres which he cultivates, often somewhat remote from his nearest neighbor. What he will plant and how much of it is determined largely by his own inclinations, and by the influences of what his father and his grandfather planted before him. As a rule, the products of his farm are marketed by him as an individual, with little regard for the effect upon his near and remote neighbors growing the same crops. 

Such rugged individualism frequently results in too much wheat and cotton, or too few eggs or T-bone steaks. Too often they are unloaded on the market all at the same time to depress prices unnecessarily. Too little attention is paid to quality and grading of product. In short, the individualist becomes a victim of a marketing system of middlemen, whose interests are neither the farmers' nor the consumers'.

A succinct statement of the situation in which the American farmer, as an individualist, finds himself today, has been made by Bernard M. Baruch, a distinguished finnacier and a close student of cooperative marketing, as follows: "The farmer selling in unlimited competition with himself, has been buying at more or less controlled prices from industries which have organized their production and marketing." 

Cooperative organizations are the agencies born of this need. The central purpose of such organizations is to develop the machinery by means of which farmers and other groups employ or become themselves their own middlemen; to perform efficiently and economically essential production, marketing or purchasing services. And through such systems of cooperative effort, once the cooperative ties have taken sufficient hold; through educational and possibly other procedures, the movement will prove effective in stabilizing price levels through orderly marketing, and may all

prove effective in the sensible adjustment of agricultural production to demand. 

Cooperatives Not a Panacea for All Ills. But of course we must realize that cooperative organizations are not a panacea for all of the ills of the farmer or the consumer. While there is much inherent magic in the movement, it does not promise to change a fool into a wise man, or a scoundrel into an honest citizen. It cannot eliminate the hundreds of inefficient farmers. 

Neither can it change the laws of nature, nor offer profits to the farmer who raises poor produce, nor bring a price higher than supply and demand justify. 

Nevertheless, cooperation is a movement of such wide extent and of such formidable proportions, that it cannot be dismissed as merely a Utopian dream. It is a very potent present force in world affairs. In a sense it harmonizes very well with the general aspirations of the people of the world in their movement toward greater democracy, and greater localization of the control of social functions; for the very fundamental principle of cooperation is that each individual in it shall have equal say with every other individual concerning the policies proposed and the operations carried on. In fact, some believe that the cooperative movement will continue to expand until eventually all production, distribution and consumption will be integrated and interwoven into one network of cooperative effort, resulting in a higher standard of living for all.

Attitude of the Church Toward Cooperation. At this point it may be well to remind our readers that "the Church stresses cooperation as a means of banishing poverty and providing for the needs of all men.' 991 

¹Widtsoe, John A., Program of the Church of Jesus Christ of 

Latter-day Saints.

The Latter-day Saints have practiced cooperation in many ways. The early irrigation canals were dug by the people who were to use the water, on a basis proportionate to the lands owned. The canal and the water rights became the joint property of the group. Many public buildings also have been erected in the same manner, and even private homes have been built by group effort under a cooperative plan. Business organizations like the Z. C. M. I. (Zion's Cooperative Mercantile Institution) originated in a similar manner in the early days of Church history. In fact the entire organization of the Mormon Church is a beehive of cooperative effort; a symbol of the whole cooperative movement at its best. 

And be it known to all, that some of the best managed and most successful cooperative marketing organizations in the United States are located in Utah and the Intermountain and Pacific Coast region. 

Factors Affecting Success of Cooperatives: In concluding this treatise on the strength of cooperation, perhaps we should at least call attention to a few of the major factors that have come to be associated with success in cooperative ventures wherever they have persisted for any length of time. Cooperative organizations have been a part of our natural social-economic structure for a long enough time for the experience gained in them to show what some of the more basic considerations in their successful cooperation have been. 

Some of these associations have been eminently successful, others only moderately so, and yet others have been dismal failures. Lessons have been learned from the former as to what is best to do, and from the latter as to what is best not to do. One of our outstanding treatises on agricultural cooperation designates seven

principles involved in a successful cooperative organization as follows:" (1). The association must meet an economic need. (2) The association should be soundly organized. (3) The association must be guided and supported by its members. (Loyalty on the part of its membership is absolutely essential to its integrity of functioning.) (4) Cooperative marketing associations must improve the grading and handling practices. (5) The association must handle an adequate volume of business. (6) The association must be efficiently managed and financially stable. (7) The association must make the community a better place in which to live.

1. Clarence Poe says, "The difference between a corporation and a cooperative is that a corporation is an aggregate of dollars for the purpose of hiring men to make profits for the dollars, while a cooperative marketing association is a collection of men for the purpose of hiring dollars to make profits for the men.' Explain. 11 

2. Is cooperative marketing to be considered a panacea for all ills of the farmer? 

3. How was cooperative effort utilized in the early history of 

Utah and the West? How today?

Chapter 15 

"We believe that if other communities would adopt the plans of consecration, fasting, and tithing, which the Lord has revealed to the Latter-day Saints, and carry them out in spirit, with faith and works, that poverty and pauperism would be greatly reduced or entirely overcome."-Joseph F. Smith.

Comprehensive Plan of the Church. The L. D. S. 

Church through its divinely inspired organization provides for both the temporal and spiritual salvation of its members. It is probably the most comprehensive program of human welfare ever instituted for the benefit of mankind. Besides the religious experiences and social activities provided for all ages, from the cradle to the grave, the economic welfare and security of its members is ever foremost in the plans and workings of the Church. 

Financial Plan of the Church. To carry out such an ambitious economic, social and spiritual program requires a superior type of financial plan that will insure continuous success of the numerous projects, organizations and institutions operating throughout most of the civilized world. Such a financial plan must provide adequate revenue with a minimum of cost in administration, and without being compulsory, unpopular or burdensome to the members.

Such standards of perfection are truly difficult to meet. Many worthy religious and social movements have failed either through inadequacy or poor management of finances. Many governmental units find it extremely difficult to carry on the necessary functions of government through lack of funds, even though they

have the legal power of taxation, of compulsory contribution.

Yet the Mormon Church has continued to grow and expand its ambitious program for over one hundred years, with a financial plan based upon voluntary contributions; and we find the Church today with one of the strongest credit ratings in the Nation. It is completely free from debt-free from mortgages, from assignments, or from any financial obligations, except its current monthly bills and payrolls. But the L. D. S. financial scheme is a divinely inspired plan of freewill offerings, geared to the members' financial ability to pay. 

It is collected locally by the Bishops of over 1,000 Wards. 

It may be paid weekly, monthly or yearly, according to the receipt of the family income. Hence the cost of collection is negligible, the method of payment is convenient to the members, and the financial burden is limited to a definite percentage of earnings.

Justification of Church Contributions. Of course some people question the advisability of contributing so much to a Church program as the L. D. S. people are asked to donate. They are not converted to the social or spiritual values promised from participation in such an ambitious religious endeavor. They prefer to spend their earnings as they wish, doing a little private charity here and there, and aiding the Community Chest, the Red Cross, and similar public welfare movements. 

But such people do not realize or appreciate fully the greater efficiency of organized endeavor. It is the same selfish psychology that leads many people to look upon all forms of taxation as a burden. They fail to realize that, with few exceptions, the average citizen gets more for his tax dollar than for any other dollar he spends. This is because the Government can do so

many things for him that he could not do for himselfand do them far better. The operation of schools, the building of roads, the disposal of garbage, the protection of property and life, the provision for the unemployed, are all examples of governmental services considered essential and proper for governmental agencies to handle.

In like manner, the L. D. S. financial program can be fully justified from a practical point of view, on the basis of comparative value received; for most of the 

Church activities are of such a nature that they cannot be duplicated either by individual performance or governmental service. The Church organizations provide a peculiar type of education, of social activities, and of spiritual enrichment that could not be obtained in any other way. Such unique services require a specially designed organization with a highly qualified personnel, and with a flexibility of operation not found in private enterprise or governmental agencies.

Furthermore, without Church organization, how could an individual carry on temple work for the living and the dead; religious education for the young; missionary work throughout the civilized world; religious activity for the entire family; and proper care for the needy? Without adequate finances, how could a Church organization carry on these necessary activities?

Therefore, in a progressive Christian community, where one is expected to be his brother's keeper, to love his neighbor as himself, and to love the Lord with all his mind, might and strength, it is taken for granted by most conscientious people that a Church organization is the most effective way of doing certain things. 

To carry on such a program requires an equally ambitious financial program. However, if the voluntary

contributions made by the members of such oraganizations buy more and better social, religious and spiritual values than could be purchased by the individual-had he kept the money himself to spend as he saw fit-then the financial requirements of the program, if fairly assessed, is fully justified from an economic point of view, as well as from a religious or spiritual point of view. 

The Joy of Giving. But there is another feature of the L. D. S. Church financial plan that deserves special consideration. It is the joy and satisfaction one gets from giving to a worthy cause. When Christ said. that it was better to give than to receive, he enunciated a very fundamental doctrine of human nature, and perhaps the best verification of this principle is found in the extensive Christmas traditions and customs that have developed in commemoration of the birth of this greatest of all teachers and philosophers. The elaborate system of exchanging gifts among friends, of giving to the poor and of making little children happy (and parents happier) through the Santa Claus-gifts-tradition, is ample proof of the psychological principle involved in the joy of giving.

So it is with the Mormon Church voluntary system of tithes and offerings. Church members secure great joy and satisfaction from their freewill offerings, more so than from other funds otherwise expended. And perhaps that is why they generally look upon their Church donations as being less of a burden than their tax payments for governmental services, which are mandatory and frequently inequitably assessed. 

It is also a belief among conscientious tithepayers and consistent fast offering donors that freewill offerings produce greater moral and spiritual strength among those who give fully and whole-heartedly. Whether one

gives money, goods or talents, if the gift be for a worthy purpose, increased personal power usually follows. And the wise, generous giver usually prospers more than the stingly, niggardly person.

But since all offerings of the Mormon Church are voluntary, with no undue pressure brought to secure them, the members are left free to do as they choose. 

However, the purpose of giving is continually explained to the members; they are frequently reminded of the divine principle of giving, with the accompanying gifts and blessings promised those who participate in the program.

Kinds of Offerings. Three main types of freewill offerings are included in the Church financial program. 

The first and most important is the tithing-one-tenth of one's annual increase, net earnings, net income, wage income, or income from commissions, fees, etc. This is given to the Church through the Ward Bishop for general Church purposes. These tithes, given in the form of money or produce, are used for the building and maintenance of temples, meetinghouses and other Church edifices; for the support of educational and missionary systems; for the care of the sick and the indigent, and for other Church activities.

The second type of freewill contribution is called the fast offering. This is so-named because once a month the members of the Church fast for from one to three meals. A sum of money equivalent in value to the amount saved by the meals not eaten, is also given to the Bishop, who uses part of it in helping the poor in his own Ward, and the balance is turned over to the Church Welfare committee to be used in the ecomonic rehabilitation of members who are in adverse financial circumstances.

Such an occasional fast is supposed to be good for the body, and the saving thus effected may be used in a very efficient program of human conservation and rehabilitation. Furthermore, if all people were to adopt such a system of caring for the needy, it is probable that considerable progress would be made, through a very simple and inexpensive method, in caring for the underprivileged members of society. 

Under the third type of freewill offerings are grouped such contributions as special gifts to help build ward chapels, contributions to missionary funds, ward maintenance, membership dues, and special donations for boy scout work, ward outings, socials, etc.

Such contributions vary with the different wards and communities, with the extensiveness of their local program, their aspirations and their financial ability. 

Some wards have what is called a budget system, that combines most of these activities under one financial program. The Bishopric estimate the yearly cost of the entire program, levy a suggested schedule of contributions per family according to their known financial ability, and then attempt to live within the budget according to the revenue received, without further call upon the members except for emergencies.

Success of Church Financial System. The appar ent success of this freewill offering type of financial system in caring for such an ambitious educational, social and religious program is due first to its inspired origin, second to its voluntary features, and third to its efficient management,-the latter partially accounted for in the small margin of overhead required in its administration, due to the almost complete absence of paid personnel in Church service.

1. If all members of the Church paid an honest tithing, would there be sufficient revenue to finance all of the needs of the Church? 

2. Should the practice of fast offerings become world-wide, would the problems of charity and relief be solved? 

3. Why is one justified from an economic point of view in giving such a large percentage of his income to a church? 

4. Suppose there were no churches, to what extent would individuals be engaged in Christian endeavors now performed by the Church? 

5. How could Christ's statement be true, "It is better to give than to receive? 

6. To what extent has the Mormon Church financial system been a success?
