Widtsoe. Improvement Era, August, 1938.

Chapter 16 THE LAW OF CONSECRATION 

"And behold, thou wilt remember the poor, and consecrate of thy properties for their support that which thou hast to impart unto them with a covenant and a deed which cannot be broken. 

Every man shall be made accountable unto me, a steward over his own property, or that which he has received by consecration, as much as is sufficient for himself and family. “And again, if there shall be properties in the hands of the Church or any individuals of it, more than is necessary for their support, after this first consecration, which is a residue to be consecrated unto the Bishop, it shall be kept to administer to those who have not, from time to time, that every man who has need may be amply supplied, and receive according to his wants." (D. & C., Sec. 42:30-34.)

Origin of the Law of Consecration. A new social order was given to the Saints in Kirtland, Ohio, in February, 1831, by the Prophet Joseph Smith. The first attempt to establish the new order was made at Thompson, Ohio, in May, 1831, by Saints from Coleville, New York, some of the most devoted of the Prophet's followers. However, the Ohio community did not get completely organized before they left for Missouri, where the center stake of Zion was to be established. 

The second attempt to institute the "Law of Consecration" was in Jackson County, Missouri, again under adverse circumstances, due to the influx of so many poor Saints from other sections. Before it had a thorough chance to be tried out, the Mormon communities began to suffer from outside persecution, were driven first into Illinois, and finally into the Rocky Mountains. 

¹Fox, F. Y., Social Security Program of the Mormon Church under Joseph Smith and Brigham Young. (A paper read before the Utah Academy of Sciences, Arts and Letters, May, 1938.)

The third and final attempt to establish this unique plan of living was attempted in Utah in 1874. It was first started in St. George, later in Richfield, Brigham 

City and Orderville. The new order flourished for a short period, eventually developed internal troubles, and was finally abandoned after two or three years of existence in most places, and after a period of ten years' duration in Orderville, the most idealistic and successful of the so-called United Order experiments.

Differences in the Law of Consecration Plan and the United Order. It will be observed that Joseph

Smith always referred to his new social order as "The Law of Consecration and Stewardship," whereas the 

Utah experiments established under the direction of 

Brigham Young were called the "United Order." The former was given to the Saints through divine revelation, as a superior economic and social plan of life, if the people were unselfish enough to live such an idealistic system. It contemplates that each individual shall give over to the Church all of his property. The transfer is made by a legal deed of gift, and a special "covenant that cannot be broken." The applicant thus enters the Order of Stewardship by a nact of consecration, evidenced by a transfer to the Bishop as the agent for the Church of all that he possesses. 

Then the Bishop assigns to the member a stewardship, which permits him to manage the property assigned to him with almost complete freedom. He is allowed to retain sufficient of the product of his labor and management to care for the needs of himself and family, and then any surplus over and above his living 'Ibid. 

'Doctrine and Covenants 42:30-32.

THE LAW OF CONSECRATION 

127 is turned over to the Bishop for the benefit of the Church.

This act of consecration and stewardship assignment also entitles the individual and his family to participate in the productive life of the community. 

For example, in Missouri every head of a family was to receive a building lot in the city. The farmers were to receive, in addition, an allotment of land outside of the city. The mechanic was to receive the necessary tools and materials for his trade. Teachers, writers, musicians, etc., were to have a home site, a license, and an appointment to serve the community according to their respective abilities. And they were entitled to participate in the produce of the farmer, the miller, and the clothing manufacturers, according to their needs, the size of the family, etc.

The essence of the plan was to give each person a stewardship according to his training, talents, and ability; to give him full responsibility over his stewardship, and to make the most of it that his capabilites and circumstances would permit. If he proved to be an unusually efficient producer, he would get satisfaction out of the amount of surplus he could turn over to the Church for the use of the entire community-always, of course, under the supervision of the Bishop. If he were a school teacher, he would be expected to render the highest type of service he was capable of, depnding upon the Church surplus funds and commodities for his living. In such cases, success would be measured by the effectiveness of his service-by the fruits of his teaching.

The United Order, as it was established in Utah, was a modification of the stewardship plans instituted in Ohio and Missouri. Those who participated in the

Utah movement consecrated all of their property to the United Order corporation, but lived as one big family, and not as stewardships individually operated. The consecrated property became community property and was managed by the chosen leaders of the Order. The members of the Order were assigned to different tasks and types of work by the leaders, according to the program of work outlined from day to day. 

In Orderville, where the plan was operated more idealistically, the families all ate at one table, lived in "shanties," all grouped together, and worked as a unit in all enterprises-"all for one and one for all." There were no individual holdings, no individual management, and no family operated units.

Of course there was considerable variation in the operation of the plan in the different communities. There were varying degrees of individualistic operation and of "United Order" operation. In the larger cities, a considerable amount of property was consecrated to the Chuch by actual deeds, legally executed. But, generally, the transfer was only nominal, the individual actually retaining complete control and management of the property so consecrated. Such, in fact, was the manner in which Brigham Young himself participated in the movement. He consecrated all of his property to the 

Church, through a legal transfer, by a properly executed deed. But he retained complete control of his property under a stewardship, managed it himself personally, and lived with his family just as we do now. 

However, in some of the small rural communities referred to above, the members joined the United Order completely, surrendering all of their possessions to the corporation, and were thereafter absorbed by the united family-like organizations.

THE LAW OF CONSECRATION 

129 Appraisal of the Two Systems. We are told that the first attempts at establishing a new social order failed because there were too many new arrivals who were very poor and not enough well-to-do members; too much selfishness among the participants; and too short a period of operation to be given a fair chance. The attempt to establish the United Order in Utah also failed because the Saints were unable to produce enough to care for the great influx of poor, just as in Jackson County. Besides, the more well-to-do of the Church were reluctant to enter into an association that limited their operations, curbed their initiative and regulated consumption. Even President Young did not enter into it as completely as the Orderville group did, giving as his reason his inability to find men as capable as himself in managing his property and employing his capital profitably. At the last conference he attended before his death he said also that, "I do not wish for one moment to recognize the idea that in order to establish the United Order our property has to be divided equally among the people, to let them do what they please with it. But the idea is to get the people into the same state of unity in all things temporal that we find ourselves in with regard to things spiritual. Then let those who possess ability and wisdom direct the labors of those not so endowed, until they, too, develop the talents within them and in time acquire the same degree of ability.”6

The United Order was officially brought to an end in 1882 by President Taylor, when he said in part: "Our relations with the world and our own imperfections prevent establishment of this system at the present time, and therefore, as Joseph stated in an early day, it cannot yet be carried out.”7 "History of the Church, Vol. 1, p. 381. 

L. D. S. Pamphlet III, pp. 621-28. 

Journal of Discourses, XVIII, p. 354. 

Fox, F. Y., Social Security Program of the Mormon Church under Joseph Smith and Brigham Young.

In substance, as one Sevier County brother viewed the results of the undertaking, "It takes a pure people to live pure principles." 

However, looking at the experiments in the light of historical developments and present day criteria, some important principles may be observed. First, the 

Law of Consecration and Stewardship offers an ideal economic order if the people are thoroughly united in a common purpose and completely unselfish in temporal things. It preserves the freedom of individual initiative absent in the family type of United Order, and allows each individual to develop in the direction of his talents and inclination. It centers responsibility and trust in the management of the stewardship, but allows no great inequalities of income nor gross differences in living standards.

The principal difference between such a system and the present trend toward heavy taxation of large incomes to finance free schools, social security, and some day, perhaps, free (tax supported) medical care, is in the voluntary versus the compulsory nature of the contributions-compulsory to the Government under the present system, or voluntary to the Bishop in the form of a surplus under the Law of Consecration plan. 

Second, the United Order. if workable at all, in modern times is probably more suited to an isolated community, under emergency conditions of economic stress, such as prevailed in the severe nation-wide economic depression in the 70's, or in the paralyzed conditions of business and trade during the recent serious disturbances, when the self-help cooperative enterprises gained such popularity throughout the country. In other 

Fox, F. Y., Social Security Program of the Mormon Church under Joseph Smith and Brigham Young.

THE LAW OF CONSECRATION 

131 words, the United Order is more of a temporary substitute plan of economic security for an emergency condition, in isolated communities, where the principle of self-sufficiency is all important. Whereas, the Law of 

Consecration and the Order of Stewardship is a higher plan of life, designed for unselfish religious groups who are ready to consecrate their all on the altar of sacrifice and service for the brotherhood of man in the Kingdom of God on earth. However, until such perfection is attained by the people, the members of the L. D. S. 

Church will probably continue to live under the law of tithing and free-will offerings as now constituted. 

1. Would the contributions to the Church be greater under the Law of Consecration than they are under the Law of Tithing? 

2. How would the amount of the surplus be determined under the Stewardship plan? How would the needs of the family be fixed? 

3. Did Joseph Smith advocate an equal division of property or equal participation in products of wealth? What did Brigham 

Young advocate? 

4. What effect did the influx of the poor have on the success of the new social orders of Joseph Smith and Brigham Young? Is it an inherent weakness of all idealistic schemes to equalize wealth? 

5. How is the Church Welfare Program of today similar to or different from the earlier plans of Consecration and United Order?

References: Doctrine and Covenants. History of the Church. 

Social Security Program Under Joseph Smith and Brigham Young, by F. Y. Fox.

Chapter 17 

INVENTORYING OUR NATURAL RESOURCES 

Utah and the surrounding region, where the Mormon people largely dwell, are richly endowed by the Creator with a wealth of natural resources. Man has at his disposal three sources on which he may draw: (1) The natural resources, with which he is endowed by the Creator; (2) man-made resources, consisting largely of education and institutions; and (3) human energy, which he has the responsibility of organizing and husbanding in such a way that he can do the most and best work. With these three things, and with the help of God, he must earn his living, train his mind, and work out his salvation so far as it can be done on this earth. 

Natural and Man-Made Resources. "Natural re sources" is the name given to those things in nature which the Creator has supplied to man and which man uses to help provide the necessities of life, and after that to enrich his living. Such resources are numerous, but chief among them are the earth as a home, the air, sunshine, land and water, forests and ranges, wildlife, and places of great natural beauty, including the mountains and canyons, coal, oil, minerals, building materials, salt, and phosphate deposits. These things are provided ready-made by nature, and it is man's function to make the best and wisest use of them.

Different from these endowments of nature are the long list of useful tools, machines, and institutions for aiding man as an individual or as part of society. Among the institutions that man has made for himself are schools for helping to provide education, government for pro-

INVENTORYING OUR NATURAL RESOURCES 

133 tecting him against aggression from the outside or from other men, mercantile establishments for buying and selling goods, banks for handling financial transactions, machines and factories for manufacturing various articles, handicraft skill for doing many useful things, and houses and furniture to make living more comfortable. 

Even our manners of living in family and community groups are man-made institutions to a large degree. 

Natural Resources Classified. Some of the most valuable and absolutely essential resources are supplied to man so generously that he tends to overlook them as being of value. Man often forgets that the Creator has given him the earth on which to live, on which to walk, and on which he is generally safe so long as he does not travel too fast, tamper with explosives, or oppose nature in other ways. Likewise, because of its abundance, man forgets how dependent he is on the air around him or that he would live only a few minutes without it. Man has so little control over the supply of sunshine and sunlight that he seldom recalls that, with the help of the green coloring matter of plants (chlorophayll), sunlight manufactures all his food and most of the material which provides clothing, fuel, shelter, and much of the raw products used in manufacturing such useful articles as books, fountain pens, pencils, and rugs. 

Agriculture is based on sunlight, almost wholly. This also applies equally to gardens, lawns, flowers, and houseplants.

Besides these essential things supplied by nature in almost unlimited quantities, other valuable resources occur in quantities that are more or less limited. Since they are limited in quantity, or at least in available quantities, man is conscious of their value, and usually remembers that they are valuable, though often he appreciates them

only in part. It is to this class of natural products that the term "natural resources" is usually applied. To this class belong land, water, forests, grasses, herbs, shrubs, coal, oil, mineral, salt, phosphates, building materials, wildlife and spots of great natural beauty. They have money value because they are too scarce to be available to everybody without effort, or without preventing others from having all of each that man may desire.

Geographic Pattern of the Intermountain Region as an Example. Mountains and the valley plains at the base of mountains constitute the most important phase of the geography of the Intermountain region. Moderately smooth, deep soils, suitable for crop growing, lie in the valley plains and obtain their moisture supply largely from the mountain streams, or from storms precipitated by mountains, which block the path of stormbearing winds. From the mountains come also timber for lumber, fuel, logs for farm structures, fencing sup plies, and numerous other farm-used materials, such as timber for bridges, hay derricks, and farm tools. 

In a dry region where the broad flat plains have less than ten inches of precipitation annually, the mountains, by causing winds to ascend their sides, bring more storms to the adjacent valleys, providing 12 to 20 inches of precipitation in the valleys, and still more on the mountain face, increasing the precipitation at high altitudes up to 20 to 40 inches. The mountain streams all arise in these areas of heavier snow and rainfall. In addition, mountains and distances north of the equator have a definite bearing on winter and summer climates. 

Deep soils in the valleys were originaly covered naturally with sagebrush and bunchgrass, and shallow soils in the mountains with grasses, herbs, shrubs, and

INVENTORYING OUR NATURAL RESOURCES 135 

forests. Far away from the mountains, out beyond the farming areas, the desert valley, with less than 10 inches of precipitation and light snowfall in winter, are clothed with the short desert shrubs and grasses. These areas supply range forage for live stock at different seasons of the year. 

Wildlife, including birds, game, and fur-bearing animals are more abundant in the moister parts of the foothills and in the mountains. Waterfowl congregate on the marshes and lakes that are fed by water from the mountains. Most areas of great scenic beauty are in the mountains, and most hunting and fishing places are either along the streams or in the mountains. Picnic grounds, where people may go in the evening of hot days, are in the canyons, along streams, or on the edges of lakes.

Wealth in Land and Water. The West's most permanent resources are its land and water. The soils are as a whole much richer in plant nutrients than are the soils of wetter regions, where the heavy rainfall leaches the soluble soil material and carries it away in streams, eventually to reach the ocean. Arid regions nearly always have some lands where the soil salts are concentrated in too great quantities to permit ordinary agriculture. Arid soils are also porous to a great depth and permit deep rooting of plants. On the other hand, they are likely to be scant in their supply of partly decayed plant residues. Good deep soils in the Intermountain region are very productive. Our pioneer forefathers made no mistake, therefore, when they chose Salt Lake Valley as an area with fertile soils; these, with streams of mountain water, made a productive region. Valuable as is natural rainfall, continuous mountain water is still more reliable, and when not spread over too great areas

affords the high security needed in the period of early settlement. Today, the same security exists, where irrigation of more land than the water can properly serve has not been attempted. 

Resources in Forests and Ranges. Most of the land not capable of being irrigated or dry-farmed to advantage produces a natural plant-cover, valuable for grazing by livestock. Cattle and sheep can feed in the high mountains in summer, in the foothills and higher valley edges in the fall, and again in spring, and out in the desert valleys during the winter. About 85 percent of the Intermountain country is suitable for grazing, and has, as a whole, a varied climate for the different seasons for grazing. Much of the range land has not been properly managed, and is not now as productive as it might have been; but the resource is still present and will be restored when proper management is practiced. 

The forests are not equal to those of the great lumbering regions, but they are highly useful for rough lumber, slabs, poles, posts, derrick and bridge timber, fuel, and for railroad ties and mining timbers. These were indispensable supplies in the days of early settlement, and remain so today in the more isolated communities. In addition, a considerable amount of income is derived from work on roads, bridges, and dams in the forests.

Values in Wildlife and Recreational Places. Much use for food was made of wildlife-deer and elk, waterfowl, grouse, sagehens, rabbits, and fish during early settlement history. These aided materially in periods of food scarcity and added variety to the diet at all times. 

The population of waterfowl and land birds is not much reduced. The recent increase in deer and elk in certain localities has made their numbers excessive, so far as

INVENTORYING OUR NATURAL RESOURCES 137 

winter ranges are concerned. They may, however, still be a great food resource when properly handled. They are also of vast recreational value, in that they make naturally attractive canyons, forests and parks still more attractive.

The fact that four-fifths of all the towns in the Intermountain region are within an hour by automobile travel of cool, picnic spots by a mountain stream, is another valuable resource. Fishing for those who partake of this sport, hunting in the proper season, and occasional visits to the great natural beauty spots in the mountains, add greatly to the health and social resources. Moreover, just next door are the world-famous Yellowstone and Teton Parks, Grand Canyon of the Colorado, Zion 

Canyon, Cedar Breaks, and Bryce Canyon. 

Coal and Mineral Resources. Not far away, and also in the foothills or mountains, are the mines from which are extracted greater money value than comes from all the agriculture of the region. Copper, silver, lead, zinc, iron, gold are all relatively abundant. Great beds of coal occur in Carbon, Emery, Iron, Kane, and 

Garfield counties, Utah, and near Green River, Wyoming. Petroleum and natural gas, though not known to exist in large quantities in the Intermountain region, are abundant in nearby Wyoming and California. 

Stone, gravel and sand, useful as building materials, abound in the hills and valley edges. The salt deposits around the margins of Great Salt Lake are among the most abundant in the world. The phosphate beds in 

Utah, Idaho, and Wyoming probably surpass any other in the whole world. Lime, clay for brick and tile, materials for making cement, gypsum and sulphur, and water power that can be used to manufacture electricity, are at hand as raw materials with which to develop small or large industries.

Chemurgy and Manufacturing. Now that Utah and adjacent areas have a comparatively dense population in proportion to the developed resources, and has therefore surplus labor, attention should be given to applying this labor to turning raw products into more useful materials or more beneficial forms. Before a careful study is made, it is not certain just what might be done, but handicrafts and home industries, or at least industries in small establishments, may afford opportunities not anticipated.

Larger scale processing in specialized factory plants, and the using of complicated machinery and operations, is more properly designated as manufacturing. The 

Intermountain region has beet-sugar and canning industries that add greatly to the income from agriculture and trade, as well as help in crop rotations, in utilizing crop surpluses, and furnishing by-product foods. Established industries in the manufacture of woolen goods and food products employ many workmen. Concentrating low-grade ores and smelting the concentrated products also employ many men, but this employment is sometimes slack, especially during periods of financial depression, when other industries are also least active. 

Eventually iron and steel smelting may grow into a considerable industry in the Intermountain region. 

Treating phosphates with sulphuric acid or by means of the electric current to make fertilizer, will also probably develop in the next few years. Both, however, may have to wait until a more urgent need forces a somewhat stronger demand for commodities produced by these industries. Another prospective industry, but one still more remote in time at which it will likely develop, as compared with previously named industries, is the extraction of oil from oil shale, now a much more costly

INVENTORYING OUR NATURAL RESOURCES 139 

way of getting oil than importing it from regions having wells of free oil.

Variety of Occupations. Many are the occupations of man. He who keeps himself well-read and openminded will not be left stranded should employment in his chosen trade be decreased. Too many, however, are helpless and know not where to turn if their employment ceases. Man must have elasticity to adjust to new conditions. If he does this, all is usually well, for only in periods of severe general depression are all forms of employment slack at the same time. In this vast variety of employment resides one of man's most permanent forms of security. 

Health and Human Energy as Resources. He who is afflicted with disease cannot work, at least not effectively. To escape disease, and to maintain general physical vigor, are important steps in economic well-being. 

To avoid idleness, whether caused by lack of energy, by vice, by lack of skill, or by inability to adjust to new employment, is man's great step to security. Clean living is a long stride in the right direction, and one that every person can take if he will. Good health, physical vigor, and the shunning of idleness, follow clean living, and together with it, constitute man's surest protection against penury and want.

Planning the Wise Use of Resources. Each fam ily, each individual for that matter, can do much by planning a wise use of the resources available for more secure and better living. Every Church unit has welfare committees that should be called on to aid in this respect. 

Such help is not degrading, it is the most effective education.

The Creator has been Kind to Us. Man in the Intermountain region is richly endowed. The Creator has

provided a wealth of resources in good land, dependable high-quality water, in forests and ranges, in minerals, wildlife and recreational places. In few regions is there such an abundance and variety of resources. With rare exceptions, health and energy abound if man will only live a clean, active life and plan an intelligent use of his resources. The Creator has indeed been kind to us, as we shall see in the following lessons.

1. Keeping in mind the definition of "natural resources" given in this chapter, name those natural resources that may be found within the boundaries of the stake in which you live. 

2. Enumerate the man-made resources within your ward and within your stake boundaries. 

3. Define these terms: (a) resources, (b) natural resources, (c) man-made resources, (d) unlimited resources, and (e) limited resources. 

4. Explain why the geographic pattern of mountains, valleys, and deserts in the Intermountain region is favorable to man. 

5. Name the major resources of the Intermountain region and show how each contributes to the welfare of the dependent communities. 

6. Why must conservation of resources be practiced by the 

Mormon people? 

7. What is the relation of natural resources to the Church 

Welfare Plan? 

8. What is required in addition to material resources to make a great nation? A righteous people?

Chapter 18 

"For the strength of the hills we bless Thee," was not a vain boast of the pioneer settlers. Not only did the mountains provide water, reservoirs, timber, forage, and wildlife, but also of the staple minerals, gold, silver, lead, zinc, copper, and iron. Coal was early found near 

Coalville, Utah, and later in better quality and bigger deposits in Emery and Carbon counties. Mining was at first restricted by Brigham Young, but General Connor. his soldiers, and other non-Mormons, developed mining at first in Bingham and then at other camps.

The region also abounds in common salt, phosphates, potash, high-quality sand and gravel, building stone, cement materials, lime and clay suitable for brick and tile. Still valuable but more localized are deposits of gilsonite and other bitumens, sulphur, gypsum, radioactive ores, and some of the rare elements. Oil in a free state has been found only in small quantities, but is abundant in oil shale. 

Wealth in the Metals. From 1860 to 1929 Utah's mining industry was highly productive, producing in gross value more than 1,700 million dollars and paying dividends amounting to more than 350 million dollars. 

Utah in 1929 was the leading state of the United States in silver, second in copper and lead, and fifth in gold and zinc. These precious and useful metals have contributed mightily to the welfare of the State, employing approximately one-third of the labor and yielding onethird of the wealth. Idaho, Nevada, and Wyoming have also been heavy contributors to mineral wealth, but as

a whole the wealth from the mining industry in these states has been more largely in the non-Mormon settlements.

Mining, with its concentrating mills and smelters, has been supported rather largely by outside capital. Because of this, the profits have also flowed out of the region. The accompanying local business, the taxes, and the labor, however, have been great local assets. Considerable parts of local railroad lines have been built either directly or indirectly as a result of the mining industry.

The fact that most of the ores are comparatively low-grade has led to the development of nearby concentrating processes. Since the beginning of the mining industry in Utah, Bingham has produced about half the mineral and yielded almost two-thirds of the dividends. 

Park City and Tintic district have been next in importance. Other productive mining was done in Beaver and 

Tooele counties, and in the Wasatch range south of Salt

Lake City, with smaller producing areas scattered over the State. Physical proximity and facilities for finance, business and transportation have made Salt Lake City the center of the mining industry of Utah and immediately adjacent territory. Eastern Nevada and southern Idaho have important metal industries, though, as noted, these have added only indirectly to the resources of the Mormon people.

The Infant Iron Industry. Utah contains vast de posits of iron, but owing to the immensely rich and much more available deposits in Minnesota and Michigan, there has as yet been little need for drawing on Utah iron. The superb transport facilities of the Great Lakes deliver the surface-minded iron to the eastern Ohio and Pittsburg districts, near great beds of coking coal, at

"FOR THE STRENGTH OF THE HILLS” 

143 costs far lower than would permit the handling of the Utah deposits of iron, which are largely in Iron and 

Washington counties.

The Utah deposits of more than a billion tons of hematite ore (57% iron) constitute a vast future reserve. It has been commonly accepted that use of this would come only in the far-distant future, but already the United States Steel Corporation is operating a large modern steel mill near Provo. This point is at the junction of railroads bringing iron from Iron county and good coking coal from Carbon county. Large deposits of coal in Iron, Kane, and Garfield counties are unsuitable for steel manufacture because of their high sulphur content. In 1928, Utah was the fifteenth state of the 

Union in iron production, but the fact that it produced less than one-half of one percent of the iron in the United States shows the small local development of this industry. Long distances to eastern consumption areas and the high costs of railroad hauling, in comparison with water transportation, will postpone for some time yet a material increase in the production of Utah iron. 

Notwithstanding this, the deposits of rich iron ore stand as a great reserve resource in future efforts toward security. 

Coal, a Vast Resource. Utah and southwestern

Wyoming have immense deposits of coal, hundreds of billions of tons, far in excess of the needs of the Intermountain region. It is shipped to California, Oregon, Washington, Montana, and Nebraska. One-sixth of

Utah is underlain with coal of workable thickness. The first development was near Coalville in Summit county, and was worked from beds about four feet or less in thickness. Others in Wales, Sanpete county, Utah, and in Cedar City, Iron county, were worked in early 10

days. Now beds less than four feet thick are not considered commercial and most of the mining is done from beds eight to seventeen feet in thickness. In many cases, this causes heavy waste by leaving the thinner beds unmined, and when a given unit is mined out and abandoned, all the remnants are then unprofitable even at much higher prices. 

Coals from the tertiary geological formations as a whole are low in quality, and also banded with dirt and shale. Utah's coal beds in cretaceous formations are of high quality, especially those in Carbon and Emery counties, which are hard, dry, clean, and deep-bedded. 

They are also low in ash and sulfur, of high heat value, and do not slack readily. These Carbon-Emery beds underlie also much of Uintah and Duchesne counties.

The coals of Iron, Kane, and Garfield counties are of cretaceous age, but of lower grade than the Carbon county coal. They are soft, high in moisture, dirty and bear much sulphur. 

Good coking coal is mined near Sunnyside, in Carbon county, Utah. Hundreds of coking ovens are in operation there, and others at Ironton near Provo. Volatile tars taken as by-products from the coking ovens are very valuable, and are thought by some to be more valuable than the coke. It has also been urged that removal of these by-products from coal before it is used for heating would largely overcome the smoke nuisance in Salt Lake City and the larger towns of the region. 

Certainly, something should be done to conserve these valuable by-products and also to reduce the serious smoke nuisance.

Oil and Oil Shale for the Future. So far only small deposits of petroleum have been found in the region, along with promises in one or two other areas that have

not yet materialized. Neighboring Wyoming and California are heavy producers, and Salt Lake City has developed a considerable refinery business on outside Dil, including some from New Mexico. The Salt Lake plant is thoroughly modern and does a sizable business, employing hundreds of men.

Oil shale in the Green River geological formations is extremely abundant in the Uintah Basin, but also occurs west of Manti in Sanpete and Juab counties. Each ton of shale is said to contain around forty gallons of oil. The deposits are extensive, but all Green 

River shales do not bear oil or at least are not rich in it. The oil-bearing strata are from a few inches to one hundred feet in thickness. The U. S. Geological Survey says that a conservative estimate places the volume of oil at seventy-five to eighty billion barrels, which is double the amount of petroleum that has been taken from the earth in the United States since the industry began in 1859. When oil is extracted from oil shale, ammonium sulphate, a valuable fertilizer, is obtained as a by-product. It has been estimated that the Utah oil shales would yield, if carefully worked, the tremendous volume of 750 million tons of this fertilizer. 

At present, however, these vast reserves of oil and fertilizer remain unused, and unless new processes are developed, probably will remain so for generations. 

Costs of extraction, at present by distillation, are far above that of ordinary petroleum, and cannot be justified, under present circumstances. Should conditions of supply and price become favorable, owing either to scarcity of petroleum or to the introduction of cheap processes of extraction, Utah can begin to draw on this tremendous reserve resource. Colorado and Wyoming also have vast beds of oil shale.

What the future may bring in the use of coal-tar products, as substitutes for petroleum, is not known, but this is another possible resource that lies in the vast coal deposits of Utah.

Radio-Active Minerals and Helium. The radioactive minerals were so limited in their availability that their use was almost nil until radium and uranium deposits of considerable size were found in Belgian Congo, Africa. Until that time, the mining of ores containing these two minerals in Utah seemed to have great promise, as small but valuable deposits have been found in 

Grand, San Juan, Emery and Wayne counties. The richest deposit of these minerals occur in petrified bones and trees, found in coarse sandstones.

Another radio-active mineral, vanadium, is being milled in a good-sized plant near Monticello. This seems likely to become a regularly operated industry. 

Helium, a rare gas, along with carbon monoxide, was found near Woodside in Emery county. Nearby were also found large supplies of carbon dioxide, particularly valuable for industrial use because it is free from sulphur. These supplies occurred at a depth of about 3,000 feet in sandstone. 

Salt for all Time. The Intermountain region has immense supplies of common salt, chiefly in Great Salt Lake and in its shore deposits. Lake water, or salt bodies treated with water, is concentrated in vats of many acres in extent, largely by solar evaporation. 

Nearly all the solids in solution is common salt, and by drawing off the water just bfeore it is dry, a yield of nearly pure commercial salt is obtained. This is used. for stock and industrial purposes in a coarse form, and with some factory treatment as fine white salt for human consumption. Operators of livestock in commu-

nities near the Great Salt Lake formerly hauled salt from shore beds.

Rock-salt has long been mined in block pieces in Sevier and Sanpete counties, though during late years ordinary salt, with or without additions of sulphur and phosphates, have been made into blocks of stock salt. 

Other Non-Metallic Wealth. Phosphates, one of the principle fertilizing substances, occurs in vast rock deposits in northeastern Utah, southwestern Wyoming and southeastern Idaho. This is by all odds the greatest accumulation of phosphates in the United States and possibly in the world. Up to the present the phosphates, used largely for fertilizing truck crops and cotton crops in the Atlantic coastal plain and in the old cotton districts of the South. have been mined in 

Florida and Tennessee. These deposits are largely exhausted and sooner or later new sources must be found. If no nearer ones are available, the deposits in the Intermountain country may be developed rapidly. 

An abundance of sulphuric acid, required for treating rock-phosphates and obtained from metal smelters, is available at low prices. Long railroad hauls to the points of use are partly offset by producing a highly concentrated product as is now being done in a limited way near Montpelier, Idaho.

Potash resources also occur in Utah in alunite rock in the Tushar range near Marysvale, in the brines of Great Salt Lake, and in the mineral carnallite found near Thompson, in Grand county, Utah. Potash is very valuable as a fertilizer and in industries. At present, however, the Stassfurt deposits of Central Germany and other deposits in Alsace and adjacent regions are much cheaper and of higher quality. A mill operated during the World War near Marysvale, Utah, closed

down when peace again made the German and Alsatian potash available. Large resources, however, for ultimate use are available when called forth by demand. 

At a point about twenty miles north of Beaver,

Utah, sulfur has been mined intermittently during about fifty years, at first by the Mormon Church and afterwards by private owners. The deposit is large and rich enough for a considerable industry. Another important deposit, as yet entirely unused, has been described by the U. S. Geological Survey along the San 

Rafael river in Emery county. 

Gypsum, valuable for plaster, casts, and fresco work occurs in several localities in central Utah. One mill in Nephi, Utah, has opeated for many years, as also has the Jumbo Plaster Mill at Sigurd, Utah. These deposits are both large and of high quality. 

Limestone suitable for making good lime is abundant, and a growing industry is developing. Clay, valuable for brick and tile, is also abundant and supports a considerable industry and would support a much greater one. Quartzite suitable for glass, as well as the presence of other materials required in glass manufacture, makes possible the manufacture of glass if extensive markets were developed.

Gilsonite and related bitumens are abundant in the Uintah Basin, especially near Watson, where an extensive high-quality bed is known. These solid relatives of petroleum are valuable for use in varnishes, Japans, acid-proof paints, and waterproofing compounds, as well as for making mineral rubber and insulating material.

Labor Demand Irregular. Mining in its various aspects has been and will be a source of employment to thousands of men. The fact that mines usually run

slack and frequently suspend operations altogether in periods of low metal prices, makes employment less secure than would otherwise be the case. Some of the metal mines as well as coal mines are irregular in periods of operation, usually closing when depressions are worst. Some of the big operators have, however, extended themselves to furnish at least part time employment for the men who regularly depend on them for labor.

Anything that could help the industry to become more regular in the demand for man labor would be a real improvement. Development of new phases of mining or the growth of small ones seem a possibility, and in that they provide labor would be valuable especially if they can be made more regular in the use of men. 

Building Materials. Practically inexhaustible deposits of high-quality gravel and sand occur in the hundreds and hundreds of miles of ancient beach deposits of Lake Bonneville. The lake water assorted the gravels and sands derived from hard rocks, according to size, and provided probably the most enormous and uniformly high-quality accumulation in the civilized world, certainly in the United States. 

Added to the fact that clay and marl suitable for cement manufacture are also abundant, these sand and gravel deposits make possible cement construction, that is both cheap and most excellent in quality. Raw materials for a manifold greater industry occur naturally assorted. These deposits also supply much of the surrounding region with gravel, both for highways and railroad beds.

High-quality granite quarries are found in the Cottonwood district, which furnished stone for Salt Lake Temple. Excellent building stone of quartzite, lime-

stone, and marble occur at various places, and have from the first been used for larger buildings, both public and private, and for many excellent residences. The 

L. D. S. Chapel in Washington, D. C., erected from

Utah materials, is a structure of outstanding beauty. 

Blest Above All Other People. The preceding dis cussion has made clear that Utah, as the Mormon center of the Intermountain region, has a wealth of metallic and non-metallic resources equalled in few regions. The metal industry has been developed largely by outside capital, and many possible non-metallic industries have been developed only in a minor way, or not at all. Almost unbelievably large resources exist suitable, under the right circumstances, for industrial development in iron and steel, in phosphate and potash fertilizer and in cement structures. These developments are largely for the future and await proper conditions to become active. Oil from oil shale is also a vast resource, whose utilization is probably still farther in the future than the others. In this extremely great variety and volume of metallic and non-metallic resources, the Creator has blessed the Mormon people in a magnificent manner, that promises even more in the future than for the present. 

Duty to God and Posterity. Man has a great responsibility to God to cherish the blessings brought by these resources and to provide careful, conservative use of them so as not to waste or dissipate them. Conservation means not to lock up resources against use, but to provide that they be used for the greatest good of all, and for all time, by an orderly effective consumption. The Mormon people are by doctrine committed against wasteful or destructive exploitation. They also believe it necessary to provide for their posterity and

for God's children who are yet to come. They also believe that God helps those most who try sincerely to help themselves. It behooves the Latter-day Saint people, therefore, to take hold of these immense and varied resources and develop them into productive enterprises or industries that will help to provide permanent temporal security in a constructive manner: 

When that is adequately done, they may truly sing: 

Thou hast led us here in safety, Where the mountain bulwark stands,

As the guardian of the loved ones, Thou hast brought from many lands:

For the rock and for the river, The valley's fertile sod;

For the strength of the hill we bless Thee, Our God, Our fathers' God.

13. What home-like community virtues may be found in the early colonized settlements of the Latter-day Saints founded expressly to take advantage of natural resources? 

14. Are these same community virtues found to be the same extent in industrial or metropolitan areas?

Chapter 19 

The Latter-day Saint people have from the first lived close to the land. At the time of the gold rush to 

California, Brigham Young wisely took, and successfully maintained, the position that the welfare of the new communities in Utah depended not on mining but on agriculture. Time has established mining in the region as a major industry, but the greater number of people still draw their living from crop-growing and livestock ranching or from industries and commercial enterprises related to agriculture. Vast numbers of those who work in non-agricultural industries, even where they live in cities, get pleasures as well as profit from fruit, vegetable, or flower gardens. Our wealth in land and water is both great and varied. 

Mormons Lead in Irrigation and Dry-Farm ing. Although before 1847 there were a few cases in 

New Mexico, Colorado and Oregon where water had been used to irrigate growing crops, to the Mormons belong the credit of organizing irrigation on a community scale, and making it really usable by putting in operation a cooperative plan of building dams and digging canals. Almost, if not fully, as important was the development of the idea of distributing the water "by turns" in rotation, so as to establish, even in periods of drought, security in the right to enough water for subsistence farms. Making irrigation a social institution is a far greater honor than being merely the first to turn water on the land. Egyptians, Sumerians, Babylonians, Sabaeans, Romans, Peruvians, and Spaniards, all practiced irrigation before the time of Columbus.

Dry-farming was known in Italy, Persia, and North Africa before the time of Christ. It may be much older than even this. The Mormons, true pioneers as they were, not only showed how to adapt irrigation to their new homes, but they showed near Bear River City and on the Ogden Sand Ridge how to supplement the scanty supply of irrigation water with dry-farming. It was fitted into the scheme of community agriculture and made to dovetail with irrigation and livestock growing.

What Water Means to the Intermountain Country. 

Water is the very life-blood of communities in the Intermountain country. On it depends the successful growing of crops, orchards, home gardens, flowers, and shade trees. It not only makes possible the production of food, forages, and market crops, but it also helps to make living comfortable and progressive. By supporting a comparatively dense population in compact settlements, it provides a means of better education and more effective Church organization. Its influence is, however, far wider than even this. The successful use of an area of range land, ten to twenty times as large as that cultivated, depends directly on irrigated tracts of land which furnish ranching headquarters and business and school centers. Mining is made much easier because of the reliable food supply from nearby irrigated communities. Thus, water is seen to be the basic support of all human life in the Intermountain region. 

The Soil Resource. The great wealth in the soils of the Intermountain region is often overlooked. Because there is more land than can be adequately supplied with water, the great scarcity and value of water is obvious. The water, however, would be of little value if there were not good soil on which to use it. Deep. porous soils with plant nutrients carried to a consider-

able depth and abundant lime, are great assets in comparison with the shallow, or compact, or the lime-deficient, or otherwise unproductive soils of many rainy regions. Arid soils, on the other hand, are likely to be deficient in organic matter, that is, in the partly decayed plant residues known as humus. 

Since only one-fifth to one-fourth of the soils in the

United States are good enough for successful cropgrowing, it is indeed fortunate that in a broken, irregular country, such as the Intermountain region, more good land is available than the water can be made to serve adequately. This very fact, however, imposes on man the obligation to make sure the water is used only on good land, for if it is used on poor land it is at least partly wasted. 

Irrigation vs. Humid Agriculture. Crop yields are much higher on good irrigated land than on land of the same general quality in a humid region. As already indicated, the good soils are generally naturally more productive and more likely to be rich in lime, a much needed soil constituent. Under irrigation, water can be supplied when the crops need it and in proper amounts, whereas the farmers of rainy regions can do little to influence the time or the manner in which precipitation comes, with respect to crop needs. Nor can they regulate the amount of moisture in a storm, or the speed with which it comes, although they can in part govern the absorptive properties of the soil and hence the depth to which the moisture penetrates. A farmer certainly cannot arrange for a rainstorm to moisten the potato field and the garden, and leave the newmown hay dry. During the rainy spells of a humid region, it is practically impossible to make good hay for weeks at a time, occasionally for the entire haying season. The

power of the irrigation farmer to apply water to his pasture and sugar-beets, and withhold it from alfalfa and grain ready to harvest, gives the irrigator a great advantage. Control of a major factor in production, such as water, makes for increased security of output. 

Crop Responses. This control over the use of irrigation water gives the Intermountain farmer a means of controlling the responses of his crops to the application of water. Within reasonable limits the soils on which potatoes, sugar-beets, and similar crops are grown, can be kept moist to a good depth. Small-grain crops may be so irrigated as to get more grain and avoid water-logging, by regulating the amount of water used, and the time at which it is applied. Much water increases the yield of corn stalks, or small-grain straw, in proportion to grain, whereas less water increases the proportion of grain. The time and amount of water applied makes fruit large and well-colored or small and faded. Some water in the fall, but not too much, makes trees hardy against winter cold. Pastures that are over-irrigated permit the coming in of rushes, sedges, barley grass and docks, instead of luscious bluegrass and clover, which thrive under moderate irrigation. 

Irrigation, Alkali, and Civilization. Unwise irrigation does much to offset the advantages gained from the proper use of water. If excess irrigation water remains on the land it causes water-logging and encourages the occupation of the land by water-loving plants. 

If it runs it causes erosion, thereby carrying away valuable top soil; if it percolates through the soil it removes valuable nutrients, and carries along soil salts, which cause seeped areas and salt spots on lower lands. These dangers of irrigation were known in Asia Minor, in

Afghanistan, in India, and in other places where valu-

able lands became impregnated with soil salts and were thus lost to use. In the countries mentioned are remnants of considerable farming towns now extinct as a result of seepage. Irrigation civilization demands great care in the control of water to avoid destruction of the land by alkali, or erosion, or both. 

Economic Use of Water. It has already been noted that there is not enough water to irrigate all the good land, at least in many parts of the Intermountain country. Unwise irrigation, especially over-irrigation, has been shown to cause the growth of the nearly useless water-loving plants, or the accumulation of alkali, which has in some countries destroyed the natural wealth in land and water. In order to obtain the benefits of proper irrigation and avoid the dangers of using water unwisely, some means of bringing about a truly beneficial use of water is urgently needed. 

How to limit the use of water to good land, how to avoid over-irrigation and the consequent accumulation of soil salt, are problems that must be faced by individuals and by community groups. Life on many of the lands of Utah, Idaho and adjacent states cannot long be secure and peaceful, unless some way is found to avoid the pitfalls that accompany unskilled irrigation. Possibly some good general social legislation might contribute appreciably in this respect. Such laws must constitute a basis for wise, well-thought-out procedure, and must above all depend on accurate data. 

Livestock and Farm-Crop Rotation. Many farm products, such as hay, pasturage and silage, by-products such as straw, stubble, beet tops, and small potatoes and fruits are profitably used only as livestock feed. Too many farmers fail to make sufficient use of these roughages for cattle, hogs, or poultry. Many incomes could

be appreciably increased by the planned use of such roughages and by-product feeds. Sometimes small land holdings are made much more productive by this means. 

Livestock on the farm also serve to produce manures much needed by the arid soils, naturally low in their content of organic matter. More regular feeding of farm-grown hay and grain and more attention to milch cows or beef animals will probably in the long run make both for more reliable incomes and for better producing soils.

Crop rotations, along with livestock growing, are another means of increasing yields, preventing erosion, reducing alkali injury, and of increasing crop production. Good practices in crop rotations have been followed by only a small percentage of farmers, but will be more and more required on all land as our agriculture becomes more fully developed. 

Even city dwellers will find it to their advantage to practice rotation in their vegetable and flower gardens to avoid the accumulation of weeds, insects and diseases that attack particular plants. Often the same plants cannot successfully be grown year after year on the same soil, whereas a shift every two or three years might greatly benefit the plants. Farm manure will in most cases benefit flowers and shrubs, and is nearly always required for vegetables. 

God's Gift to Us. As we have seen, God has given us a magnificent heritage in our arid soils,-deep, porous, rich in lime and other plant nutrients. Not only is lime a plant food, but it is required by nearly all crop plants, in quantities sufficient to neutralize any acid materials. An abundance of lime favors also the beneficial soil bacteria and helps to keep the soil granulated and in a condition to permit ready penetration of air

and water, and consequently favors the deep rooting plants. Abundant lime also makes for strong-boned healthy men and animals. 

Just at hand are the mountains from which come clear streams, which under favorable circumstances of well-vegetated watersheds maintain a moderately even flow in the hot months of late summer. Deep snows at the higher elevation to supply water, and warm, bright sunshiny days in the valley to make the plants grow, are a combination that makes the Intermountain region "a land blest above all others."

Not only is irrigation farming productive, and relatively reliable even in drought years, but the quality of the crops is superior to those of rainy regions. Usually they are more nutritious, because they are richer in protein, in minerals and in vitamins. It will be well to appreciate more deeply what God has done for us. 

Doing our Part. The most sincere appreciation that can be rendered to God for such gifts is to do our part in preserving them and in using them in the most effective manner. The soils can be husbanded by crop rotation and by the use of farm wastes. Losses of the soil by erosion and its injury by over-irrigation should be avoided as completely as possible. Irrigation requires study, both by individuals and community groups. 

Another way in which man may show his appreciation is by using the by-product feeds, such as stubble, beet tops, small potatoes, and surplus or fallen fruit, for hogs, some hens, or an extra cow. In such thrift lies real security for the farmer or the suburban dweller. 

Even in the city, vegetable gardens are often possible and flowers are always to be enjoyed. They make many homes more attractive. They are also needed to relieve the monotony of drab conditions everywhere. 11

Chapter 20 

 AS A MODE OF LIVING 

Farming and ranching as modes of living have many advantages, though they are devoid of much of the glamor that accompanies some city life. Rush and turmoil, bright lights and excitement are not by any means the only surroundings suited to harbor an interesting life. Moreover, it is highly probable that the richest and best life for the common-run of mankind is not one of restless hub-bub but a quiet one, in which family groups are enough by themselves to permit thought and reverence. The farm and ranch present better opportunities for this than do most parts of large cities. The well-to-do and thoughtful people in cities go to great expense to provide in part the privacy that the country-side furnishes without cost. 

Security in Food and Shelter. Farmers and ranchers, like other people, suffer in depression periods. 

Their lot, however, is much less sordid than that of city laborers, or even than that of skilled workmen who are thrown out of employment by the closing of factories or by slack business. 

Poor markets for crops and livestock during depression periods may provide little cash income, but bread and fruit, milk, meat and eggs are usually abundant on the well-planned farm. These home products, together with vegetables from the garden, provide food that may be plain but varied, nourishing, healthful and adequate. Most of us have seen city families who have neither these essential foods nor money with which to

buy them at the corner grocery. Charity and doles may be greatly lessened by supplies of a good variety of farm and ranch foods. Usually, too, shelter and fuel can be planned without a large outlay of cash. A home is also found on nearly every farm. Wood may, for a time, decrease the coal requirement, and in some localities adjacent to a national forest replace it altogether. Gingham dresses and overalls are cheap and can be neat and clean. More stylish dresses and suits last a long while when worn only on Sundays and on special occasions. Though not desirable as regular practice such modes of dresses lessen the pinch of depression. 

Agriculture Can be Healthful. Sunshine and outdoor living can in the country be turned into great assets. Premises can be clean, beautiful and health-giving, though sometimes this is not the case. Corrals, yards, and farm buildings, if properly cared for, may be sanitary. The danger of contamination that lies in water, milk, and filth in livestock pens may be averted. 

A country slum is really nasty, and is an unnecessary evil. Freedom from smoke, from bus loads of sneezing and coughing crowds, and from adjacent neighbors who ignore all quarantine regulations, is easier to provide in the country than in the crowded town. 

Spring, summer and autumn in the country are often as good as hospital rests. Wet, cold winters, without pavement and with livestock to tend is a hard season. Proper clothing and persistent cleanliness will largely overcome this handicap. Agriculture, with intelligent care, is a healthful occupation for men, women, and children. Sanitary living conditions require real effort, and pay good dividends for the intelligence and care invested in them, whether in the factory or on

the farm. But there is no substitute; there is nothing just as good for health as sunshine, exercise, and good food. Nor do farmers and ranchers have to fight the fumes and stenches, and the harmful dusts of some factories, smelters, or metal works. Pure air and sunlight are great blessings to every country side. 

Conquest of the Land. The Bible says "that God blessed man and woman" and said unto them, "Be fruitful and multiply, and replenish the earth and subdue it." This is no small order. Moreover, it is full of wisdom. It is good for man to have a problem, and subduing the earth in such a way as to provide for conservation is a real man-sized problem. Our pioneer forefathers dug most of the ditches and then built bridges over them. They plowed up the sod and hauled off the rocks, or pulled the sagebrush. But every generation of men on the land has the problem of subduing it,—taming or harnessing it, as the case may be. The pioneers tamed the land; present-day farmers must harness it.

Today the problems are to prevent soil erosion, to avoid over-irrigation, to hold alkali in check, and to maintain soil fertility by rotating crops, hauling manure and sometimes by using phosphates. Harnesses have many straps, hame straps, back straps, and breeching.

Then there are the collar and the tugs. So it is with the harness that the husbandman must put on the land.

He must not mix the belly-band with the bridle nor the reins with the tugs. A lifelong acquaintance and some scientific knowledge of land is required to make soil and plants work well together in the harness. 

Stewardship of the Land. The welfare of every country side, and of every town drawing trade from the outlying farms, depends directly on how fully farmers

and stockmen accept the land as a trusteeship. In the end the Nation is strongly affected by the sincerity with which the people who live on the land undertake the task of preventing erosion of the soil, preserving the plant cover and thereby maintaining the watersheds, of revegetating the ranges, and providing stability to the livestock industry.

Only 25 percent of the area of the United States is clothed with soil suitable for cultivated agriculture, and of this by far the greatest productive power resides in the surface layer-the top few inches of dark-colored soil. Should this top layer be lost by erosion or injured by gullying, alkali, or persistent perennial weeds, America's power to resist an enemy or to provide a good living for her own people is weakened just that much, and at the very foundations. None of the natural resources in the long-run counts so powerfully toward lasting strength and security as do well-preserved soils and vigorous plant cover, on the uncultivated as well as the cultivated land. When land lies bare for a long period, it is exposed to wastage that will eventually weaken it. Herein is one danger of the summer fallow. 

The highest form of patriotism is not a lot of noise on July 4th, but in bending every effort to protect the farms, ranges and watersheds from being abused and wasted. The day will come, though it may still be far-distant, when the preserver of the land and its plant life will be honored as a national benefactor. Several countries of western Europe are as yet far ahead of us in taking seriously the responsibility of being good stewards of the land. In better stewardship lies one of America's great opportunities.

America, like all new nations, has been built upon the underlying principle that the natural resources are

inexhaustible and may be "mined" without restriction for private benefit. Americans have moved across a continent and used, often wastefully, one of the greatest heritages God has given to mankind. Originally, timber was a handicap to farming, as it had to be removed, often by fire. When land became unproductive the farmer moved west to new land.

In the Intermountain country range and farm land at first seemed unlimited. When a range came to yield less forage, the stockman merely moved to another. 

Now that good farming land can no longer be secured as a homestead, that the ranges are all occupied, and that our water supply is known to be limited, the Mormon people must face squarely the fact that increased food and income is to be had only by a more intelligent and planned use of their resources.

Cooperative with Nature. In undertaking the job of conserving our natural agricultural resources and putting them to the highest use, man can work hand in hand with nature. Man cannot make the soil, but he can help preserve it and be trained how to use it most effectively. Man cannot of his own power make plants grow on overused ranges and thinly covered watersheds, but he can so arrange the conditions of use that these desirable things will be the natural consequence. 

One of the great charms of farming is to be a teammate with nature in making plants and animals grow, and in helping nature to restore wastage. 

The real stockman gets pleasure that is beyond the power of the politician to know, when he sees his animals in thrifty condition, growing rapidly and reaching a productive maturity. To stroke his favorite cow or slap the neck of his best horse is joy not at all comprehended by the sophisticated city office worker.

Working With Life. Good clay is a joy to the artist, as is story-telling to the author, and selling "goods" to the merchant. More thrilling even than music to the soul of the composer is the joy that comes to the farmer and stockman who love his plants and animals. To guide growth of good animals and vigorous plants is second only to the joy of rearing up sons and daughters to be true, God-fearing men and women. 

To know how the pollen particles from corn tassels lights on the silk of the ear and grows all the way down through it to the cob and there causes the kernel to develop, is culture that many a city dweller never feels. To know where to plant alfalfa and how to make it into bright green hay is infinitely more important and valuable than having seen a Knight of the Garter, or sat in the club cafe to watch the jazz girls "hot-foot" a dizzy dance. In fact the chances of true culture are all in favor of the kind that wide-awake country people get. 

Mystery of Life. With no one is the old saying, "that truth is stranger than fiction," so well applied as to the farmer and stockman. Rollicking stories and mystery plays are all poor substitutes for the Creator's great mystery, the nature and behavior of life itself. 

What makes some corn yellow and some white? What makes the Holstein spotted white and black, and the 

Hereford milk-white across the face? Why are some hens better layers than others? How comes it that all sugar-beets until a few years ago were ruined with "white fly," and now one strain may be bitten and still keep growing, smooth-leaved and big-rooted, as beets ought to grow? Why is it that some wheats smut and others are hardly affected? Why are some roses bronze instead of white and red? And why are your

daughter's eyes brown, like her mother's, instead of blue like father's? In the answers to these questions lie the mystery of life itself, and its pulse thrills him who feels the beat. 

Animal and Plant Improvement. A cow that produces 400 pounds of butter instead of 200 is a real gain to the dairyman and the Nation, as is also wheat that does not smut, or the sugar-beet that does not curl and dwarf when the leafhopper ("white" fly) bites it. The production of these valuable strains of living things involves a knowledge of breeding, called "genetics" by students of science, and known to all as the principles of heredity. To man God has given several master keys, but none excel in value and power and joy to him who wields it, the knowledge of how to mate his animals and plants to unite in the offspring the good qualities of two parents. No force of nature works so surely, so silently nor so untiringly for man, as this force of heredity when once he has harnessed it. To control in part the mystery of life, with God's help, is the crowning achievement of man, and one of the greatest joys granted him.

Solitude Favors Thought. The grower of plants and animals is required to think his way through life. 

Too many factory hands are only human machines, mere followers of routine. They have not the time to think, nor does the buzz and grind help them. Besides the employer of the factory "hand," the typist and even the book-keeper wishes them to be merely automatons. 

This earns profits for him. Moreover, office workers are too closely surrounded by others to think deeply. 

The open country, with its hours of solitude, its absence of the clash and bang of heavy machinery, and its freedom from the constant distraction of the crowd, is a

place where man can concentrate his thought on crops and animals, or morals and philosophy, and on the ways of man and of God. The country is superior in many ways to the big city, and not the least of these is the quiet serenity of a lovely mountain or stream, meadow or farmyard in comparison with noisy enticements of the concessions at a town carnival. 

In the aspen 

God in the Great Out-of-Doors. grove, astride a good horse, the stockman pauses and feels nature to be grand. His soul is uplifted and God is near. The farmer at sunset gazes across the golden billows of grain and scents the delicious odor of curing alfalfa. The gardener at sunrise sees his flowers pearled with morning dew. These are moments of silent greatness and infinite pleasure. Everywhere with healthy plants and animals, right next to life, man stands just through the veil from God. Often he feels 

Him move, and worships in reverence.

Is it any wonder that Jehovah took Moses into the desert for 40 years before He trusted him with leading the Children of Israel out of Egypt? Did not Christ pray, isolated in the Garden, and did He not accept

His temptation on the rocky crags? And why? Simply because God is near to that place where nature surrounds man and puts his mind in tune.
