Chapter 21  

A shortage of water for irrigation is the natural condition of the region where most of the Mormon people live. It is important, therefore, that forces which reduce or injure the water supply be not allowed to develop, or to remain if developed. The natural timber supply which grows on the watersheds, is likewise not abundant and, as with other scarce resources, must be properly husbanded lest an early scarcity handicaps, in one way or another, the well-rounded development of the communities that depend on one of its various uses.

The Inter- 

Watersheds and Their Importance. mountain region depends on mountain water, not only for its irrigation enterprises, but also for good drinking water for its people and for a moderately ample supply in city systems to water lawns, flowers, shrubs and trees, without which towns in this arid region would be bare, hot and dusty. As will be seen the quantity, the quality, and the distribution of water are all influenced by the condition of vegetation on the watersheds (from which the water comes).

Irrigation and culinary water in the Intermountain region both originate on mountain watersheds. Moisture from storms in the lower foothills and valleys is of little value in this respect, because it does not sink deeply enough into the soil to become part of the ground water. Though storms that merely wet the surface soil contribute to plant growth and to cooling the atmosphere, the moisture never accumulates into

a stream, being taken up by the plant or passed back into the air in the form of vapor. Unfortunately, a large part of the moisture in summer storms, on both the high mountains and the foothills, does not percolate into the soil but accumulates into streams that flow away as surface run-off.

How to control this surface run-off constitutes one of the most seriously-studied problems in the whole West. When watershed conditions are favorable, such as existed in the region at the time of settlement, the water from melting snow and from summer rains largely entered the soil, and accumulated into underground streams or in reservoirs, and later came out as springs. These fed the permanent streams in the canyons and afforded a supply that kept up the volume of water in July and August, when streams would almost disappear except for such storage in the soil and in underground channels and reservoirs.

Vegetation of Watersheds Preserve Water. The vegetation on the watershed catches the waters from rains and melted snow, and by retarding their surface flow, gives them time to sink into the soil. Each plant, especially grasses and other small herbs, forms a tiny dam that holds back the water. In a few seconds, the water disappears in the porous soil or down the tiny channels beside the plant stems and roots. Old leaves and stems lying on the surface, and called "litter" for want of a better name, also form small dams, and, by covering the surface of the soil itself with humus, keep open the tiny channels in the porous structure of the soil.

Even heavy storms on well-preserved watersheds do not cause destructive floods. The flow of water from excessive storms is distributed through several

hours and sometimes over a period of two or three days. 

Such were the floods along the Wasatch Mountain front in early days of settlement. Several floods are recorded but none were destructive in the sense that the later ones since 1923 have been destructive. 

Recent Floods. On August 13, 1923, a heavy thunder shower on the west face of the Wasatch mountains caused destructive floods at Willard, Farmington,

Centerville and other places in Utah. In less than an hour deluges of water and mud roared down the canyon ravines, ripping out gullies 50 to 100 feet deep in the old gravel beach deposits of Lake Bonneville, which in most cases had remained undisturbed since the lake built them approximately 20,000 to 25,000 years ago. 

The gravel, raw soil, and boulders, scoured out of the ravines by this avalanche, were spewed over the orchards, garden and farming lands, carrying away several homes and filling many others with water and mud. Several lives were lost when a camping party in Farmington Canyon was engulfed. About a thousand acres of the highest priced farming land in Davis and Boxelder counties were entirely destroyed by being covered deep with gravel and boulders, some of which weighed 110 to 50 tons or more. Two thousand other acres were flooded and later recovered with great labor and some injury to the land. In July and August, 1930, floods again poured off several of the same watersheds and from others in various parts of the region. 

By following up the gullies formed by the floods, scientists found that the destructive waters had all accumulated on comparatively small parts on the watersheds, only 6 percent of the area in Davis county, from which man-caused fires or unmanaged grazing had previously removed the plant cover. These early bare

areas, which had also lost a large part of the porous, dark-colored surface soil, offered no resistance to the accumulation of water, when finally a heavy summer storm struck full upon them. Instead of being held back by plants and porous soil, streams formed at once and the waters piled one upon the other, rushed down the ravines, uprooted the plants, carried along all sorts of debris, and almost without warning vomited it upon settlements below, much as a bilious person might do. 

These were sick watersheds, and, like a sick person, must be restored to health to prevent spasmodic recurrences of the dangerous symptoms.

Good Soil Removed by Erosion. Injury to lands close to the foot of the mountain is not by any means the whole injury suffered from floods. As soon as mountain vegetation is reduced in the density of its cover, in fact, almost as soon as it is injured in any way, the dark-colored top soil moves down the slope faster than it is formed by nature. In a few years, especially when the vegetation is repeatedly grazed in an improper manner or weakened by fires running through the dried plants, only raw unproductive subsoil is left. Such soils, with the humus gone and the natural openings sealed up, are both poor in their power to absorb water and in their ability to grow plants. 

Because of this intimate relation of the soil to runoff and to plant growth, the revegetation of such deteriorated watersheds is much more difficult than where part or all of the surface soil still remains. Dark-colored top soil is to a watershed or range what physical vigor is to a man. It is a natural resource easily and quickly dissipated, difficult to regain, and when too far spent requires hospital care or similar nursing to prevent a break-down.

Culinary Water Impaired by Erosion. Soil erosion causes streams to be muddy, at any rate for a time after storms, and otherwise impairs the quality of water in mountain streams by making settling, filtering and chemical treatment necessary. These treatments warm the water, give it an undesirable taste, or remove its crystal clearness. Such impairment can be escaped or prevented by maintaining a good plant cover on the watershed, by otherwise providing the area with good management, and by avoiding contamination, especially from man himself. 

Restoring Damaged Watersheds. As with a sick man, the first step in bringing about the recuperation of a sick watershed is to remove the cause of the trouble. Escaped fires need to be lessened in every possible way, that is, both reduced in number and put out before they spread far. City people and country folks, campers from far and near, can all make great contributions in this respect. Since 50 to 90 percent of fires are man-caused, consistent vigilance will largely remove this menace, or rather reduce it to the point of little injury.

Three things are necessary,-simple crude procedure that any person can do if he only wills so to do. 

First, he must build a camp fire only where the danger of spread is not great. When dry vegetation is too abundant he should remove it from several square feet down to bare mineral soil and hold the fire in a hole. 

In periods of high fire hazard, he should not build the fire at all. Secondly, no fire should be left smoldering. The coals should be spread out and thoroughly doused with water, or covered with mineral soil and tramped on. Thirdly, when a person sees a fire he should at once notify the nearest responsible officer, that is, on a national forest, the ranger or fire guard.

Where concentrated or untimely grazing is the cause of the deteriorated plant cover, proper management and control of grazing is the remedy. As will be shown in the next lesson, grazing in keeping with the amount and kind of forage is not harmful and, except to provide special care for "sore spots," need not be entirely prevented. On injured areas, however, grazing must be removed or reduced and managed until such a time as the vegetation can be re-established by natural revegetation. 

When natural processes are not effective or when these operate too slowly, special direct efforts are required to prevent a further breakdown of the watershed and to set in operation the restoration of the vegetation. On the Davis county watersheds, the areas on which floods originated have been terraced and reseeded to good grasses, largely by CCC boys under direction of the Intermountain Forest and Range Experiment Station and the Wasatch National Forest. A volume of water from summer storms, equal to those which caused the floods of 1923 and 1930, has been caught by the terrace trenches and held until it sank into the soil.

Experiments have shown that smooth bromegrass, mountain brome, slender wheat grass, and other grasses, with a little winter rye, have become established on the terraced areas and provide a dense plant cover effective in holding the soil in place and causing the water to percolate into the soil. In other areas a restoration of the vegetation has been provided by reseeding when no terraces have been made, as, for example, on one part of the Arrowrock addition to the Boise National Forest.

Forest Lands and Forest Supplies. Timber re- 

12

sources in its many aspects are closely related to watersheds, since on the best watersheds some sort of timberstand usually occurs. Removal of the trees, has, in many cases, been one of the causes of the watershed deterioration already noted. The partial maintenance of timber production is one of the factors in the restoration and the preservation of effective watersheds. 

Forest lands in the vicinity of the Mormon settlements have been and are now, in the main, valuable as sources of local timber supply rather than as sources of commercial lumbering. Usually the timber trees are small, and the forests broken into small areas that in rough country are well suited to the sort of lumber production required by the commercial lumber industry. 

During the period of early occupation and settlement in the Intermountain region, local sawmills sawed native timber into rough and knotty but strong lumber which was used for houses, farm buildings, and bridges, but not to any appreciable extent for a commercial lumber business. The pioneers in general did their own logging and had the sawing done in local mills, operated either cooperatively, or by a neighbor who did it for a fee. Before the railroads were extended to most of the towns, only native lumber and structural timbers were to be had. After the advent of the railroads, finishing lumber and much of the lower grades, and a large part of the structural timber were shipped in. 

The compact Mormon settlement system, based on irrigation and livestock production, required not only logs and squared timbers for buildings, but poles, posts, and short hand-hewed timbers for bridges, dams, irrigation headgates and flumes, for fences, corrals, gates, hay derricks, and many farm implements, such as leveler land-drags, harrow frames, hayracks, wagon

reaches and, in early days, wagon brakes, plow beams, and handles for forks, axes, picks, and shovels. Wood also provided fuel for the entire community in pioneer times and today for many outlying farms and villages. 

The available timber for such purposes made it possible in early days to maintain self-sufficient communities, without which Mormonism would have had an even more difficult time to get started in the Great Basin. 

As railroad building progressed and mining developed, many local residents, during the fall and winter, added considerably to their farm income by cutting railroad ties and mining props for market. In the 

Uintah mountains and along the upper Provo river much of the remaining timber is suitable for ties and mining props. Cedar posts were formerly a considerable source of winter income to local residents not otherwise employed. Nearby sources of these native 

Junipers, however, are now almost exhausted. Juniper posts are still seen, but they are hauled long distances or else the posts are rather small.

Forestry Improvements as a Source of Income. Long continued and varying demands on the small local timber supplies gradually reduced these and in some areas allowed the accumulation of tops, limbs and partly decayed wood, which sooner or later were burned over by fires that escaped human control. Removal of the larger trees for lumber, of the smaller ones for poles, posts and bridge timbers, etc., left the scant stand on all readily accessible areas rather closely used. 

The United States Forest Service is in many areas employing men at off-seasons of farm and other work to build roads and trails, and to remove dead and overthick stands, to develop recreational grounds, to provide erosion control and flood prevention measures. A

much larger program to care more fully for such needs is being talked of in some circles, and if it is brought about, will greatly aid in providing part time work for local farmers, stockmen, and laborers not fully employed. The national forests can become vast work reservoirs if the Federal government enters into this much needed program of improving its public lands. 

God Blessed the Intermountain Region. Though the water supply for the Intermountain region is not large, it was at first dependable and contributed mightily to the security of living in early days and especially during drought periods. God has blessed the region with high mountains and good valley land, so close together that water from small mountain streams can be used on rich soil in the valley. Mountain water also furnishes the cleanest and best drinking water. This is another major blessing which people on the wide plains do not enjoy.

Even the scarce supply of timber in the region is a blessing because it is close at hand, and also because that while not suited for commercial lumbering, it is even better than lumber forests for aiding in community development. Lumbering as well as mining has often led to the neglect of agriculture in the effort of residents to get rich by exploitation of great deposits of a natural resource. Small timber in varied sizes and quality aided greatly in farming, and thereby helped to make living secure in the early Mormon settlements, and will do so again when the people bring themselves to make the necessary provisions for managing this resource properly.

Chapter 22 

RANGE, WILDLIFE AND RECREATIONAL RESOURCES 

Wild lands serve not only as watersheds and as places to grow timber, but also as grazing ranges for domestic livestock, as homes for wildlife, and as spots for the rest or relaxation of human beings. As will be seen in succeeding lessons, uncultivated lands also contribute mightily to our resources and to good living by supplying coal, oil, minerals, phosphates, salt and building materials. Land put to one of these uses may in some cases not be available for other uses, but in the main the several uses may go on at the same time without great interference. For example, most of the mountainous land of the Intermountain region is subject to such "multiple use" as watersheds, timber-producing areas, home for wildlife, vacation grounds, and ranges for domestic livestock. When the various forms of land use are properly managed they disturb each other but little, as will be evident in the next few paragraphs. 

Ranges as a Source of Forage. Both mountain and valley land in Utah and neighboring states produce naturally a wealth of forage. Most of the timbergrowing areas have spaces between the trees which are partly occupied with bushes, grasses, and other herbs. All the grasses, with one or two exceptions, are valuable for livestock feed. A large part of the softstemmed flowering plants also furnish feed, though a number of them are gummy or otherwise unpalatable. 

The leaves and young shoots of several of the most common bushes are also browsed by both cattle and

RECREATIONAL RESOURCES 

181 sheep. A few of the herbs and shrubs, however, when eaten in large quantities, are poisonous to animals. 

Since snow remains in the high mountains, long after it has gone from the valleys and foothills, it is only in summer that plants can there make active growth. 

On such growth, domestic livestock can subsist in summer if the vegetation is allowed to keep well ahead of the grazing. When fall approaches, cold weather comes first at high elevations, and the animals must come down into the foothills and valleys, where, under proper management, they find dry forage and a little regrowth for the fall period. When snows come in the foothills and valleys the animals must go to the farms, or out to the "desert" winter ranges where the snows are not too deep to permit winter grazing. Finally, when spring comes, the animals return to the foothills and nearby valley edges, for forage during the spring period. 

In the mountain-valley-desert relationship, the Creator has given man seasonal feeding grounds for his livestock, which together with farm feeds can be made to carry the animals throughout the year. The balance between the different seasonal ranges is not perfect, however, and man must use intelligence and planning to make adequate feed supply available in all parts of the year. Again, man is called on to do his part in making such use of this great natural resource, as will result in the greatest good to the greatest number.

The Range Livestock Industry. More than six million sheep and about a million cattle subsist on the range lands of the Intermountain region from two to twelve months in the year. All the sheep, except a few purebred outfits, spend the entire year on the range in Utah, Nevada, and part of southern Idaho. North-

ward, winter feeding is practiced and lambing is done in sheds during February. Here, ordinarily, Rambouillet ewes are crossed with black-faced bucks to produce fast-growing desirable market lambs, which are usually sold about July, when prices are higher than they are later in the season.

In the yearlong grazing industry of the southern part of the region, cross-breeding of sheep is less generally practiced and Ramouillets predominate. Wool is more important than northward and market lambs are less important, though both contribute heavily to the income. Lambs are born on the open range usually in May and June, and are ready for market in early 

October. About this time the greatest number of lambs reach the market and the prices are likely to be lower than earlier in the year. The lower prices are largely compensated for by lower costs of production on the range.

Northward, most range cattle are kept on the farm in winter, and fed some hay to supplement the roughage gathered by grazing stubble fields. Where the winters are milder and where suitable winter range is available, cattle are also wintered without much regular feeding. Dairy cattle, some beef cattle, and horses are kept on the farm most of the year, though dry cattle and unused horses of many communities are grazed on national forest land in summer. 

The range industry is a peculiar joint product of the farms, private ranges, national forests, Taylor Act grazing districts, and remnant public domain not yet under control. Difficulties arise in some cases from regarding these various forms of land control as unrelated to each other, and also from the lack of full coordination between the stockmen and the agencies handling the land.

RECREATIONAL RESOURCES 

183 Ranges as a Source of Income. About one-third of the agricultural income of the Intermountain region is derived directly from the range industry. In addition nearly half of the remaining income from agriculture receives indirect contributions from grazing. Moreover, much of the business in the outlying towns arises directly or indirectly from range forage. A considerable part of business in Salt Lake City, Ogden, Pocatello, Boise, and Reno also originates on the range. This applies to sales, purchases, merchandising, banking, credit and professional practice. Outside the region, Chicago, Omaha, Kansas City, Denver, Los Angeles, San Francisco and Portland, benefit materially from the livestock trade of the Intermountain region.

The income from range livestock affects primarily the stockmen and their families. Formerly, the family incomes were more certain, and could be relied on year after year. During the last twenty to thirty years, however, incomes have fluctuated erratically, varying with range conditions, drought, market demands, and general financial trends. Markedly high prices one year and very low ones another year, together with an increase in indebtedness and mortgages on the land and livestock, have all contributed to making range livestock somewhat of a gambling enterprise at times more like mining than agriculture, so far as economic stability is concerned.

The forage production on the ranges, except on those of managed national-forest lands, is no longer so dependable as it was a generation ago. Many of the good forage plants are dead, and the remaining ones are weak. Sometimes this leaves more bare ground than was originally the case, sometimes the spaces formerly used by good forages have been occupied by

less valuable ones, some of which are nearly useless, and some even poisonous. Deterioration in forage production, when further accentuated by drought, untimely grazing, and overstocking, has brought about heavy death losses, and caused the animals to reach market time in poor condition of flesh and wool.

In an effort to overcome these effects on the animals, stockmen are feeding corn, or cottonseed cake to supplement the range forage. This causes expenses to mount, and incomes to waver. Some years, as in 

1932, when drought, high expenses, and depressed markets worked jointly against the stockmen, many outfits were taken over by the banks and credit associations.

Problems of Deteriorated Range. The problems of deteriorated range are widespread and general. It is extremely difficult to make accurate comparisons of plant-cover conditions now and at the time of settlement. Many of the older men now alive will remember grassy areas where now only sagebrush, or lowvalue weeds are to be found. A few areas of range that have been protected or partly protected against untimely grazing and over-use bear several times more grass or other forage than does similar open range. 

The national forests and a few ranges in other ownerships that have been conservatively grazed also show a much better plant cover. 

Originally grasses that lived several years constituted a large part of the feed value found on the ranges. On large tracts these valuable perennial grasses are very scarce and in some places are gone completely. Annual weeds, such as Russian thistle and the fall-annual cheatgrass, have largely replaced the original perennial bunch-grasses and valuable broad-leaved herbs.

RECREATIONAL RESOURCES 

185 The plants that cause "bighead" in sheep, and a number of other plants such as larkspur, lupines, death camas, and poisonous milkweeds, have increased greatly in some localities. Less acutely destructive, but nevertheless a serious menace, are the nearly inedible plants such as desert rabbitbrush, tarweed, and hound's tongue, so abundant on many of the once highly productive ranges. Not all grazing lands are deteriorated in the ways mentioned, but the best estimates indicate that about 75 percent of the range area has declined in forage production during thirty to forty years, and that as a whole the ranges produce only about half of what they originally did. This is a serious problem, but not a hopeless one, as will be seen in the next paragraph, where it is pointed out what man can do about it, and what must be done if the range livestock industry is to thrive.

Range Rehabilitation. It is estimated that out of 

135 million acres of range land in the Intermountain region, about 100 million acres will be improved by better range management. This means that stocking must be no heavier than the forage will support, that the grazing must not be too early in spring, and that the animals be not allowed to congregate too frequently at watering places, on bed grounds, and in resting places. At the Deseret Range Experimental Station fifty miles west of Milford, Utah, this kind of management in four years (1933-1937) more than doubled the forage production. Not all ranges will respond so quickly, but all can be put on the upward trend by good management.

Smaller, though still important areas, both in size and value, will need to be artificially reseeded to good grasses in order to restore them to a reasonable forage

production. About twelve million acres of Intermountain range land stand in need of this treatment. While experimental work to find the best means of doing this has barely got under way, real success has been ob- :ained when adapted grasses have been used and the proper seeding practices followed. Bare spots on the 

Davis county watersheds, from which floods originated a few years ago, are now well covered with long-lived valuable grasses produced by reseeding.

Such rehabilitation is a difficult task, but man is given intelligence with which to overcome obstacles and grows by exercising this power properly. It will take years to solve these problems, but a generation is not long in the life of a nation, in the economy of nature, or in the salvation of a people. Solomon wisely said, "Where there is no vision the people perish" (Proverbs 

29:18). Reasonable vision and intelligent application and cooperative effort, for which the Mormon people are well-known, can set in operation the adjustments needed to bring about range restoration, and the corresponding security of living among the range people. 

Wildlife Resources and their Management. Deer, elk, upland birds, waterfowl and small fur-bearers, once plentiful in Utah, Idaho and western Wyoming, were very scarce a few years ago. Then came an effort to restore them by protection as to time of hunting and by providing them with favorable home conditions. Elk were imported to several parts of Utah and together with deer have multiplied rapidly, especially on the favorable summer ranges of the national forests. The estimated 100,000 deer in Utah, and the elk, are not too many were they more or less uniformly distributed. 

Could they be controlled to reasonable numbers in given localities, these animals would not interfere mate-

RECREATIONAL RESOURCES 

187 rially with domestic livestock and would add both to the beauty and the landscape and provide for hunting. 

Deer and elk have increased so greatly in areas that have good summer range that they are a problem in winter. Their winter ranges are seriously reduced in area, owing to the fact that the land now cultivated was, before settlement, the winter feeding grounds of deer and elk. This shortage of winter range causes them to encroach on private land, sometimes on orchards and haystacks. While farmers and ranchmen like game, they are concerned about the over population of game in certain areas which use the feed needed by domestic stock, and cause trespass of game on private lands.

Cooperative management on the part of the Forest Service who control most of the summer ranges, the 

State Game Commission who technically own the game, and the stockmen, would overcome the worst difficulties. There has been some conflict of interest and of plans between sportsmen who have at times restricted the hunting of female deer or elk, and stockmen who are locally being badly crowded. Mormon communities should express themselves in such a way as to overcome this lack of cooperation. 

Good waterfowl refuges at the Bear River marshes and smaller ones in other places have permitted the increase of waterfowl, and especially of ducks. Regulation of hunting and the establishment of state game and bird preserves have afforded some protection to land birds, though they are still very scarce. Beaver, once nearly extinct, are also seen occasionally, and along with the muskrat may be increased by protection. 

Recreation Resources and Facilities. One of the great assets of living in the Intermountain country is

Many of the streams and fishing, though some are 

Not much, though a little access to the canyons, streams, and mountain recesses, where excellent hot weather recreation places may be reached in a short time by automobile. Country and town people can readily have an afternoon picnic and find relief from valley heat. mountain lakes also supply nearly barren of fish food. more distant, are the great scenic wonders of Grand 

Canyon, Teton Peaks, Yellowstone Park, Bryce and 

Zion Canyons, and Cedar Breaks. Craters of the Moon, Timpanogos Cave, Lehman Cave, Shoshone Falls and other spots of natural beauty are not far away. Spots of such transcendent beauty or sublimity relax the mind and release it from the tension of everyday struggle and worry.

The United States Forest Service and the National 

Park Service, have much improved the ease and comfort of camping by developing camp grounds and providing culinary water, tables, and outdoor stoves. Their greatest service to the resources, however, has been to preserve from vandalism these spots of beauty, coolness, relaxation or fishing. Streams, too, have been stocked with fish, and game and pheasants imported.

Restocking is less valuable, in the long run, however, than is the preservation of the natural environment in which the fish or animals live securely and reproduce readily. It is largely the preservation of the natural environment on the national forests and parks that has preserved the game or removed them from near extinction.

Cooperation With God. Endowed by the Creator with a splendid set of resources, man needs to bring his affairs and activities into tune with the law of God and cooperate with Him in preserving the range, wild-

RECREATIONAL RESOURCES 

189 life and recreational resources. Man cannot of himself make streams, shady nooks or grassy glades, or change bare spots into plant-covered ranges. Only nature can do this, by following the eternal laws of the universe. 

God has given to man the mountains, the streams, the plants and the animals. To man God has given also intelligence and a versatile body. It is not too much to expect that man shall use his intelligence to put his affairs in tune with the Creator, and so to govern his use of the natural resources as to preserve them for his children, and his children's children. It is no easy problem to solve, but man will reach his greatest development and make his greatest progress, both temporally and spiritually, by conquest over great difficulties. 

When man's effort is strongest, the assurance that he will be given Divine assistance is greatest.

Chapter 23 

Manufacturing requires raw material and properly directed human labor which changes the raw material into a more useful form. The value of the manufactured articles may be principally in the material of which it is made, as in the case of wheat flour, but usually the amount and kind of work that have been employed in various steps of the manufacturing processes exceeds in cost by many times the value of the original material. 

An example of this is a pocketknife or a watch, in either of which the raw materials represent a mere trifle in the value of the final product, the value of labor in the finished product being twenty or perhaps one hundred times as great. In manufacturing processes skilled labor is more expensive and more effective than is unskilled, and brain force than physical force. 

For example, crude iron ore worth $10 a ton made into pig iron may be worth $20; into horse-shoes $90; into knife-blades $200; and into watch springs $1,000. 

All of the value in the watch springs, except $10 for the original iron, is in the mental and skilled physical labor that have been used in refining, shaping, and altering the product at various steps. Some steps in the process were done by machinery, which in turn were largely the result of accumulated labor. 

Early Utah Manufacturing. Because pioneer settlements in Utah were 1,500 miles from Missouri ports, the people were forced to make at home everything they possibly could. Now, the great needs of any people are food, clothing, and shelter. After these 13

needs are satisfied, the more cultural desires, such as that for beautiful ornaments and education, are given more consideration than when man is struggling for actual existence. Accordingly, the pioneers developed flour mills, sawmills, and woolen mills. Silk and cotton textiles were made for a time, but after the railroads came and industry developed, cotton and silk goods were brought in, and manufacturing settled down to the commodities for which raw products were more readily and more naturally provided. 

Crude iron foundries were made and metal works begun. Shoes, harnesses, saddles, furniture, woodwork, and many other articles were made, almost entirely by hand. Before long printing was started, and a newspaper was going by 1850, the presses being hauled across the plains to Salt Lake City. Paper was made locally, though not until several years later. 

Some of these early industries grew gradually with time and with the material progress of the colony. 

Others made little real advancement, as for example the iron industry, in which it was 1924 before the first modern plant was erected three miles south of Provo. 

Some early attempts, such as that with silk, disappeared. 

Handicrafts in wood, metal, leather, clothing, and similar trade skills were at first carried on readily, because many of the early immigrants to Utah came from the industrial districts of England. Leather work, including hand shoemaking, which once thrived, is scarcely seen any more. Harness making and saddlery also have declined since automobiles became so extremely important. Metal craftsmen are now seldom seen, and only a few hand cabinet makers are left. Tailoring of men's suits is still practiced by those who learned the craft in the Old World. Hat making is, however, no

longer followed as a vocation. The same is true of good bookbinding and picture framing. Deterioration in the handicrafts is one of the causes of much unemployment nowadays. Skilled workmen are few, and only a small proportion of those who are out of employment are able craftsmen. Perhaps the machine industries have developed too far and it may be necessary to go back a step to more hand work.

Classes of Industrial Development. Some phases of industry are rather well advanced, whereas others have lagged considerably. A truer picture of manufacturing in the Intermountain region can be drawn by looking at each major phase separately, rather than trying to view the whole, which is anything but uniform in the progress of the different industries. 

The major phases of industry in the principal area of Mormon occupation are (1) Textiles and clothing, (2) foods, (3) metal and oil, (4) wood crafts, (5) building industries, and (6) power and transportation.

Textiles. The woolen industries lasted long and grew. Blanket making is still active, and several knitting mills for sweaters, women's dress goods, and underwear are operating vigorously. Overalls, work shirts, women's house dresses and some middle class ready-to-wear clothing is made in Ogden and near 

Salt Lake City. Millinery is a considerable industry. 

Awnings, tents, and mattresses are made in sizable quantities. Accompanying industries, such as laundry and dry-cleaning, are large enough to care for local needs.

The textile industry, however, falls far short of its possibilities, even in woolens. The large factory of the 

Provo Woolen Mills has not reopened since a fire stopped it a few years ago. The historic advantages and

the financing customs all favor shipping wool in the grease to New England, where it is cleaned, graded, spun into cloth and manufactured into clothing. In spite of Utah's magnificent crop of fine wools, and in spite of a considerable local industry, the great volume of business in wool goes through Boston even for such crude work as cleaning. Some think this is because of legitimate trade channel procedure, but others think there is a definite attempt on the part of New England industry to choke off any attempts in the wool producing states. Whatever the motives and underlying causes, the great volume of wool manufacturing is 2,500 miles from the Great Basin. Whether this tide is too strong to be changed remains to be seen. 

Foods. With foods the factory processes are mainly those of removing coarse parts and of preserving perishable products. The actual change of the product in manufacture is much less complete than is the case with textiles or with metals. Flour milling products are largely various grades of flour, the production of which consists of removing the bran and grinding the interior of the kernel and sieving the various fragments before the last reduction to flour. Bran and shorts are by-products of the flour-making process. 

Cereal manufacture consists also largely in physical operations. Various biscuit, bread, pastry and special preparations are made, also by simple operations. Good chemical work is done in the large mills as an aid in making uniform products. Several fully modern large capacity mills operate in the region, largely in and near Ogden, though smaller mills are scattered throughout the region. Small grist mills, not competing successfully with large plants that have modern equipment, are gradually disappearing.

Bakeries, from small one-room affairs to large establishments, make bread and pastry in its many forms, both fancy and plain. Home-baking has greatly decreased and centralized baking increased proportionately during the last fifteen to twenty years. The cracker industry has also grown, but a great volume of package material is still imported. Some maccaroni is also made.

The canning of fruits and vegetables is now a major industry, and much of it is done in large central plants, many of which are controlled by California capital. Utah seemed, a few years ago, to have a great future, owing to the high quality fruits, tomatoes, peas and beans, but of late years the markets have often been poor and production has been greatly limited. Pickles, too, are made in a small way.

The curing of meats has been a home industry from the beginning, and still is in many country districts. 

Like home-baking, however, it is being more and more given up in the home, especially in the towns, where it has almost disappeared. Cattle, sheep, and hogs were sold, and beef, lamb, bacon and ham bought as cured products and at greatly advanced prices. Until a few years ago, meat curing was all done outside the region. 

Good plants are now operating at North Salt Lake and Ogden. These plants are a great boon to the stockmen, not only for supplying local markets, but for encouraging the fattening of livestock, which previously had been largely done in the corn belt. Irrigation agriculture, which produces an abundance of high-grade feeds, requires the feeding industry to utilize cheap forages otherwise not fully used, to furnish winter labor, to supply farm fertilizers, and to produce a marketable commodity. This form of near-manufacture as an ad-

junct of the meat-packing industry ought to be made more of in Intermountain agriculture.

Dairying is a vast industry and is treated as a chemurgic enterprise. The butter and ice cream industries are thriving and have markets beyond the region. For the country as a whole the demand for butter and cheese is often over-supplied and prices then waver. High-quality cheese and butter have opportunities. Cottage cheese and the by-product feeds need careful attention.

Sugar-making is a big chemurgic process, and its related industries of honey extraction and candy making have made much progress, but are limited in outside markets by competition with outside products. Many chocolate and cocoa products are made, but not to a truly large-scale extent.

The food industries, while well developed, have not succeeded in reaching outside markets in a major way. Even the sugar industry, the largest of all Intermountain factory processes, requires tariff protection to enable it to hold its own against sugar made from tropical sugar cane. 

Metals and Oils. The vast metal industry has developed only the first crude steps in local industrial growth. Some of the largest industrial plants are metal concentrating mills and smelters not far from the Bingham metal district. These, however, chiefly prepare the metals for shipment in a concentrated form to points outside the region where the real manufacturing is done in the making of machinery, tools, utensils, and useful small articles. Utah is the first-ranking silver-producing state in the Union, but has not a real silver manufacturing plant. Some articles are made of copper, and the one steel mill has already been noted. Whether the In-

termountain region must remain merely a producer of raw material products is a vital question to her future welfare. Occasionally, indications of local factory development have been noted and cast hopeful beams on the horizon of possible industry. Salt Lake City has a large oil refinery, operated entirely on outside oil, but with good railroad facilities such intermediate point industries may develop importance.

Wood Crafts. Not being a major timber area, the 

Intermountain region may never become important in the wood crafts. Several good planing mills are in operation, but shingles once made here are now imported from the Pacific Northwest. A big development in railroad ties and mining timbers has been noted in the lesson regarding watersheds and timber. There is no furniture factory of real industrial complexion in the entire region. 

Building Industries. Building and construction industries are active, some phases of them reaching far outside the region. Local constructors of large office buildings and major steel and concrete projects have been successful, building the famous Boulder Dam and hundreds of less imposing but still substantial structures throughout the West. Street paving is a specialty of several concerns and dwelling-house builders are numerous.

Accompanying the building industries are brick kilns which produce high-grade bricks of several sorts. 

Cement making is also a vigorous industry, based on ample supplies of raw materials. Lime, clay tile, and plaster industries were noted in the lesson on "The 

Strength of the Hills."

Some paint and roofing material is also produced, and with raw products at hand could become important, unless commercial obstacles are encountered.

Power Production. The Intermountain region should always be an important producer of electric power. All local needs, or at least practically all, can be met from local water power and from the vast coal beds. Electricity is one of the new forces in industrial growth. On the recent Federal water-storage projects, the struggle for control of a substantial part of the electricity has been as intense as that for water. Cheap lighting, heating, power for operating machinery, and transportation energy are essential to factory growth and accompanying trades that are much needed in Utah and adjacent territory. 

Benefits of Manufacturing. Many are the benefits of manufacturing to the community ready for it. The production of the required raw products is stimulated, both in the quantities necessary for factory operation and also in the "grades" desired for a high-quality product. In most cases the intensity of farming is increased if it is an agricultural commodity that is being processed. If it is a non-agricultural commodity, production is organized and put on a business basis. Usually, too, manufacturing helps to consume surplus produce and utilizes what might otherwise have been waste. It may introduce new crops or provide for other raw materials not hitherto used. Altogether it calls for a more uniform quality of source material and stimulates labor, skill, and education needed to provide that uniformity.

Not only is the production of raw materials affected for the better, but the people of the community are also affected. New and greater business allows for community growth; men and women who might otherwise be unemployed, or only partly employed, now have more complete employment. Production of raw mate-

rials as a chief occupation ordinarily results in seasons of active employment and other seasons of slack employment. Manufacturing usually lengthens the period of activity, and, under favorable circumstances, brings about a demand for labor that extends throughout the year.

People Strengthened by Industry. The laborers. that are used in factory processes and in providing uniform raw material are more valuable if they are skilled in the particular operations required and if educated in ordinary business affairs. Usually, therefore, labor is better paid in a manufacturing community because it is more skilled. Schools are ordinarily more efficient and there is also a more businesslike attitude and more interest in cultural development. In large cities many of the more favorable results of manufacturing do not reach the laboring people, whereas in the smaller semiagricultural communities, such as those of the Intermountain region, there is every reason for regarding an increased industry as a forward step. 

Many commodities are too bulky to ship long distances without their being concentrated, as in the case of sugarbeets or low-grade ores, making them cheaper and less risky to handle as with chilled meat; making them less perishable as with canned fruits and vegetables; or more quickly marketable as with ready-made clothing. In addition, the value of the manufactured articles is increased as a result of the labor expended in processing them. Often, because of its greater usefulness, the new article is worth even more than the sum of the raw product plus the labor devoted to its manufacture. 

As a result of all these direct and indirect benefits of manufacturing, people in such a community have

more regular and better paid employment, and have a better opportunity of providing living incomes for young people who wish to start homes and families and for the aging mature people. Not only may a community grow in size and in wealth, but the morale of its members will likely be better and less fraught with fear of idleness or insecure employment when small factory industries are added to the farming or mining occupations. The Church Welfare Plan favors such development of small industries on account of this being one of the most dependable means of aiding in the effort to restore broken-spirited men and families and of preventing the occurrence of discouragement in others. (See chapter 5)

1. What manufacturing industries are active in the Intermountain country? Why did woolen industries persist and silk and cotton industries disappear? 

2. In the mining industries, what is meant by concentrating mills? By smelters? By foreign capital? What becomes of the profits of the mining industry? Why, then, are these industries assets? 

3. What great future resources has Utah in reserve? Why are they not used now? When will they be utilized? 

4. Why is manufacturing valuable for a people? What conditions are required for manufacturing to be successful? 

5. Should manufacturing be encouraged as part of the Church 

Welfare Plan? Explain your answer. 

6. Why have many of the pioneer manufacturing enterprises among the Latter-day Saints been discontinued? 

7. Review Chapter 5 and discuss the possibilities of increasing manufacturing enterprises in your locality.

References: 

1. Utah: Resources and Activities, Utah State Department of Public Instruction, 1933.* 

2. Parkins and Whittaker, Our Natural Resources and Their 

Conservation, John Wiley, 1936.* 

3. Howe, Chemistry in Industry, Volume II, New York Chemical Foundation, 1924.* *May be obtained from libraries or by purchase.

Chapter 24 

"Chemurgy" is a word artifically coined to express the growing of crops especially to be manufactured into some marketable factory product. It is much talked of lately, having been urged by its supporters as a better means of handling crop surpluses than is reduction in acreage. It has, however, a rather different approach and may not be intimately related to acreage control as has been suggested. At any rate it has no connection with either national or local politics, being purely industrial in its nature. It is not new, as shall be seen shortly. It is partly a new name for an old phase of handling certain agricultural crops, and partly a revival of the old idea that the farmer would benefit by applying industrial uses to crops grown on the farm. 

Irrigation Farming Invites Chemurgy. The high cost of irrigation farming makes it urgent that some, and if possible all, of the crops grown be of such a nature as to produce high returns per acre. Applying water to farm crops is a very expensive operation. 

Great outlays of money and labor are necessary to build dams and storage reservoirs, to divert the water through headgates, to construct canals and farm ditches. Much water is lost by seepage between the headgate and the field, and the most careful attention is necessary to distribute the water properly over the field. Moreover, the land must previously have been prepared for irrigation. These high costs make it absolutely necessary that the farmer get high returns, and this in turn calls for intensive farming, that is, for the use of much labor or fertilizer or both on each acre.

As has been already noted, the soils of the arid region are rich in plant nutrients and tend to produce crops of greater yield and of superior quality. The control that the farmer can exercise over the quantity of water used and the time at which it is applied, give him an ability not possible in ordinary rainfall farming, to make the crop run to stalks, seeds, or roots as the case may require. The bright sunshiny weather of the Intermountain region tends also to produce higher yields of crops, which are also richer in sugar or starch than are crops of more cloudy regions. In some cases, the cool nights of autumn also favor superior quality, as in the case of sugar beets.

The compact settlement system under irrigation, and in particular the Mormon village system, also favor the introduction of factory processing by making labor available. The cooperative action required in developing irrigation as an institution, and the Priesthood quorum organizations, are all natural assets to industrial enterprises both in large factories, such as beetsugar making, and in small units, such as small canning plants. Surplus labor also needs such an avenue of employment as crop factories would supply. 

Unemployment conditions and the need for finding a greater and more regular demand for man labor have brought to the front consideration of such opportunities as chemurgic development might bring. Dr. John A. 

Widtsoe, of the Council of the Twelve, made an admirable statement at the Pacific Northwest Conference when he said:

In Utah five things must be done: First, we must find new uses for old crops; second, we must encourage, by means of propaganda, the use of crops that are now already being manufactured; third, we must discover new crops that may be manufactured; fourth, we must care for the wastes on the farm; fifth, we must find outlets for farm surplus through chemurgical means.

This, then, is the problem with which agriculture is faced. Finally, in solving the economic phases of it, it must ever be kept in mind that even more important than industrial development, is the development of social conditions in which better and richer lives may result.

Sugar Beets a Suitable Industrial Crop. Sugar beet growing is a good example of the relation of chemurgy to agriculture. Beets require deep, thorough tillage, a good crop rotation and the application of manure in order to get a high acre yield. The land is well prepared in order that good stands may be obtained, and thinning may be done without killing too many beets.

Weeding and irrigation are usually done in a thorough manner, and insects fought when they appear. At harvest the beets are topped by hand and either hauled at once or piled and covered with tops to reduce losses in weight of the beets by evaporation. Whether hauled at once or later from piles, they are loaded by hand. 

This is a good example of the extra hand labor involved in growing high-value crops, suitable for irrigated agriculture. The intensive cultivation, the rotation and manuring required to obtain high yields of beets, leave the land in good condition for succeeding crops, whether grain, potatoes or alfalfa, which, after beets, ordinarily produce higher yields than other crops. 

In the factory the beets are washed, sliced, steamed, the juice extracted and treated chemically, boiled down, the syrup run through the spinners, and the sugar sacked and stored. The beet pulp and tops in the field, together with alfalfa, hay and grain grown in the crop rotations, are excellent feeds for livestock. Beet tops and pulp, stubble grazing, straw and old hay sweetened with some beet molasses are largely unmarketable, but

when turned into livestock products constitute another source of income.

Moreover, beets are grown by contract and farmers know at planting time what the harvest price is to be, thereby largely removing the uncertainty that accompanies the marketing of potatoes, fruits or wheat. This definite price, the great amount of hand labor used in growing the crop and in manufacturing the roots into sugar, and the feed by-products, as well as indirect benefits of its intensive cultivation, mark sugar-beets as one step in the prosperity of irrigation agriculture. 

Canning Fruits and Vegetables. Another example of the advantages of chemurgy is found in the canning of fruits and vegetables. When crops of peaches, and cherries in other parts of the country were poor, the direct market might absorb the fresh crop at good prices. At other times, and especially when high yields were obtained in other sections of the country, the demand for fresh fruit was weak and the prices scarcely paid the costs of moving the crop. At such times the waste was so enormous that canning was resorted to, and several large factories were built and fruit grown according to contract. The prices were below those obtained in the few favorable years, but much above that of years with glutted markets.

Large factories require that several crops be utilized to prevent the costly canning plant from remaining idle. Tomatoes, green peas, green beans, corn and carrots have been canned to lengthen out the operating season. In the canning plants much sorting is necessary to help make the product uniform and of high quality. Women and girls do much of the hand work inside the factory, such as peeling fruits and tomatoes and removing foreign substances from peas and beans.

Men, too, have employment on the heavier work of handling the machinery, unloading the fresh product from the farm, hauling away, and stacking the cases of filled cans, and later loading them for market. 

The crops are grown under direction of the firm that processes them. Contracting farmers get seed of the best varieties, grow the crop by the most approved methods, get help in combatting insects and diseases, and receive instructions as to the best time and the best methods to follow in harvesting the crops in order that the most uniform and most marketable product may be obtained.

Some by-products of canning are useful also for livestock feeds, as in the case of pea silage. Tomato and fruit wastes are not as yet handled so effectively, but with some attention could be profitably made into vinegar, or with suitable roughage to absorb the juices might be preserved for livestock feeding. Experimental studies applied to this problem have promise of yielding valuable results. Though canning has much improved crop conditions, as compared with those prevailing previously, much yet remains to be done in disposing of the by-product wastes and in marketing the canned crops, before this industry can be considered fully successful. 

Processing Milk. Butter and cheese factories, and condensed milk factories, have developed in an effort to handle milk more profitably. The dairy industry has increased greatly as a result. A more complete employment of local labor in caring for the cows, milking, and operating the factory has resulted and done much to make incomes more regular and dependable. These phases of processing, however, while not exactly in that field of manufacturing known as chemurgy, are

closely related to it and may for present purposes be considered to belong.

Skim milk as a by-product from butter-making, as well as curds and whey from cheese manufacturing, are splendid hog and poultry feeds, rich in protein and vitamins, and, in the case of skim milk, in mineral nutrients. The liquid feeds help also in making the consumption of dry roughages and grains more effective. 

Dairy manufacturers, accompanied as they are in the larger towns by the business of providing more sanitary fresh bottled milk, are great aids to preserving public health. The increased cleanliness sought in caring for the cows and handling the milk are real benefits to the consuming public and to the farmers who practice new sanitary methods.

Dried milk, and using the milk casein for artificial ivory in combs, fountain pens, trinket boxes, etc., has not yet developed very fully in this region. 

Rayon, Man-Made Silk. The silk worm in China and Japan feeds on mulberry leaves, and when the time has come to go into its resting stage, it spins a cocoon around itself consisting of five hundred to six hundred yards of silk fiber. Considerable interest has been taken for generations in the possibility of making silk artificially, but it was not until 1884 that Chardonnet, a 

French nobleman, thought out the correct procedure. 

Later, in 1905, Cross, Bevan, and Beadle, English chemists, introduced "viscose" silk, which is now commonly called "rayon." Of four processes, this one is now the most used. By 1926, American rayon plants were producing 60,000,000 pounds out of the world's output of 200,000,000. By 1933, America manufactured 

200,000,000 of the world's total of 650,000,000 pounds, which is more than six times the amount of silk produced by silk worms.

The manufacture of rayon begins with the short and nearly useless fibers on cottonseed known as "linters," or with spruce wood ground and made into pulp.

When treated with caustic soda “lye" and other chemicals, and washed, the result is a paste of glue-like consistency which in chemistry is said to be viscous, that is, like thick paste. This paste is put in machines that spin rapidly and force the paste through very small tubes, after which it is wound on reels and becomes, after bleaching, thread ready for making into cloth.

Rayon is strong, wears well, takes dyes readily and makes fast color, and has a bright luster, making it desirable for dress goods. It is becoming used in place of ordinary cotton dress goods and of some woolens. 

This may not be a net gain for agriculture, but it expresses the underlying idea of chemurgy, to use waste or cheap agricultural materials and manufacture them into usable marketable products.

This industry is not of great importance to the Intermountain region, but is to the South, and may become so in the Pacific Northwest, where woods suitable for rayon pulp are found in abundance. 

Possible New Chemurgic Industries. With so much benefit derived from a few successful, or partly successful, industries, and with such favorable community and crop-growing conditions, it would help much to add a few more crops capable of industrial processing.

Flax has been much talked of and indeed has possibilities. The crop grows successfully in the region. and produces good yields of both seed and fiber. The seed, used primarily for oil manufacture, furnishes in the remnant oilmeal a by-product feed rich in protein, oil and minerals, and therefore of great value. The fiber industry, when properly developed, makes good 14

returns in thread and yarn for cordage, bags, and in Europe for linen cloth. There is much hand labor involved in retting, cleaning, and milling of the fiber. It has so much promise it should be given a thorough investigation as to its possibilities. It may be that all the flax industry needs to get started is a concern that will provide for handling the product.

The making of fuel alcohol has also been much talked of, and may have great possibilities, but much industrial pioneering remains to be done before the way is clear for this industry to develop. Ordinary potatoes, sweet potatoes, and Jerusalem artichokes are all considered as sources of starch. The starch must be changed to sugar with some sort of yeast, and the sugar fermented into alcohol. It is then necessary to denature the alcohol and make up the proper mixtures. For this there are several proposals, two of the most common ones being very distinct from each other.

One proposes a ten to fifteen percent mixture of alcohol in gasoline, which would increase the use of alcohol about twelve to fifteen times over its present use. This use is said to reduce the production of carbon monoxide, a deadly poison to humans, and also to overcome much of the "knock" in gasoline. 

The second proposal is to make a fuel called "agol," which is about 78 per cent alcohol, with only a trace of gasoline in it, which is to be later mixed with various proportions of gasoline. Motor machinery may have to be adjusted to consuming alcohol-rich fuels. 

Discussions at the Chemurgic conferences indicated considerable promise for this fuel, but it has not yet come into wide use.

The chief requirements of starches to be used in the making of alcohol is cheap source material, such as

small and surplus potatoes, surplus sweet potatoes, and the Jerusalem artichoke, which is not an artichoke at all, but a relative to the common sunflower, which has bulbous underground stems, something similar to potatoes only more elongated and irregular in shape. This plant, a native of Eastern United States and the Great Plains, is drought-enduring, and is hardy in other respects. 

It is said to produce 400 to 1,000 bushels an acre under favorable circumstances. It grows well in Utah, and is an excellent pasturage feed for hogs to root out. The residue from sugar or alcohol manufacture is also good for feeding. The industrial connection between the crop and alcohol, or the high-grade sugar that can be made from it, has not yet been completed. The possibilities need to be developed by way of practical application, as the chemistry for the industry has been shown to be sound.

The manufacture of starch from ordinary potatoes has some virtue, as its quality for sizing cloth is superior. The dextrin made from potato starch is also known to have splendid sticking properties, and is useful for its effective sealing properties, as is required in pastes for wallpaper and similar work. 

The soy bean is a basic crop in China and Japan, where it is manufactured into oil, the remnant meal being a high-protein food for man. Good casein products are also made from it as from milk. As animal feed it is also superior and is grown rather widely in parts of the corn belt. It has yet to become established as an irrigated crop in the Intermountain region, though it has been grown here successfully. 

Occasionally, interest flares up for a short time in plants that are thought to have rubber-producing qualities. One of the milkweeds and a Mexican shrub called

guayule have each had an inning, but as yet no scores have been made. It does not follow, however, that because nothing has yet come of a crop that nothing will do so. They all deserve to be investigated.

Leading the Way with New Industrial Crops. For several of the crops named, it seems likely that proper commercial development is what is most needed. Many things that look possible develop slowly or have some hidden difficulty that prevents development. A little research may overcome the obstacle, and given a proper sound promotion may grow into a real industry. 

Someone must lead out with the capital necessary to get such things under way. As yet no one has seen fit to do this in the case of flax, fuel alcohol, potato starch, soybeans, or in rubber-producing plants. The likelihood that the first capital so used may not yield dividends adds to the difficulty. Possibly some form of group promotion may have to show the way into these new chemurgic enterprises. They would be boons to irrigated agriculture and to employment in the region if they succeeded in establishing new industries. 

 1. What is meant by chemurgy? What industries of the In termountain region are chemurgic? 

2. What added employment comes from sugar beets as compared with potatoes? In what ways are industrial crops superior to market crops? What uses are made of the by-products from sugar and canning industries? 

3. Where is flax an important chemurgic crop? What are its chief products and its by-products? Why is it not grown in the West? 

4. Tell the story of rayon, bringing out its advantages and disadvantages to Western United States. To the South. 

5. What is fuel alcohol? What are its values? How may it be used? What crops are its possible sources? 

6. What virtue has Jerusalem artichokes? Why is not this valuable crop grown?

7. Why is chemurgy of interest to workers in the Church Welfare Program? 

8. From your State Agricultural College obtain up-to-date information as to the progress made in your state to promote "Chemurgy." 

9. What conditions have made imperative the study of new uses for farm crops? 

10. Why is the welfare of city communities directly proportionate to the increase or decrease of farm prosperity?

3. Pacific Northwest Chemurgic Conference.* 

4. Chamberlain and Browne, Chemistry in Agriculture, New
