Chapter 25 

During pioneer periods when manufactured products had to be hauled from Missouri River ports, partly worn goods were repaired or made over. Later, when the railroads reached most communities, when factory goods were cheap and wages high, it was the common practice to discard things that were only somewhat worn or slightly damaged. Periods of hard times, following financial depressions, reduced incomes but left most families without skill to mend, repair, or make over such things as clothes, shoes, or furniture. 

Welfare Industries. Prolonged periods, during which low incomes prevailed among large fractions of city population, again brought into existence an organized program of making over, mending, remodeling, and practicing other forms of economy. Social welfare organizations have developed various means of collecting discarded clothes, shoes, rugs, furniture, and children's toys. Branches of women's clubs or welfare groups of other sorts meet at stated intervals to clean, mend, or remodel the discarded articles that needy families can use to supply various basic wants. 

Just before Christmas time, dolls, toys, and nicknacks of many sorts are cleaned, repaired, redressed, and painted and later used as gifts for children who would otherwise not be visited by Santa Claus. Playthings and pretty trinkets have become holiday institutions and bring a degree of pleasure and cheerfulness to people with low incomes, far out of proportion to the value of the simple objects that are so used.

Fruits and vegetables are in many cases canned or dried and stored for periods of distress. The food requirements of poor families can, thus, be much more correctly provided than if left to unorganized charity, or to organized charities that have no means of using the able-bodied but needy men and women, in helping to operate the industries that feed and clothe them. 

Cleaning and Mending Clothes. Discarded clothing, systematically collected in cities and towns and brought to a central place, usually has, among badly used ones, many articles slightly injured and not greatly worn. The district and regional storehouses, now in operation by groups of L. D. S. stakes in Utah and 

Idaho, are good examples. One visit to the Four Stake

Bishop's Storehouse at Ogden revealed more than fifty good-quality street and business dresses for women ready to distribute on a bishop's order. Some of these had required only cleaning, and many others only a few stitches to repair a ripped seam or replace a few buttons. of Not less than eighty cotton dresses ready to wear could be seen hanging in two long rows. The array sizes would warrant a fit for almost any ordinary-sized woman, girl or child. The woman foreman in charge explained that besides washing and pressing, only a few minutes and a few cents' worth of material had been required to put each of these dresses in its now usable and attractive condition.

About a dozen men's suits, and a similar number of extra trousers, with miscellaneous coats and jackets, were also ready for use. No man would need be ashamed to be seen at an ordinary office in clothes as good as these, nor need any one who had them be exposed unduly to wind or cold. They were substantial clothes of stylish pattern.

All of these dresses, suits, trousers and jackets come in at "old clothes" collections and need only to be sorted, cleaned, and slightly repaired. Many women of well-to-do families when they go to town, wear dresses of no better material nor better tailored, nor of more attractive design than half of the dresses seen here. In fact, many of them had been used only a few times before being discarded. One wondered who could have afforded to discard them. Other clothes were plain but good.

Old Suits Made New. Suspended in another row were remodeled jackets, dresses, winter coats, children's rompers, and small boy's trousers, made over from women's dresses or men's suits that were too worn or too badly torn to permit being made attractive in their present size and form. Here the skill of the lady foreman was evident. Security women workers, in some cases, had done most of the work under direction. 

In other cases, Relief Society leaders had worked for a time.

These made-overs showed no evidence of the previous wear or the rents they had received. Many jackets had zipper fasteners, elastic wrist bands, and gathered backs. Most of them had stylish pocket vents and many had overlap fronts with neck flaps. A torn pair of man's trousers was made into a thoroughly good-looking pair of small boy's all-wool bib overalls. 

Any unaffected professional man should have been pleased to see his five to seven-year-old son in this serviceable and really good-looking garb. These are only examples of a large number of similar articles of suitable attire.

Thoughtful visitors were even more profoundly impressed by the system of training in vogue. Ward

work leaders had on several occasions come in for training, and after five to ten lessons have gone back to their own Relief Society groups and put on a program of cleaning, mending, and remodeling in their own wards. From here the program was carried to the homes of competent but needy families, and the mothers taught to do such work for themselves. Such help as enables the families to help themselves is really social work with a capital "S." It would be better if there were more of it, systematically operated. 

Scraps of Cloth. Scraps of cloth, such as remnants from made-overs, from badly worn dresses or other light-weight but strong attire, were saved and made into fancy quilt covers. Heavy goods from men's suits and women's coats went into heavy quilts for cold weather. Handwork on the quilts was done largely as ward group projects, several from one elders' quorum, who reported an enthusiastic miscellaneous committee activity which drew into these parties several inactive quorum members and wives. One would at first think that the fancy quilts were made by skilled women, and would be surprised to find that two dainty ones were made by "men only."

Discarded underwear, shirts, dresses, and suits, too worn for use as whole cloth, have been in some wards made into "carpet" rags for rugs and matting. 

Older people were reminded of the days when the floors of all rooms except the parlor were covered with home-made carpets thus provided, and wondered why it could not again be done. Many a housewife in wellto-do families prides herself on small rag rugs to be put by beds, in the bathroom and playroom. The wife of a professor at a Utah college has made magnificent hooked rugs from discarded silk stockings. The ques-

tion literally jumped into the visitor's mind as to what might be made of old neckties, dress belts, flounces, etc., in the way of pads or light-weight rugs. 

Old window curtains and drapes were also repaired or made into new articles. Worn towels, shirts, underwear, pajamas and similar cloth scraps can be dyed and worked into a variety of new things, such as rugs, pads, doll dresses, the cloth parts of playthings and decorations for playrooms or children's nooks,needed even in families with low incomes. Cultivation of individual hobbies among children from families of the poor might become an important form of insurance against social delinquency.

New Soles on Old Shoes. Old shoe collections brought in also every variety of style, from men's hightop work boots to ladies' ball slippers. Many were fully worn, but many, too, had good uppers and needed only a half-sole to be suitable for wear. Most of the collection in Ogden were ordinary low-cut business man's shoes, and when half-soled and the heel built up a bit, with a good polishing, were ready to wear to the office or to school without danger of detection that they came from the storehouse.

The man who served as guide explained that a few shoes needed only polishing, that most needed only halfsoles, and that some were too worn to be mended readily, but served to furnish pieces of leather for mending others, and for miscellaneous repairs, including furniture.

Heavy work shoes were kept in repair at all times, the guide said, and about twenty pairs ready to wear were seen, three pairs of hightops and the others in various "makes," but all suitable for either indoor heavy work or field work. As the visit was made at the height

of the fruit and vegetable canning season, all hands were at that task, with shelf after shelf full of shoes waiting to be done when space, man power, and supervision could be released for cobbling. This work would be done under the direction of experienced tradesmen, most of whom had worked for years at the trade, but many of whom were nearly idle owing to cheap factory manufacture.

A Furniture Hospital. Old and broken furniture, but with the basic parts strong and in good condition, filled two entire stories of one wing of the storehouse. 

There were chairs from the plain wooden kitchen variety to upholstered arm chairs, children's high chairs, and several really good diningroom pieces with loose legs. A number of substantial dining tables with a broken board in the top was seen as well as some small tables slightly injured. One could see beds, and springs awaiting repair by a metal worker or a carpenter. Perhaps forty mattresses were waiting to be "built up." 

Four or five stoves, good coal ranges, that had been replaced in homes by electric or gas cookers, stood in an alcove. They needed a piece of grate, a lid, an adjusted damper and some stove bolts to make them ready for some needy home. One missed buckets and cooking utensils that were to be soldered and given other minor repairs. An inquiry brought out that "We haven't got to that yet, but it may come any time."

Nails and Paint. Buildings and fences, as well as clothes and furniture, become worn or injured. Paint weathers and scales off or is rubbed by bumps from hard objects. Nails pull out or a board comes loose and partly splinters away. Nothing detracts more from the appearance or increases the feeling of neglect so

much as dilapidated buildings and fences. When these minor defects are attended to at once it is no great chore to keep the farmstead and homestead looking neat, Loose boards should be nailed firmly, and broken or splintered pieces replaced. Loosened hinges allow doors and gates to sag, become hard to open and shut, and pull rapidly to pieces when out of position. A few screws and nails properly used work wonders. 

Paint, too, is a real wonder-worker. Nothing about the home, outbuildings and yard pays such handsome dividends in improving appearance as a little paint properly chosen and well applied. An otherwise good home with the paint weathered off, with several loose boards, and with the screen doors sagged out of position, presents a sorry sight outside. If the paper is rubbed and dirty, and if the moldboards and window sills are scuffed, the inside is likewise anything but attractive. Let a good workman repair the defects, paint the outside and paper the inside, and it is worth twice as much as a rent or sale prospect. 

About the farm, gates and bridges have heavier wear and show the effects more quickly. Not only do broken planks look bad, but they are dangerous to man and beast. Abandoned farm machinery, or that in current use, is too often in full view, standing where frightened or playing animals may get a bad wound. 

Good machines ought to be under cover and abandoned ones so disposed of that they are not a hazard. Pieces of metal also endanger automobile tires. Besides, scrap iron often has some sale value.

Oiling gears and joints of machinery adds greatly to their life, appearance, and effectiveness when used. 

Linseed oil is also a great preserver of wagon felloes and all wooden parts subject to alternate wetting and drying and to severe exposure to weather.

Cracks in cement walks, foundations or other structures are easily repaired if the edges are not allowed to weather and crumble before pouring in a little soft mortar of sand and cement. Leaky troughs, barrels, and buckets are both a great nuisance and unsanitary. 

Harnesses with the sewing broken and buckles off, or loose, deserve immediate attention. Small tools such as hammers, wrenches, pliers, etc., each in its proper place, are much easier to find when needed, and less likely to be broken.

Revamping Old Meeting-Houses. Church build ings need repairs and paint at intervals to keep them attractive. Well-chosen interior decorations add to the spirit of worship and do much to encourage people to come again. Many old church edifices are good buildings in their basic structure and plans, and need only minor repairs, paint and interior decorating. For example, members of the Lyndyl ward remodeled an old hall, built an addition of railroad ties and stuccoed the whole, making an attractive chapel with but little cost in money. Except as a temporary makeshift it is not wisdom, however, to spend much on a building that cannot be made satisfactory. Money saved for a new building is often a much more satisfactory answer. 

Ever so many churches and public buildings would be much improved by a few trees or shrubs to make the surroundings attractive. Under favorable circumstances some lawn and flowers add greatly to the attractiveness, but even when it is not wise to attempt lawn and flowers, trees and shrubs are well worthwhile in almost any circumstances that are likely to be found.

Street trees, and home trees, too, respond to a little careful pruning and irrigating. If all stubs are

removed by being sawed off close to the trunk or large branch, the tree soon heals and looks well kept. Dead stubs and dry crooked branches damage both the life of the tree and its beauty.

Repairing Men. Ponce de Leon discovered Flo rida while he was looking for a fabled fountain, bathing in which would keep a man young. That magic fountain has not been found, but we know many ways to keep men and women young in spirit and outlook. 

Happiness in work and in the home, pleasant social relations, plenty of nutritious food, proper exercise, and a happy smile are powerful rejuvenators.

The real problem is to rebuild discouraged, unemployed men and restore them to faith in themselves. 

The Church Welfare Program has before it a great work in helping needy people to get food and clothing, but a still more important task in teaching men and women how to work for themselves, and to become fitted back into the social group of which they are a part. 

Weak and failing souls usually are restored if they can be made to succeed in their efforts to do a few things satisfactorily. Help to accomplish this and to restore their courage as well as their income is indeed God's work.

1. Explain the part taken by welfare industries in public charity. 

2. In what ways does the Four-Stake Storehouse at Ogden illustrate the operation of the Church Welfare Plan? 

3. Show what it is doing with "old" clothes; with shoes; with furniture; with quilts. 

4. How is instruction in the art of making over old things made to reach the wards? The needy families? 

5. How can Melchizedek quorums be organized to do part of this work in welfare projects? What indirect benefit arises to quorums from doing such work? Illustrate.

6. To what extent may Church buildings be made over? Give an example. 

7. Build up a list of projects not yet in use that are suitable to your ward.

Houghton, Mifflin Company, 1924.* 

2. Goodsell, The Family as a Social and Educational Institu tion, Macmillan Company, 1926.* 

3. Parmelee, Poverty and Social Progress, Macmillan Company, 1926.* 

4. Richmond, Social Diagnosis, Russel Sage Foundation, 1917.*

Chapter 26 

: THE VALUE OF CLEAN LIVING 

Uses of Human Energy. Man has at his disposal only three resources that contribute to his physical wellbeing. The first two of these, (1) material resources, and (2) the use of intelligence to obtain power over these material resources, have already received attention in the preceding lessons. The third resource is human energy, and is, in the final analysis, the only one over which man has complete control. Land, water, forests, minerals, and even organizing ability may be beyond his reach, but human energy is at his command. 

With the energy that one individual has he can build himself a great life, acquire an education, and under favorable circumstances, earn a good living for himself and family. During periods of normal financial conditions, industry is active and human labor is required to produce food and clothing, to haul it to market or deliver it to the consumer direct; to cut logs and make lumber; to mine the oil and coal; to manufacture the many articles we need or desire; to work in offices, stores, banks, dentist, medical, and law offices; and to teach in the schools.

Every one of these tasks, and many others not mentioned, is performed wholly or in part by human energy. Some of the tasks are done partly by machines, but others largely without machines or with machines that require human attention or physical strength to keep them operating properly. It is by human energy, including mental energy, that all of the

world's work is made possible, as machines cannot operate themselves. Moreover, a large part of the value in machines is made up of human energy previously expended in making the machines. 

Waste of Human Energy. On the brink of Niagara, just before the stream leaps the falls, the water is thirty feet deep, more than a mile wide, and racing with mighty force. Notwithstanding this immense volume and mighty force, moss and lichens can be seen plainly on the rock surface of the stream bed. In our mountain streams the stones in a small stream are scoured free of all growth by the sand particles carried in the stream. 

All the sand, and even the clay particles have settled in Lake Erie from which Niagara river draws its water. 

Because water without sand can do nothing in the way of scouring its bed, the moss and lichens thrive undisturbed. So far as scouring away the moss is concerned, the vast energy of Niagara is useless-utterly wasted. Scouring moss from the surface of stones is not a very high purpose, but even if it were, it would still be unaccomplished by Niagara's overwhelming power.

The total volume of human energy wasted each day of our lives would turn all the wheels that Niagara sets in motion. This wasted human energy, however, differs from Niagara in that the human energy is wasted in small amounts by each of millions of individuals, a few minutes here, an hour there, and the whole day by some. Losses of energy owing to idleness for long or short periods, by few or many who should be busy, add up to a tremendous volume, even in a small town. 

Ignorance of how to use energy most efficiently, or as to what are wrong or right uses, also make up a vast waste. Fully as much to be regretted is the waste 15

that arises from ill health, already discussed, and from such vices as drunkenness and dissipation. Besides these direct wastes, thousands of people misuse their energy by working hard to do things that are not beneficial, or which, in some cases, are positively harmful. 

Such indirect wastes, as we shall see, are also astoundingly large.

Strong and Weak Points of Machines. Machines save greatly in human labor. Some, such as derricks, handle loads too heavy for man; some, such as printing presses, perform routine tasks rapidly and with precision; others, such as typewriters, replace long-hand writing and make the script easier to read and also provide duplicate copies; and still others, such as automobiles, make travel easy or carry heavy loads. Telephones, vacuum sweepers, lawn mowers, road graders, steam shovels, grain binders make the task easier or perform the work that it would require many men to do with their hands. If we had no machines our lives would be poor in material things. Every man in America has many mechanical slaves helping him. 

On the other hand, in our civilization, not as yet fully balanced, machines may in periods of depression stand in the way of men's finding employment. A road grader does the work it would take 20, 50, or 100 men to do; or a cement mixer, that of 15 or 20 men. 

Often the machine does the work more thoroughly, as in the case of mixing sand, gravel, and cement for concrete. Most articles, however, produced in vast number by machinery are cheap, inartistic, and short-lived or shoddy. The lasting qualities, the substantialness and the individual beauty of professional handicraftmanship have almost disappeared, and with them many of the enjoyable and skillful occupations. Machines,

too, have been agents in piling up surplus goods that accentuate unemployment in depression periods. 

Idleness or Waste. Many men are idle today, at least partly so. Such men belong to either of two classes: (1) the unemployed or (2) the leisure group.

A small amount of unemployment is natural, but when it increases to many times this normal amount, as it has done during the last several years, good, capable people suffer, and the valuable goods they might produce are not provided, causing everybody to suffer indirectly. One of the worst results of unemployment is that it mentally disturbs the unemployed. Some worry so much they are in an unfit condition to do good work, some lose heart, and others slump into a condition of not wishing to do work. Unemployment loses for society, therefore, both the things that could have been produced and also much of the ability to produce effectively.

An equally serious form of waste arises when capable people are idle from choice. The hours of labor in some occupations are shorter, affording more leisure. Some have incomes without working, some acquire early in life enough savings to provide a living, some get pensions, and a few live by appropriating the property of others. All these are great wastes. 

Those persons having short hours of labor, leisure lives, and incomes on which to retire, are often the more valuable producers were they kept in the harness. 

When they merely stop working, they not only withdraw from society their ability and help, but they soon lose the ability they had. When, on the other hand, they do necessary public service in helping to organize welfare movements, or produce things of beauty in picture, song, literature or landscaping, they perform

valuable services that may not otherwise be provided. 

These members of the leisure class, because they are often talented persons, might also be of great assistance in the making of plans for projects whose purpose is to afford work for the unemployed. 

Ignorance, as a Source of Waste. Doing things in a poor way, or in such a way as to produce lowquality products, is a highly wasteful process, that arises from not understanding how to produce better goods or how to produce them more effectively. Lack of reasonable skill, lack of knowledge as how to organize one's efforts, and lack of knowledge as to the general relationship of his vocation to community life, are wasteful forms of ignorance, that no man can afford to tolerate in himself or in his children. There are available, too, many means of learning such things to justify an upstanding individual's long remaining unskilled or ignorant.

Society also tolerates waste by permitting some persons to destroy confidence of the group in business dealings or in the safety of persons, property, and intellectual achievement. These needless but great wastes must be prevented by more seriously protecting girls, women, and even men on the streets at night, by reducing kidnaping and burglary, and by providing that the honor of accomplishment shall come to the one who does something worth while. No waste is more deadly in its effect than to have another person by force or indirection plunder the rightful owner, either of property or honor. Confidence is absolutely necessary to intelligent living.

One means of overcoming waste is to have standardized goods and a dependable conduct of business affairs. Every intelligent business man recognizes the

urgent need of carrying goods reliable as to quality and size, and of inspiring confidence in his customers that they will be treated fairly. The individual on his part must prepare himself by training and study to inspire equal confidence in his ability to produce reliable goods and in his reliability to deal fairly. Defects in the goods a man produces, or shiftiness in his dealings, greatly lessens his value as a workman and as a citizen. 

Vice Causes Waste. Personal dissipation, whether resulting from the use of liquor and drugs, or arising from personal uncleanliness or licentiousness, is vice. 

They waste not only the time of the individual that ought to be used in producing something valuable, but they destroy the individual himself. To avoid this waste is man's first duty to himself. The Word of Wisdom is man's only safe guide to strength and security. 

If one had to ride on a bus over a mountain road at night, and if he knew the driver to be under the influence of alcohol, he would have great cause for alarm. 

The vice of drink not only removes a person from active production and injures him fundamentally, but it may endanger the lives, persons, or property of his neighbors or asociates. It is a vile form of waste, and because it is habit-forming tends, like the octopus, to tighten its tentacles around the man and drag him from dissipation into degradation.

Exploitation vs. Production. Another waster of human energy is the man who merely exploits for his individual gain the natural resources or the labors of other men. Merely mining coal or catching oil as it gushes forth is not a productive occupation. The mining and the oil industries must be so conducted as to save the small and poor deposits while appropriating the large ones, and to do this in an orderly fashion with

an eye on the volume that is required to satisfy the needs of the day. Over-rapid mining is waste, in that it reduces the price of the commodity and makes it unprofitable to work the scattered and lowgrade deposits. 

The spirit of exploitation prevails not only in the mining industry but in the other walks of life. It may appear even in a Sunday School, which may be conducted as advertising mediums for the superintendent instead of to provide for young people real spiritual education and practice in church ordinances.

Only the producer of substantial physical, intellectual, or spiritual goods is performing real service. 

It is only the effective producer of values that is conserving human energy. The mere exploiter bears the same relation to the producer that tinsel bears to gold.

Much that glitters has only the surface luster. Substantialness is an essential part of achievement. If it is polished, so much the better, but shininess by itself may have no depth. The contrary occurs so often, that glitter nearly always suggests that an examination is necessary to verify high quality. 

Helping to Provide Employment. The urgent need of more or less regular employment, not only to provide a means of livelihood for the worker and his family, but also to keep him from deteriorating into a person unfit for employment, makes any program of providing employment doubly, or even trebly, important. 

Idleness works havoc with a man's moral fiber. He weakens under it, and may even decay. 

The Church Welfare Program, therefore, deserves active support. Passive acceptance is not enough. We must support it at all angles; it is vital to strong living and enriched lives. It is Mormondom's best antidote for a flabby existence. It can, at its best, lead to a so-

ciety of strong, independent men and women who may achieve much of their salvation on this earth. 

Removal of Waste and Ignorance. The elimina tion of waste and the removal of ignorance can be brought about only by a thorough-going program of education on the part of the individual. Schools should be used always by the young, and sometimes by the matured and the aged. Schools, however, only begin a man's education. Every person should study his profession for a time each day. Also, a little time should be devoted to current events and general reading, in addition to the study of Church doctrines and Priesthood lessons. No person need remain uneducated, though many cannot in their mature years attend schools. 

Value of Clean Living. Clean, vigorous living is the only kind that makes a man master of his life. Health must be protected in the manner previously indicated; human energy must be conserved by study, by organization, and by a program to aid the needy in times of stress; vice and personal uncleanliness must be weeded out with relentless zeal. Alcohol and other narcotics weaken the body and degrade the mind. As Dr. David 

Starr Jordan, a great student of young men and one of the world's great scientists, when president of Stanford 

University, said in "The Strength of Being Clean:"

All that a drug can do, for the most part, is to change the stress in the process of life. It can create nothing. It cannot bring health. (p. 40.)

The race down cocktail route helps to forget. Suddenly a man gets sight of himself. He catches his face in the glass. He sees himself as others see him. Instead of "the jolly good fellow which nobody can deny," he gets the glimpse of a useless, helpless sot. 

He sees a man who has spent his substance, has disgraced his name, has ruined his home, has broken the heart of his wife, has beggared his children, has lost the respect of others, and the respect of himself. (pp. 35-36.)

A man ought to be stronger than anything that can happen to him. He is the strong man who can say NO. He is the wise man who, for all his life, can keep his mind and soul and body clean. (pp. 44-45.)

One would think Dr. Jordan was a Mormon, but he was not. He was an honest, straight-forward, deepthinking scientist, who knew what life means. 

Latter-day Saints have long recognized in the Word of Wisdom-last year so profitably studied in the Priesthood lessons-the urgent necessity of abstaining from the use of alcohol, tobacco, and other drugs, and such stimulants as are found in tea and coffee. Some of these substances have great effect on the human body, some more than others; but all are harmful to it, and in the degree that they are harmful they decrease our available supply of human energy, sometimes by reducing the amount of work done, sometimes by impairing the quality of the work, and sometimes by making it spasmodic and uncertain as to time of completion.

Always we must remember the injunction of the 

Lord, that only by clean, vigorous living can we “run and not be weary, walk and not faint."

1. In what ways does human energy enter into the tasks of the world? Compare mental and physical energy as to effectiveness. 

2. Name the ways in which human energy is wasted? Give the strong and weak points of machines. 

3. Name the two classes of idle people. Is all idleness waste? 

In what ways is ignorance waste? What is the part of standardization in preventing waste? 

4. What is meant by vice? Why is it a source of waste?

What is the mission of the Church Welfare Plan in overcoming vice and idleness? Compare production and exploitation. 

5. How should idleness be grappled with? How may flabby living be overcome? What is the part of education in solving this problem?

6. What is the value of clean living in making better lives? 

In furthering the Welfare Plan?

3. Widtsoe and Widtsoe, Word of Wisdom, Deseret News, 1937.

Chapter 27 

The most precious possession of man is his life, and a great mission that gives it purpose. The purpose of life will be more likely to be fulfilled, and the life itself will be infinitely more pleasant, if the man is blessed with vigorous health. How to preserve life and make the most of it is a major study in the health resources of man. 

Duration and Value of Life. At birth, the expectation of life is 23 years in India; 45 to 48 in France, Germany and Italy; 51 in England; 58 in the United 

States; and 65 in New Zealand. In 1880, the span of life in America averaged 40 years; in 1901 it had increased to 49 years; and in 1925 it had reached 58 years. These are remarkable facts, and even more remarkable is it that the length of life in America is still being extended. Between 1880 and 1925, American people have increased by 18 years the average time that man has on the earth. If productive life is assumed to begin at 20, man has almost double as many years in which to do his work as he had a generation ago. 

Life insurance is based on the figures as to how long people live, as a group. A careful study by the 

Metropolitan Life Insurance Company not only revealed the increasing length of life, but showed that it cost about $10,000 to bring up a child to maturity, or to the age of self-support. In the middle class of 

Americans, a child at birth was found to constitute a capitalized value of $9,300; $29,000 at the age of 18 years; $32,000 at 25 years; and $8,500 at 60 years.

These are based on the average earning power and duration of future productive life. The money value of human life in America is by all odds its most valuable resource, amounting to 1500 billion dollars, whereas the value of all material assets in 1922 was 

321 billions.

In money value, therefore, the life of the American people is about five times as great as all the property in the country. The money value of life, however, is only one aspect of life as a resource. Joy of living, a vigorous strong emotional life, is a new and different kind of asset that has untold values in addition to money-value earning power. The prospect of eternal life by following the principles of the Gospel, presents to man a view of life values not even thought of by the purely biological study of the insurance investigators. 

Value of Health. As with life, health has two sets of values: (1) earning power and (2) enjoyment values.

The same insurance study brought out that 

America spends every year about two billion dollars to overcome sickness. Health then is valued at much more than this, because the cost of time lost has to be added to the medical bill. The money value of health as represented in the earning power of workers, were they not sick, must amount to several billion dollars a year,—an enormous figure.

Health resembles life in another respect, namely, that its greatest value cannot be expressed in dollars, but in human satisfaction,-the joy of feeling good, of doing good work, of enjoying social life, and of being able to live a fuller life than ill-health would permit.

Moreover, health is an asset in that it is the leading factor in extending life itself. The increasing length

of life already noted is the result largely of preventing infection at birth and infancy, in avoiding and curing childhood and adult diseases quickly and effectively, and in staving off the on-coming effects of old age. 

Costs of Illness. The costs of illness on mankind are many and varied. The money costs just cited express only one aspect of the great price exacted by illness. Quality of work done suffers as much or more than quantity. It is impossible really to do one's best when the workman is in pain, is bilious, has fever or is deadened by the toxins of disease. When the work requires an artistic touch to complete it effectively, few are workmen with ills who can rise to the task. 

Leisure time-time after work hours are over-is the part of life when reading is done, when musical, literary, or artistic talents are cultivated. It is the time inventions are thought out, when new ideas are evolved, when the philosophy of living is formulated, when Church and social work is done, and finally when the mind is tuned to receive the message of inspiration from on high.

Ill health has been shown to be one great cause of man's becoming a misfit in society. Friction and jars in the scurry of life are hard enough to bear when there is no contributing internal force, but the difficulties are much increased by low resistance or lack of vigor that result from illness. Short temper is also another cost of illness, and it is a great cost. 

Conquest of Disease. The resources available for conquering diseases consist in part of scientific method and in part of rigorous sanitary procedure. Vaccination and serum inoculation have almost wiped out smallpox and diphtheria, and together with sanitary water and food supplies have removed typhoid from the list of

prevalent diseases. Fighting yellow and malaria fevers by destroying the haunts of the germ-bearing mosquito is a recent form of conquest. Other examples of the need to combat the intermediate hosts of dreaded disease germs are the spotted fever carried by woodticks, tuleremia carried by rabbit ticks, and bubonic plague carried by the body louse of rats. These require cleanliness of a new sort. 

Careful handling of foods, and inspection of slaughter-houses and meat packing establishments is one great achievement of sanitary science, as is also providing clean, pure water, with which the Intermountain country is blessed. Use of only safe food preservatives for meats and other perishable products has made great strides within the present generation. 

Botulinus poisoning has been greatly reduced by proper choice of product and correct procedure in canning. 

Metal poisoning has also been largely eliminated. 

Appendicitis, now rarely a serious operation, was once very dangerous, and still earlier not possible to treat. Dentistry now preserves teeth long beyond their former length of usefulness, and when necessary provides good artificial ones. Spectacles permit almost half the population to do more and better work and to make auto travel safer by an untold degree. The dull of hearing are also made more efficient and kept in social contact with society much longer. During the 

World War, deaths from previous scourges such as typhoid and infected wounds were greatly lessened. 

Disinfection and aseptic surgery are great assets. 

Safeguarding Mental Health. The World War also made clear that nervous disorders increased under such stresses as noise, turmoil, violent explosions, and the undertaking of personally dangerous sallies. Shell

shock, at first thought to be physical, was found to occur among those who never reached the shelling zones of battle. It was a nervous disorder resulting from the undue strain. Great cities produce similar effects, expressed in a hundred of different ways by the overwrought nervous system. High-speed machinery and the high-speed feeding of them have taken their toll by causing mental illness. Peculiar associations and reactions to strains of almost any sort endanger the mental health of man.

The statement of contributing causes of mental disorder suggest the need for physical quiet and the lessening of strain of all sorts. Machinery that operates with minimum grind, reduction of noise, zoning a city so as to put factories in the outskirts of town, working at healthful rates of speed, and providing short respites from taxing operations, are all means of offsetting, in part at least, the din and strain of industrial life. Man's ability to correct such abnormal conditions by planning, or at least by regulation, is a health resource. Even more valuable as a health resource is the stabilizing will of man which enables him, in a large measure, to reduce the effects of these outward stresses by holding under control his temperamental reactions toward them.

Facing Life Squarely. Man's unconquerable will is, however, his surest resource to mental health. He can in a large measure provide for his own stability by living one day at a time so as not to cross imaginary bridges. He can compel himself to make haste slowly, to avoid self-pity, regulate his impulses, and provide for relaxation during off-work hours. He can, if he wills it with sufficient strength, realize that his lot is not by any means so bad as that of hundreds of others. 

When we look at others, unless we face the issue

squarely, we see only the outward aspect and overlook entirely the inner struggles.

Man can cultivate in himself the power to look with amusement on some of his own troubles. A good laugh is a strong antidote for mental friction. After all, people are too prepossessed with their own affairs to think long or hard about those of another. If this fact is kept in mind, many a disappointed man will see a faint glimmer of humor, and less stark malice in the world. It is good to remember that oneself is the center only of his own universe. This will greatly lessen the natural impression that every man is plotting against him. Suspicion of the motives of others may thus be largely overcome. Self-control is a vast resource for safeguarding mental health.

The Twin Imps-Hurry and Worry. In man's weak moments, two imps sit at his elbow and urge him to commit this folly or that. Hurry, the first imp, tempts him to make short-cuts, to rush in without preparation, to try to pluck the fruit without waiting for it to ripen. Now it is a law of life that all good things require time to grow and mature. Nothing worth having in life lies on the bargain counter-the best things require that the full price be paid both in effort and in time. A flower torn out of its bud never blooms—it shrivels and either dies or is deformed and dwarfed. 

Moreover, hurry keeps men keyed up, rushing madly this way or that, or excitedly preparing to rush the other way. Hurry also keeps one at nervous tension, too taut and tired for good digestion, cool thinking, normal rest, or collected appraisal. Hurry is the father of dyspepsia, debility and dazed mentality.

The other imp is worry. It besets one at every turn, if given the slightest encouragement. Worry is

It a habit extremely difficult to break or escape. It predicts threatening events that never happen, embarrassments that never arise, and failures that do not occur. weakens the man and prevents him from doing his best at the very time he most needs all his strength and alertness. It causes sleeplessness, when, above all, rest is needed. It saps nervous energy and is "the most popular form of suicide."

Both hurry and worry are prevalent American habits that gnaw at the very foundation of man. Nature, however, is very un-American. Life-good lifegoes slowly. It requires building, block by block, sunshine and rain, heat and cold, dark and light, winter and summer, work and play, labor and thought. These are the resources of health which hurry and worry would shortcut or undermine. Physical work followed by food and sleep, sunshine and heat followed by darkness and coolness, work to create an appetite and make man tired enough to sleep,-these are nature's restoratives. Blessed is the man who uses them, because in their presence hurry and worry soon lose heart and leave him alone. The normal simple life is the road to a healthy body and a strong mind, and without by-paths leads directly to security of health.

The Word of Wisdom. Stimulants work on man as do hurry and worry. They stir him up to unwonted temporary speed and effort and allow a slump to set in. Drugs in tea, coffee, coca-cola, tobacco, and alcohol flatter the nerves that they may stupify them more gracefully a short time later. Just as man must work, eat good food, and sleep to avoid being duped by worry and hurry, so must he also do in order to escape the traitorous stab of drugs and alcohol. They are not to be trusted. They knife one in the back-deeply and often fatally.

Against these enemies, man has at his command two defenses, strong armors against which they stab in vain. These defenses are honest hunger and good food on the one hand, and a naturally healthful will to travel nature's road on the other. Against these two defenses, enemies such as hurry and worry, drugs and alcohol battle in vain. Honest living and a strong will can hold the fortress so long as they are active. Clean living resembles riding a bicycle in one respect. It requires constant progress at a rate fast enough to keep it from wobbling. When either a bicycle or a man wobble they shortly fall by the wayside.

The Word of Wisdom reveals another health resource, namely, that man should eat of "every herb in the season thereof and every fruit in the season thereof; all these to be used with prudence and thanksgiving" (Doctrine and Covenants 89:11). Intermountain soils rich in lime, phosphates, and other plant nutrients make for strong bone, rich blood, and healthy nerves. Bright sunshine, fertile soil, and a wide variety of natural products provide foods which are rich in vitamins and therefore health-giving. In whatever way we look at our home in the mountains, the rich resources with which the Creator has endowed it are clearly evident. 

These resources come to fruition not only in timber, water, crops, livestock, wildlife and recreational opportunities, but in abundant good health, brightened with the luster of clean living. "Even Hidden Treasures." Nutrition with minerals, vitamins, and proteins, besides the energy foods; fresh air, work, hunger and sleep; calmness, relaxation, and variety of activity; privacy without isolation, and social living, without loss of individuality,—these constitute the army of real health that will drive away 16

disease and mental illness, throw out hurry and worry, and conquer drugs and stimulants. When this is done, then in reality shall "all Saints who remember to keep and do these sayings, walking in obedience to the commandments, shall receive health in their navel and marrow to their bones, and shall find wisdom and great treasures of knowledge, even hidden treasures; and shall run and not be weary, and walk and not faint" (Doctrine and Covenants 89:18-20).

It is a great treasure to be able to run vigorously the race of life and not be weary with the constant effort, but it is a greater treasure, even a hidden treasure, to walk day after day down the path of rectitude and temperance, walking and ever walking among the thorns and temptations and not faint from the endless tugging of doubts and want. The good Latter-day

Saint will walk this road to the end, and arrive at his goal secure in body and soul.

1. What is meant by average length of life? How much has it increased? What has brought about the increase? 

2. Name the various costs of illness? Why does illness contribute to low quality in work? Which vocations suffer most in this respect? 

3. What diseases are almost conquered? State the means by which the conquest of each has been won. 

4. What conditions contribute to mental health? Why must we face life squarely? What are the parts of fear and suspicion in mental illness? Of hurry and worry? 

5. What is the part of work, rest, food, sunshine, and faith in promoting mental health? 

6. Why are drugs and alcohol not to be trusted? In what way does clean living resemble riding a bicycle? 

7. Explain the passage "run and not be weary, walk and not faint" (D. & C. 89:20). How does this become part of the Welfare Plan?

Chapter 28 

Man's first responsibility is to earn a living for himself and his family. The activity in which he engages as a means of doing this is spoken of as his vocation, that is, his occupation. One man is a farmer, another a merchant, a third a truck driver, and still others office workers, salesmen, accountants, mechanics or laborers. Unfortunately, some heads of families have no regular occupation, but do from week to week whatever they can find to do.

Earning a Living. As has been pointed out in previous lessons, a man must work at some vocation intelligently and vigorously enough to receive an income either as wages or profit that is great enough and regular enough to provide a living for himself and his dependents. It must not be overlooked that in order to be entitled to receive an income, a man's work must be productive. If operating his own business, a man has expenses of housing; of maintaining his plant, whatever its nature; of raw materials; of hired labor, if he has any; of taxes; of capital or interest, and of marketing his product. To keep his business operating successfully, the operator of the business has to produce goods enough to pay these expenses and to pay him the income required for a living. Most businesses also find it necessary to set aside a reasonable amount for replacing machinery, buildings, vehicles, that are used up in producing or selling his product. When this is not done the man finally finds himself without the

required equipment for successfully prosecuting his business.

The wage earner, on the other hand, does not take the risk and responsibility of providing buildings, machines, or raw material. He provides the human energy, the labor, whether it is hand labor or intellectual labor, with which to carry on the activities by which goods are produced. The owner pays the laborer his wages, or the professional man his salary, as the case may be. 

The funds from which the wages or salary is drawn result from the returns of the business. Unless the laborer is productive, there is nothing from which to pay him. A man, therefore, must earn enough to pay the natural expenses of his work, to provide his wages (or salary) and to allow a reasonable profit to the business. In those vocations in which a man performs only a small part of the operation, as for example an accountant, and other people perform other parts of the operation, the group as a whole must be productive enough to earn the income received, together with the expenses and the reasonable profit.

Usually a given man works in only one occupation, or at most two, at a time. His neighbors either have the same vocation, or one of many others. When all the people in a town are considered, as many as 20 to 30 vocations are followed. In a county there may be only a few more, or many more, depending on whether it is industrially a uniform or a diversified region. In a whole state, and certainly in a whole nation, the number of vocations in which some man or other works for a livelihood, counts into the thousands. 

The Many Vocations of Man. "The Book of 

Opportunities," written by R. H. Platt, and published in 1933 by G. P. Putman, bears as a sub-title, “A Dic-

tionary of Jobs-Personal Sidelights on 3,500 American Occupations." Offhand, this is a large number of vocations, and of course far more than any one person ever has contact with. The list, however, does present to us an idea that there are many different vocations being worked at to provide income for man. Not all of them, but many of them, are available in which a given man may choose to engage. 

Mr. Platt divides these 3,500 vocations, or as he calls them "opportunities for earning a living," into six major groups, as follows: (1) industry, (2) transportations, (3) business, (4) the professions, (5) personal service and (6) public service. Under each of these groups comes a number of sub-headings, such as, in the case of "Industry," A. Fishing, B. Trapping, C. Logging, D. Farming, E. Horticulture, F. Mining, G. 

Quarrying, H. Oil-drilling, I. Forestry, and J. Manufacture, under which are listed 25 separate activities from lumbering to baking, and home industry. Under farming, to follow another example, are 31 actual vocations, including: 

1. General farmer 

2. Dairy farmer 

3. Dairy laborer 

4. Corn grower 

5. Cotton planter 

6. Wheat grower 

7. Potato farmer 

8. Sugar-beet grower 

9. Sugar cane planter 

10. Tobacco planter 

11. Truck farmer 

12. Market gardener 

13. Cowboy 

14. Cattle ranchman 

15. Cattle farmer 

16. Sheep ranchman 

17. Sheep farmer 

18. Hog raiser 

19. Poultry keeper 

20. Chick culturist 

21. Frog farmer 

22. Apiarist 

23. Rat culturist 

24. Goat keeper 

25. Rattlesnake dairyman 

26. Fancier 

27. Sugar tapper 

28. Lion farmer 

29. Farm foreman 

30. Farm superintendent 

31. Farm manager

And he has failed to mention horse growers, hay farmer, and alfalfa feed grower.

Most of these are well-known vocations, but a few need a word of explanation. A rat culturist grows pet rats or ordinary rats for laboratory experiments. The rattlesnake dairyman "milks" the poison twice a month from the reptiles and sells it to be used in making serum that is used in treating rattlesnake bites. A sugar tapper catches maple sap in spring for maple sugar making. The lion farmer at El Monte, California, grows lions to sell to "movies" and zoos and circuses. 

Cubs sell at $500, and full-grown adults at $1200 to $1500.

This book also catalogs each subdivision, as has been illustrated, with farming as one form of industry. 

This, however, is sufficient to bring out clearly the immense number of activities by which man works to earn his livelihood.

This Is An Age of Specialization. One reason why so many vocations are followed by mankind, is that it has been found more productive for a man to make only one or two parts of an automobile, for example, than attempt to make all of them. This permits the development of great skill in that one operation, and probably permits a greater use of machinery in doing the work. Instead of there being only one or two jobs (vocations) in automobile manufacturing, there are dozens and dozens of them. Factory work is based on the fact that a man specialized in doing just one thing, and does that one thing faster, and, in many cases, better, than if he attempted to do many things. as the blacksmith did a generation ago.

Specializing on one or two tasks also permits the workman to study all about his job and to learn

it from beginning to end. Those workmen who have an inventive turn of mind are also encouraged to improve the machines they use, or even to invent entirely new ones. Methods of doing things also need study and improvement. A close study of what is done, and the motions gone through in doing that task, very often reveals that there is a better, a quicker or a more economical manner of doing the job. On the other hand, when a man works only on one small phase of a job, it makes him unsuited for doing anything else and prevents him from learning how to perform the whole task or make a whole tool. Whatever be the advantages or disadvantages of specialization, it is the way things are now done, and man has to adjust himself to that way of doing things. Because of this the number of vocations will probably increase rather than decrease. 

Man's Fitness For His Vocation. How well a man is fitted for the particular occupation he follows determines largely how much he will be worth in the long run. Some men and their jobs seem made for each other, whereas other men do their work poorly, or at least with such poor grace as to present every appearance of being misfits. Such men are likely to be unhappy in their work as well as rather ineffective. 

A man's fitness depends on (1) whether he has natural ability in the work he is doing; (2) whether he is well trained for the work; and (3) whether he has had enough of the right kind of experience both to be proficient and to be able to make the judgments that have been made. Some jobs require little skill, need only a strong back and give little chance of mental growth. These are the unskilled labor jobs. On the other hand, some vocations require so much skill and

experience as to make it unlikely that more than a few young men ever become really efficient. Most jobs require considerable skill and experience, but not more than can be acquired in a few years, and some in only a few months.

Nearly all the professional vocations, however, require several years of school training and several additional years of experience, and becoming acquainted with the right people, before they are thoroughly fitted to meet the problems with which they must cope. 

In this class of vocation come such things as medicine, law, architecture, engineering, writing, ministry, all phases of science, and many others. Outside of these learned professions, much of the necessary training is often done in the evenings and at odd times during the period of employment. Few people can succeed in a real way unless they study "on the job." Years of schooling does not relieve the doctor, lawyer, or teacher from continuing to study during all the years of his active service. Rising in one's profession or even holding his own requires many hours of hard work outside regular hours.

Square Pegs in Round Holes. Many men, however, lose out in their chosen vocations. Some are misfits, and others are merely unprepared to do satisfactory work. It has just been noted that some professions required years of training followed by constant study. Other vocations require only an alert willing worker and reasonable study on the job. Almost no one can keep up his efficiency in a skilled occupation unless he constantly keeps abreast of new developments, either by studying trade books, reading trade journals, taking evening extension courses, or visiting other places or workmen devoted to the same sort of

work. Whatever the job requires, that a man must do, or he lags behind in the race for effectiveness and in the end loses out. The middle-aged man who can no longer do satisfactory work in his vocation is a sad case. The real remedy is to prevent this from happening by keeping abreast of new developments and acquiring new skill when that is required. 

Sad, too, is the man who is working in an occupation for which he is low in natural ability. Some unsuccessful farmers ought to be mechanics, or salesmen, or builders, or just day laborers. Often a firstclass mechanic is happy in actual work and succeeds both financially and temperamentally. Occasionally, such a man may be promoted to a foremanship. Ordinarily, he will succeed as his employers chose him because he has the other needed qualities, but some such men are not suited to handling men and organizing a crew of workmen. A first-class mechanic may thus be a fourth-rate "boss." Some men have also drifted into law or teaching or mechanical work when they have little natural inclination for their work. High pay at the moment often entices men into vocations for which they are poorly suited. Square pegs in round holes constitute a security problem, and because they are unadjusted such men are usually mentally disturbed. 

When Employment Ceases. When employment ends for a man who depends on employment as a means of earning a livelihood, he is a problem to himself, to his family, and to the social group of which he is a member. If he is a misfit, or if he has lost out because of lagging behind, he may have to be supported by others, at least in part. Too frequently, these men are mentally unfit for competitive employment and have to be treated as incapables, or when it can be done they

have to be taught to do new work, and be re-established as earners. More and more of this re-making the man himself should be given attention, and suggests the need for trained social workers. 

When the man loses his work because his vocation is no longer generally needed, as with the blacksmith, he needs material help for a time, but needs guidance to find employment in another vocation. 

When a financial depression occurs, such as existed in 1930 and for a time thereafter, and great numbers of good workmen are out of work, then organized relief is required. 

Governmental building programs and relief work are a great boon until things become normal. An extension of relief work into the period when recuperation of industry should take place, may retard recovery from the depression. Good, capable men ought to be protected against such wholesale unemployment, and against the inactivity of old age, without their pride being injured by the feeling that they are charity cases. Here is where the Security Program of the L. D. S. Church will function admirably. 

Achieving Security. The Nation has much to do to prevent the frequent return of these depression periods, and to provide temporary employment to bridge such gaps. The greater part of the task of providing security rests with the individual himself. Chief among these is care on his part that he does not become a misfit or an incapable workman. The urgent need of study and alertness to changing conditions has been emphasized only a few paragraphs back. 

Man in his youth and prime owes it to his old age, and to his family if he should die early, to provide himself with life insurance in a form that can be turned into an annuity in his old age. Most of the really alert

men now do this, but hundreds of families are suddenly left destitute without either a provider or insurance. 

Tithing paid faithfully and regularly not only supports the Church, but under a properly administered Security Program becomes a form of insurance. 

It is well also for every person to develop a hobby or a skill different from that required by his vocation. 

The ability to work in another job besides his present one may well be acquired, bit by bit, as time permits. 

Every man should, at intervals, ask himself whether his occupation is gradually being crowded out, and if so look about intelligently for a new niche he can fill. 

He should also ask whether he is personally gaining or losing ground. If he is losing, he should face this fact before it is too late, and take himself properly in hand to prevent his being set adrift. 

Drifting about in life is both popular and dangerous. It is dangerous both to the vocational and to the spiritual welfare of man. Drifters frequently end up in dark corners, or at least shady ones. Except for a few unusually weak and leaky vessels of men, most of those who commit misdemeanors and other forms of wrong-doing and sin drift into the misdeeds. A directing hand and a propelling power are as necessary to keep a man upright and moving as to keep a bicycle upright and moving.

1. What is meant by a vocation? An occupation? An avocation? A hobby? 

2. Why has the number of vocations increased in the last generation or two? How many vocations are there? Illustrate with farming. With another industry. 

3. What preparation is needed to prepare a man for a vocation? To keep him prepared? 

4. What provision should be made by a man when his vocation is showing signs of ceasing to be used?

5. What has been the principal reasons for unemployment in depression periods? At other times? How may these conditions be improved? 

6. Should a man have a reserve vocation? Or should he concentrate on one? What is the relation of this question to the 

Church Welfare Program?

Chapter 29  

"Wherefore, verily I say unto you, that all things unto me are spiritual, and not at any time have I given unto you a law which was temporal." (D. & C. 29:34.) "For to be carnally minded is death; but to be spiritually minded is life and peace." (Romans 8:6.) 

"All who would understand the things of God must understand them by the spirit of God. We cannot talk about spiritual things without connecting with them temporal things, neither can we talk about temporal things without connecting spiritual things with them. 

Whether we are raising cattle, planting, gathering, building, or inhabiting, we are in the Lord, and all we do is within the pale of His kingdom upon the earth, consequently it is all spiritual and all temporal, no matter what we are laboring to accomplish." (Brigham 

Young, J. D. 10:329.)

Relationship of the Temporal and the Spiritual. 

The story is related of a wealthy woman who attended a theater in her community, where she witnessed a very sad and soul-stirring drama. Frequently throughout the performance she wept freely as her sympathies and pity were aroused by the actors. It was a bitter cold night. During the long performance, her footman had shivered outside in her carriage, waiting faithfully at his post for her return. Eventually she did return only to find the footman frozen to death while she had vented her sympathies on the performers inside the comfortable theater.

Religionists are frequently quite as inconsistent as the woman in the story. There are fanatical believers in every church who subscribe wholeheartedly to the "letter of the law," but who fail to see the spiritual application of that teaching to practical living. There are

those who fail to realize that charity begins at home and that the fruits of true religion may be found first in a man's love and devotion to his family. When men realize that "a man is in the service of God when he is in the service of his fellowmen," then they have learned the first lesson in the practicability of the Gospel of Jesus Christ.

There are those who would have us believe that one must be poor in order to be righteous and that poverty, sickness and misfortune are but the penalties of disobedience and failure to live God's commandments. 

President Joseph F. Smith has wisely said of the harmonious relationship of the temporal and the spiritual: "You must continue to bear in mind that the temporal and the spiritual are blended. They are not separate. One cannot be carried on without the other, so long as we are here in mortality. "The Latter-day Saints believe not only in the gospel of spiritual salvation, but also in the gospel of temporal salvation. We have to look after the cattle and the sheep and the horses, the gardens and the farms, the irrigation canals and ditches, and other necessary things for the maintenance of ourselves and our families in the earth. In this respect this Church is different from many other denominations. We do not feel that it is possible for men to be really good and faithful Christian people unless they can also be good, faithful, honest and industrious people. Therefore, we preach the gospel of economy, the gospel of sobriety. 

We preach that the idler shall not eat the bread of the laborer, and that the idler is not entitled to an inheritance in Zion. We preach that those who are industrious, those who work, those who through their integrity and industry are good citizens of the kingdom of God, are better citizens of the country in which they live than those who are not so diligent in this regard. "The work that we are engaged in is not designed to be limited by the spiritual necessities of the people alone. It is the purpose of God in restoring the Gospel and the holy priesthood, not only to benefit mankind spiritually, but also to benefit them temporally. 

The Lord has expressed this many times, in the word that He gave to his servant Joseph Smith, the prophet; He designed that His people should become the richest of all people. And this not only means the richest of all people in heavenly gifts-in spiritual bless-

ings and riches, but it also means that the people of God shall be the richest of all people with regard to temporal matters. If faithful we have a right to claim the blessings of the Lord upon the labors of our hands, our temporal labors. The farmer has a right to ask the Lord for blessings upon his farm, upon the labor that he bestows upon it. He has a right to claim the blessings of the Lord upon the animals that are necessary to the cultivation of his farm. 

He has a right to ask God to bless the grain that he sows and the seeds of the fruit that he plants in the soil. It is his privilege, not only to ask and claim these blessings at the hand of the Lord, but it is his right and privilege to receive blessings from God upon his labor, upon his farm, and upon all that he puts his hand unto in righteousness. It is our privilege to ask God to remove the curse from the earth, and to make it fruitful. If we will live so that we shall be entitled to His favor, and so that we may justly and righteously claim the blessings and gifts that He has promised unto His Saints, then that which we ask will be given, and we shall receive and enjoy them more abundantly." (Gospel Doctrine, page 260.) 

When this point of view is clearly understood, Latter-day Saints will rejoice with the man who is prospering in his business or on his farm, and enabled to enjoy the comforts and luxuries of life, and at the same time is sympathetic and helpful toward those who are unfortunate and in dire circumstances.

With such an understanding, men would realize that wealth and possessions are not evils in themselves but may become such only because of an evil use to which these riches may be applied. 

When Karl G. Maeser was sent to open the Brigham Young Academy he was told by President Brigham

Young to teach not even arithmetic without the inspiration of the Lord.

Applying that same philosophy to welfare activities, each individual should strive in all that he does to seek the inspiration of the Lord, and "with an eye single to the glory of God," to provide for his own household to the best of his ability and then literally contribute of his substance and his energies that those less fortunate

or with less opportunity or ability might be raised to a proper standard of living and permitted to enjoy the good things of the earth.

Each person is looked upon by the Latter-day Saints as an eternal being, begotten as a son or daughter unto a Heavenly Father and Mother in the spirit world, and a personality that will continue to exist after the period of mortal life. With a deep sense of gratitude for the common Fatherhood of God and an appreciation for the Universal Brotherhood of man, every Priesthood quorum member should strive with all the power and ability at his command to attain to the standard set by the First Presidency in their official message to the Church at the April Conference, 1936, "THE 

WARD MUST BE ONE GREAT FAMILY OF EQUALS." 

In

Spiritual Values in the Church Welfare Plan. 

keeping with the philosophy of the Church, each person is a moral agent who lives his own life in his own way. 

Yet the Church attempts to bring these independent agents into a community where they may unitedly work out their mutual salvation under the inspiration and authority of God.

When men and women unite in an activity of mutual helpfulness, where a feeling of selfishness is wholly eliminated or at least greatly diminished, there will be found spiritual growth, and love, charity, peace, and joy will be found to reign supreme. 

President J. Reuben Clark, Jr., speaking to a group of Priesthood and Relief Society officers during the last October conference, made the following statement: "The great work, the great achievement of the work we are doing is the spiritual advancement. "One Bishop said that two years ago he called the people and 17

had them all work together in these various projects. Two things have come out of this: 

1. People who were at outs, one with the other, who weren't able to speak because of some trifling differences, have ironed out their differences and now they are brothers and sisters in reality. 

2. Fast offerings increased greatly, and attendance at sacrament meetings increased 25 per cent. "This year they have not been cooperating in the same way and to the same extent, by mutual work and mutual contact. They have met their assignments, but that is all. Attendance at Sacrament meetings has dropped off. I wish to say that the spirit of brotherhood behind this is the most important element in it, greater by far than the mere relieving of the want, and need, great as that achievement is. This work if carried out in the way forecast by the General Committee will make of this people, a united people. 

It will bring us together as brothers and sisters, conscious of one another's troubles and trials, and our sympathies will go out to each other. We shall be a people of the Lord indeed. That this may be the end for which we live, and to which we shall come, I pray in the name of Jesus Christ, Amen." (Special meeting, Assembly Hall, October, 1938.)

Spirit of Unselfish Service on Welfare Projects. 

During a recent visit to one of the Bishop's storehouses, where a group of from fifty to sixty men and women were busily engaged in processing and preserving foods for winter distribution, a chance conversation revealed an interesting and significant phase of this project.

In this group, almost everyone was receiving assistance from the storehouse through a proper understanding with the bishop. Everybody was busy and seemingly all were very happy. Upon making inquiry as to the reason for the seeming cheerfulness one woman replied, "We have many people in the Church who are not able to work as we are, and through our efforts these unfortunate people can be helped." 

And so it was; all were happy in service for others and apparently little mindful that their own sustenance was being provided thereby. Their aim seemed to be

not what they were to get, but how much they were able to give! They gave what they had to give and in return they were to receive what they needed. 

Indeed, as remarked by a world traveler recently, who visited some of these projects, "Here is to be found an example of applied Christianity, the happiest people 

I have ever met." 

Not what we give but what we share,

For the gift without the giver is bare; 

Who gives himself with his alms feeds three, Himself, his hungry neighbor, and Me. (Vision of Sir Launfal)

How Much is a Work Receipt Worth? Every in dividual who engages on a Church Welfare Project, whether or not at present in need of assistance, is expected to receive from the work director or other supervisor designated by the ward bishop and the ward welfare committee, a work receipt for the time each has contributed on the project. In order to make for harmony between the bishop and the project director, the names of all those assigned to the project should be submitted to the bishop and the ward committee for approval inasmuch as with the work receipt there goes the implied promise of the bishop that the needs of the individual receiving such receipts will be supplied.

In a particular ward, the bishop had authorized the assignment of an unemployed member of his ward to a farm project. At the close of the day, the man returned to the bishop with the work receipt he had received and inquired as to what the receipt was worth. 

The bishop asked, "How much do you think it is worth?" "I usually get $3.50 or $4.00 a day," the man replied.

"All right, if you insist, I will give you your price," replied the bishop, "but when you set a price on the work receipt and I pay you, I have discharged every responsibility the Church has towards you and your family for your efforts."

The bishop then explained the plan by which everyone was expected to contribute of his means and his time in such productive activities as would make possible sustenance to individuals or families when in need, but that no one was expected to make application for assistance except as he may have need, but that if he were faithful in doing all possible to help on projects designed to advance the Welfare Program, he could be assured that the necessities of life would be provided. 

With this understanding hundreds of brethren of the Priesthood, and sisters of the Relief Society, have willingly donated their services, assured that if they were in need their wants would be supplied, but filled with gratitude if they are not now in need that they are privileged to help those who are less fortunate.

The Welfare Plan thus becomes an opportunity program for every Church member to render valuable service to the Church and its people. 

The Blessings of Divine Guidance. The conduct and direction of these activities is in the hands of men who bear the Holy Priesthood. These earthen vessels bear precious gifts from heaven, yet men should be careful not to err in the exercise of this power so that others may think their free agency has been denied or curtailed. The Lord has made it clear how the Priesthood should be exercised: "Behold, there are many called, but few are chosen, And why are they not chosen? "That they may be conferred upon us, it is true; but when we undertake to cover our sins, or to gratify our pride, or vain am-

"ALL THINGS ARE SPIRITUAL” 

259 bition, or to exercise control or dominion or compulsion upon the souls of the children of men, in any degree of unrighteousness, behold, the heavens withdraw themselves; and the Spirit of the

Lord is grieved; and when it is withdrawn, Amen to the Priesthood or the authority of that man. . . . "No power or influence can or ought to be maintained by virtue of the Priesthood, only by persuasion, by long-suffering, by gentleness and meekness, and by love unfeigned." (D. & Č. 

121:34, 37, 41.)

The Worth of Souls. The voice of the Lord to Joseph Smith before the Church was organized declared: "Remember, the worth of souls is great in the sight of God; "And if it so be that you should labor all your days in crying repentance unto this people, and bring, save it be one soul unto me, how great shall be your joy with him in the kingdom of my 

Father!" (D. & C. 18:10, 15.)

A project for the growing of corn had been undertaken in one of our wards by the Adult Aaronic Priesthood group. The bishop of the ward frequently joined with the men in the morning and evening before and after his own work.

Among those working on this project was a man who smoked and used profanity. At the slightest provocation while he worked on the project this man profaned frequently and smoked often while the bishop seemingly paid little heed. After the third day, this man came to the bishop and apologized for his conduct and pledged himself to overcome his bad habits and to make himself more fit to associate with the brethren of his Priesthood group. His activity with his associates in an unselfish endeavor had transformed selfishness into unselfishness, and within the year following he had become a valued worker in his ward. 

Through this welfare work the Priesthood must endeavor to bring about a thorough-going reformation throughout the Church. Along with material help

should go direct spiritual counsel. Inactive members should be encouraged by gestures of goodwill and sincere fellowship to take renewed interest in the work of the Church. The time when persecution united the members of the Church into a strong brotherhood has passed. Today the Priesthood through the spiritualized instrument of the Welfare Program must weld the members into a strong fraternity. Such a positive force with such objectives will be warmly welcomed by a troubled social order.

Spiritual Growth. If the Welfare Program of the 

Church does not foster and promote the spiritual welfare of its members as well as their temporal security it will not have succeeded in full. As the temporal wants of the needy are supplied there should be a reawakening in the field of the spirit; a spiritual yearning; a hungering for righteousness; a fuller devotion to the 

Church. As a natural and spontaneous outgrowth of the Welfare Plan there should be a vast increase in the number of tithe payers; of family prayer; of attendance at sacrament meeting; of fast donations for the support of the poor; and of all other activities which indicate the spiritual stability and moral integrity of a people. 

The Welfare Plan is not a mere scheme of social work; a soulless system of charity. It is a spiritual activity designed to bring temporal salvation to everyone. 

It is a religious brotherhood in which every unit is a spiritual force to promote equality, union, and good will. It is athrob with life and spirit; an army of voluntary workers possessing free wills.
