Such a system can in time bring true social security and spiritual satisfaction to the needy. A sense of joy in well-doing comes to those who have given their time and effort for the mutual assistance of all. From this

plan of mutual helpfulness, a spirit of devotion, activity, and profound interest in religion should follow as a natural consequence. Further, this plan should develop a spirit of thanksgiving throughout the Church. 

The entire system is so permeated with the spiritual aspect that once it is fully and properly practiced it can be said of this people as it was of the Nephites: "Surely there could not be a happier people among all the people who had been created by the hand of God." (IV Nephi 1:16.)

The key for success and power in the welfare work, rests with the Priesthood. If the knowledge of the Gospel and the power of the Priesthood are not utilized by the people to achieve the aims of the Plan, the restoration of the Priesthood and its inspired organizations will have been to that extent in vain. 

1. Why should the Church be so interested in the economic welfare of its members? 

2. What preparation has been made in the Church for such a program as the Welfare Plan? 

3. Why should the restored Church be more successful in planning and administering a program of Welfare work than any other group? 

4. Are the achievements of the present plan commensurate with the possibilities? 

5. Read II Nephi 2 and discuss the place of free agency in the Gospel plan. 

6. Read the Improvement Era, April, 1937, to see what was accomplished during the first year of the plan.

Chapter 30 

TEMPORAL SALVATION 

There is no gift greater than the gift of salvation." (D. & C. 6:13.) "Pray unto the Lord, call upon His holy name, make known

His wonderful works among the people. "Call upon the Lord, that His kingdom may go forth upon the earth, that the inhabitants thereof may receive it, and be prepared for the days to come, in the which the Son of Man shall come down in heaven, clothed in the brightness of His glory, to meet the kingdom of God which is set up on the earth. "Wherefore may the kingdom of God go forth, that the kingdom of heaven may come, that thou, O God, mayest be glorified in heaven so on earth, that thy enemies may be subdued; for thine is the honor, power and glory, for ever and ever." (D. & C. 65:4-6.)

Salvation in its Broader Interpretations. In the previous chapter it was declared that the Latter-day Saints believe not only in the gospel of spiritual salvation, but also in the Gospel of temporal salvation. 

Perhaps throughout the world there is no one thing so universally sought after by human beings as temporal "salvation," or social security in a material world. To the beggar, contentment may come when he has secured a hearty meal and shelter from the cold for the night, to another a store of necessities laid by for a winter's need, or a generous bank account, etc., may be required to bring the assurance of security.

To guarantee to themselves this security men have united together into clubs, fraternities, unions and associations for the advancement of their social and economic interests and for mutual protection. Representatives of the people in law-making bodies have passed legislation instituting insurance plans and pension sys-

TEMPORAL SALVATION 

263 tems, and unemployment compensation, all designed to safeguard the welfare of individuals affected. There is little doubt but that the thing that has caused more unhappiness and social unrest throughout the world is the fear of poverty and want.

Eight years after the Church was organized the Lord in a revelation to the Prophet Joseph, revealed to the Saints important information regarding the purpose for organizing the stakes in the land of Zion. "That the gathering together upon the land of Zion and upon her stakes, may be for a defense, and for a refuge from the storm and from wrath when it shall be poured out without mixture upon the whole earth." 

If the organized stakes of Zion functioned today as the Lord intends, the greatest possible measure of economic, moral, and spiritual security would be guaranteed to faithful Church members. When stake authorities, together with ward officers, Priesthood quorums, Relief Society and other auxiliary organizations so live up to their privileges for service to their fellowmen over whom they preside, that "angels cannot be constrained from being their associates," then there will be "no envyings, nor strifes, nor tumults, nor any manner of lasciviousness; and surely there could not be a happier people among all the people who had been created by the hand of God. (4 Nephi 16.)

The Word of Wisdom-The Order and Will of 

God in Temporal Salvation. "A Word of Wisdom, for the benefit of the council of high priests, assembled in Kirtland, and the church, and also the saints in Zion- "To be sent greeting; not by commandment or constraint, but by revelation and the word of wisdom, showing forth the order and will of God in the temporal salvation of all saints in the last days." (D. & C. 89:1-2.)

In a search for guidance toward temporal security or salvation, one must not overlook the importance of the Latter-day Saints observing strictly the Word of Wisdom, which is declared by the Lord to be "the order and will of God in the temporal salvation of all saints in the last days."

Perhaps there is no one thing that is so much responsible for creating within men who hold the Priesthood feelings of unworthiness and in divorcing them from the fellowship with the Priesthood quorum activities as is the breaking of the Word of Wisdom. 

Men who by their own acts in using tobacco, liquor, tea, coffee, etc., thus deny themselves the fraternalism and brotherhood of a Priesthood quorum whose possibilities of support and mutual helpfulness are unlimited, are denying themselves great blessings. Besides the loss of the fraternal support of the Priesthood quorum, it has been estimated that if all the money that has been spent for liquor since the repeal of the 18th Amendment would have been spent in home building, that two and one-half million $5,000 homes could have been built with that money. (Statistics compiled by 

Y. M. C. A. and furnished by Dr. Creed Haymond.) 

In 1937, there were 162,000,000,000 cigarettes used in 

America, according to government statistics. At 15c. per package for cigarettes this would amount to a total cost of 1,111,500,000. In addition to that item alone there were consumed 1,830 tons of snuff and 95,875 tons of pipe tobacco. (Information obtained from 

American Tobacco Co., by Dr. Creed Haymond.)

In the state of Utah for the year 1937, the liquor sales amounted to the huge sum of $4,900,326, or an average of $8.05 for every man, woman, and child in the state. This does not include the sale of beer, but

TEMPORAL SALVATION 

265 statistics obtained from Media records, relied upon by the State Liquor Commission, reports $3,999,810 in total beer sales for the state, or $7.89 per capita. (In formation obtained from Utah State Liquor Commis sion.) Despite these astounding figures the state of 

Utah is tenth on the list of states in the United States in the amount of total consumption, consuming only .22 of 1% of the total liquor consumed in the United States. 

What an economic waste! What could have been accomplished had this money been expended in the establishment of new industries or in the Church Welfare Program of rehabilitating families now dependent on communities for sustenance!

Furthermore, the Lord has promised that all saints who keep the Word of Wisdom shall receive health, wisdom, and great treasures of knowledge; shall be blessed with powers of endurance, and shall not be destroyed before their missions on earth are completed. 

The keeping of the Word of Wisdom must become an important foundation stone as men seek for temporal salvation.

A Dual Obligation. When an individual becomes a member of the Church, he assumes an obligation to keep the commandments of the Lord and to live in accordance with the teachings of the Gospel. His obedience to the teachings of the Church entitles him to a remission of his sins, companionship of the Holy Ghost, advancement in the Priesthood, fellowship with the Saints in the Temples, etc.

In a similar manner a person upon entrance into the Church by baptism is promised certain temporal blessings, but just as in the case of spiritual blessings, these temporal blessings are predicated upon obedience to law. The requirements of the individual in this re-

spect are that he pay his tithes and fast offerings, that he is willing to work, is thrifty, keeps the Word of 

Wisdom, exercises faith in the Lord, prays for guidance and wisdom in his temporal affairs, and yields obedience to the counsels of righteous men who preside over him.

To one who yields obedience to these requirements, the Lord has given some wonderful promises: "I, the Lord, am bound when ye do what I say; but when ye do not what I say, ye have no promise." (D. & C. 82:10) "Bring ye all the tithes into the storehouse, that there may be meat in mine house, and prove me now herewith, saith the Lord of 

Hosts, if I will not open you the windows of heaven, and pour you out a blessing, that there shall not be room enough to receive it. "And I will rebuke the devourer for your sakes, and he shall not destroy the fruits of your ground; neither shall your vine_cast her fruit before the time in the field, saith the Lord of hosts." (Malachi 3:10-11) "And it is my purpose to provide for my saints, for all things are mine. "But it must needs be done in mine own way; and behold this is the way that I, the Lord, have decreed to provide for my saints, that the poor shall be exalted, in that the rich are made low." (D. & 

C. 104:15-16.)

The Church subscribes fully to the doctrine expounded by James, the Apostle. "If any man among you seem to be religious, and bridleth not his tongue, but deceiveth his own heart, this man's religion is vain. "Pure religion and undefiled before God and the Father is this, To visit the fatherless and widows in their affliction, and to keep himself unspotted from the world." (James 1:26-27.)

And the leaders of the Church have declared that the Church proposed to "help needy and unemployed members of the Church to help themselves." The 

Church recognized an obligation to keep its members contented, happy and active and at the same time admonishes against idleness and trying to get something

TEMPORAL SALVATION 

267 for nothing. It also recognizes the fact that "self-effort is the key to self development and personal progress." 

A Warfare Against Social Ills. The significance of the Church Welfare Program in meeting the social problems existing among us at the present time is clearly set forth in a statement made by President David O. 

McKay at a Salt Lake Regional meeting held in Salt 

Lake City, February, 1937: "You are, as it were, in the front lines and trenches tonight.

The necessary material is in your hands and I hope in your minds and in your hearts, and you are ready to go "over the top" to meet the enemy. Perhaps you do not like this connotation of war, but we are engaged in a war-a war against idleness; a war aginst depression, war against social enmity. We are going to fight for the establishing of brotherhood and of cooperation, two fundamental principles of the Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints." (Deseret News, Church Section, Feb. 13, 1937.)

To wage a successful war against enemies schooled in the arts of making idleness, depression, and social enmity, will require maximum effort on the part of the individual, a fraternal unity in every Priesthood quorum, and the wisdom of the Lord manifested in intelligent leadership of those who preside. 

Priesthood and Temporal Salvation. 

Brigham Young declares: President "Our religion is adapted to the capacity of the whole human family. It does not send a portion of the people to howl in torment for ever and ever, but it reaches after the last son and daughter of Adam and Eve, and will pluck them from the prison, unlock the doors, and burst the bonds and bring forth every soul who will receive salvation."

When we understand that it is the Lord's work and glory to bring to pass the immortality and eternal life of His children, it behooves the bearers of the Priesthood, as agents of God upon earth, to be likewise concerned. The various problems, methods, and situations

moulded together under the agencies of Church Welfare, become only means of furthering "temporal salvation."

There is power in the Priesthood. When righteous men magnify this heavenly gift, their words become as the voice of God; their acts as those of heaveninspired agents. A realization of this heavenly relationship has made the work of Priesthood quorums and the missions of the Church inseparable. 

Respecting this vital subject, President Joseph F. 

Smith has said: "We want the people to understand, and we would like the world to understand, the great fact that it is not the prominent leaders of the Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints that wield all the influence among the people of this Church. We want it understood that we have fewer lay members in this Church, in proportion to the number of our membership, than you will find in any other church upon the globe. Nearly every man in the Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints holds the Melchizedek or the Aaronic Priesthood, and may act in an official capacity by authority of that priesthood whenever called upon to do so. They are priests and kings, if you please, unto God in righteousness. "So that nearly every male person in the Church, who has reached the years of accountability, is supposed to be, in his sphere, a pillar in Zion, a defender of the faith, an exemplar, a man of righteousness, truth and soberness, a man of virtue and of honor, a good citizen of the state in which he lives, and a staunch and loyal citizen of the great country that we are proud to call our home." (Gospel Doctrine, p. 197.)

The Spirit of Jesus Christ. The following beauti tiful story is related by a Christian missionary in India: "There is one vivid picture from my life in India which I should like to paint here. One Sunday night a young Indian graduate, on whose forehead was painted the symbols of a heathen god, came to tell me that he had decided to become a Christian. As we talked in the moonlight in my garden at Madras, it came out that he was a keen student of the Gospels, and had been captivated by 

Christ. I discovered that for some time he had been gathering little outcast children on to his veranda and teaching them their letters. 

When I asked him why he did this, since it involved the breaking

TEMPORAL SALVATION 

269 of his caste rules, his reply was as simple as it was sublime: 'I thought it was the kind of thing Jesus Christ would do.' I discovered also that in an important examination, when pens would not go round and a man next to him was writing in pencil, with the risk of having his papers disqualified, this man lent him his fountain pen and himself took the pencil. His reason was the same: 'I thought it was the kind of thing Jesus Christ would have done.' 

I asked him the question anyone would have asked: 'If you have studied the Gospels, and been so attracted to Christ and so caught His spirit, why did you not become a Christian before?' I shall never forget his answer. 'I am attracted,' he said, 'but Christ demands the carrying of a cross and absolute surrender, and I would not become a Christian before because I wasn't prepared to go all the way.' 

We stood there in the garden, I with collar that symbolizes the Christian ministry, and he with his forehead painted with the marks of a heathen god, but I knew who was the better Christian of the two, and it wasn't I." (Leslie D. Weatherhead, “Jesus and Ourselves.")

Such is the guide that must characterize our activities in the Church program of extending the possibilities of temporal salvation to faithful members of the Church. "What would Jesus do if He were here in our place?"

When it is understood by members of the Priesthood quorums that "Priesthood is the power of God delegated to man to act in the earth for the salvation of the human family." (Gospel Doctrine, page 173.) Their duty to faithful distressed members will be more clearly recognized in helping to make good the promises of the Lord to those who have been faithful and who have been promised that "He will open unto you the windows of heaven and pour you out a blessing that there shall not be room enough to receive it." 

In the words of Constantine, "It is alien to our character to allow anyone to perish of famine."

Our duty as members of a Priesthood quorum should not cease so long as there is one person within out fold who lacks for the necessary comforts and necessities of life. 

In the words of President Joseph F. Smith:

"It is the duty of this vast body of men holding the holy priesthood, which is after the order of the Son of God, to exert their influence and exercise their power for good among the people of Israel and the people of the world. It is their bounden duty to preach and to work righteousness, both at home and abroad." ( Gospel Doctrine p. 196.)

1. Clement of Alexandria (155-220) said: "Christ does not debar me from property. But do you see yourself overcome and overthrown by it? Leave it, throw it away, hate, renounce, flee it!"

How do such teachings fit into plans for universal welfare? 

What are the uses of property? 

2. How do the organizations of the Church help the realization of social solidarity? 

3. How does Dr. Rauschenbusch's assertion fit into a program for human welfare: "If the Church has not faith enough in its 

Christian law to assert its sovereignty over all relations of society, men will deny that it is a good and practical law at all. If the church cannot conquer business, business will conquer the church." 

4. For a pictorial record of the building program of various 

Latter-day Saint communities, see the Improvement Era for April 1937.

Chapter 31 

"And if the program of the Mormon Church can demonstrate its ability to look after its own members, it will have made the greatest contribution to the world in this generation." (New York Times.)

The quorum is responsible for the peace and welfare of its members. Upon the quorums of the Priesthood rests the great responsibility of keeping every member contented and active. Our popular system of missionary work would defeat its own purpose if we were to neglect the converts who are converted among the nations of the earth. Each quorum member must retain the missionary spirit at home and become his brother's keeper. The care of all must be the concern of all.

Brigham Young, speaking on the subject of Priesthood, made the following statement: "If men are ruled by the power, principles, and righteousness of the Priesthood, they will find themselves in possession of all the wisdom they need to meet every emergency of this changing existence, and all they require to conquer the world, the flesh and the devil." (J. of D., Vol. 9, p. 257.)

The experience of the Church Welfare Plan in the last two and a half years has shown wherever Priesthood quorums are ruled by the "power, principles, and righteousness" that should exist in a priesthood fraternity, there is no economic problem of an individual in such a group that cannot be solved by united effort. 

When a man is ordained to a grade of the Priesthood, he automatically becomes eligible for membership in 18

a quorum group of brethren who have been ordained to the same Priesthood. He is not expected to function separately and apart from but in cooperation with his brethren who bear the same Priesthood in his ward and stake. Men do not all possess similar abilities; one may lack certain qualities that may be found in his fellow quorum members. If the principle of Priesthood unity were to rule, the power that would be generated in the solution of individual problems would be unlimited. 

(a) Counseling Quorum Members: 

Experience has taught that most of the economic pitfalls into which men fall could have been avoided had they sought counsel from those who have been successful in similar endeavors. Most men who have become victims of economic depression, did not seek advice and assistance until after their affairs became so muddled that a solution was rendered well nigh impossible. If an individual member realized the value of his association with his own Priesthood quorum he would lay before these brethren his problems and would abide by their counsels in righteousness. 

(b) The Quorum as an Effective Labor Employ ment Agency.

Suppose that in a Priesthood quorum, the chairman of the personal welfare committee reports that there are three members who are out of employment. 

How can the quorum move to meet this emergency?

If membership in Melchizedek Priesthood quorums averaged from twenty-five to fifty and each of the employed members were to be given the names of the three men who were unemployed with their ages, occupational abilities, etc., and each member was constantly on the lookout for any vacancies in the institutions

where he was employed, no labor agency in the community could be more efficient in locating jobs for their unemployed. Some quorums have made a practice to invite returning missionaries to visit with them as soon as he returns home. He is asked to state what his plans are and his prospects for finding employment and what assistance the quorum may render. Such a practice has been productive of much good and a great benefit to the individual as well as to the quorum. 

These are but suggestive of the ways by which the quorum may function in the discharge of its responsibility to individual members. Men who were failing in their business and on their farms have received guidance from their more successful quorum members. 

Homes have been constructed with the quorum members supplying labor, without cost, to a brother who without such help could not have owned a home. 

In all such activities there is effectively demonstrated the wisdom and the power in a Priesthood quorum that functions as the Lord intended. 

If every quorum of the Church were to undertake to rehabilitate one of its members each year it would not be long until the objective of the Welfare Program would be realized.

Purpose and Duty of the Quorum. 

"The quorums of the Priesthood provide organizations through which the purposes of the Lord with respect to the Priesthood may be more completely accomplished. "A main purpose of the quorum is to help every individual member of the quorum and his family to attain a condition of thorough well-being in body, mind, and spirit. Every need of a man holding the Priesthood should be the concern of the quorum to which he belongs." (Guide for Priesthood Quorums, p. 24.) 

President Joseph F. Smith explained that: "It is the duty of this vast body of men holding the holy Priest-

hood, which is after the order of the Son of God, to exert their influence and exercise their power for good among the people of Israel and the people of the world. It is their bounden duty to preach and to work righteousness, both at home and abroad.” (Gospel Doctrine, p. 196.) 

Obligations of Quorum Members. "If the quorum thus assumes responsibility for the welfare of its members, it follows that each member must dedicate himself to the task imposed upon the quorum. A man who accepts the Priesthood accepts the obligations of the Priesthood, and must so conduct his life and use his gifts as to be qualified to perform the work properly required of those who hold the Priesthood. He must prepare himself by careful training and earnest labor, to assume the five-fold responsibility of all who hold the Priesthood. "1. He must keep himself fit, physically, mentally, and spiritually, to do the work of the Priesthood. "2. He must so provide, temporarily and spiritually, for his family, that they will not suffer because of his labors in the Priesthood. "3. He must develop such a relationship with society, that is his fellow men, that he can readily and effectively work with and for other men.

This is essential in his work in the Priesthood. "4. He must practice the arts of citizenship, so that his loyalty to his country and to its laws and institutions, may be unquestioned, otherwise his work in the Priesthood will be hindered. "5. He must maintain the proper active relationship to the 

Church, by living its precepts and participating in its activities and ordinances." (Guide for Priesthood Quorums, p. 27.)

In this unselfish mission of human welfare the great responsibility rests upon each member to be worthy of the Priesthood which he bears. The admonition of the Savior to those who represent Him in the ministry, "Be ye clean," rings in his ears as he goes forth to render service in his community. He knows that his power in the Priesthood is dependent upon his faithfulness (Doc. and Cov. 121:34-36), and he should be inspired to live a better life as he becomes more active in the ministry.

The Welfare Program becomes a widespread agency of reform and relief the moment all quorum

members prepare themselves for active participation in its service. At the close of 1938 there were 124 stakes in the Church, 118 High Priests' quorums, with 

20,214 members; 220 quorums of Seventy with 13,621 members; 611 Elders' quorums with 53,198 members, and 850 Priests' quorums, 820 Teachers' quorums, and 

1,351 Deacons' quorums. With a total of 167,000 men and boys united in a brotherhood of service, and nearly an equal number of women engaged in the Relief Society program, it is hardly conceivable the vast amount of good that may result from the welfare work, if each individual accepts his personal responsibility for the kingdom of God on earth.

Emotional Health. The quorum is an ideal group for the development of emotional poise or personality. 

There is no better agency for polishing "rough diamonds" and making "polished shafts" more useful still.

Personality is not born altogether, but is developed by practice. It has been found that people who are active 

Church members have significantly better personality traits than those who do not become active members of a church. The quorum is in a unique position to become a strong agency for personality and character development. By recognizing and employing the elements of leadership and genius in its members it can become a training field for the emotional improvement of every member of the group.

Economic Training. In training the mind and offering the service of vocational guidance to its members, the quorums have scarcely glimpsed the vision of what lies within their reach in this respect. Other groups are conducting Adult Education movements; the Priesthood quorums must catch the spirit and real-

ize the possibilities that await their united efforts for training in economic skill.

Little has been done in the way of organizing the available talent in the quorum or in neighboring quorums for the purpose of providing training for backward members or others who wish to add to their skills. 

More should be done. 

Social Opportunities. As a Priesthood agency, the quorum provides social as well as intellectual opportunities for the member and his family. The valuable departments of the Mutual Improvement Association and aid to the Priesthood, is available for the older members of the family, as well as the younger. Training in athletic, social, and aesthetic activity, together with the educational program of leisure time guidance, provides contacts and opportunities of great value. 

The Relief Society, the Sunday School, the Primary, the Seminary, all together furnish a full curriculum for the advancement of every member of the family of the Priesthood brand.

Besides, every quorum has quorum social affairs, which bring together quorum members and their families for more intimate acquaintanceship. 

The active quorum member takes advantage of all these social devices, as does his family. Through these aids, as well as in the home, the Gospel is taught to the family. There follow as a natural sequence, successful marriages for his sons and daughters, healthy progeny, less delinquency, greater happiness.

The active and faithful Priesthood member gains a world of experience in instruction and contact, which the inactive member knows nothing about. The active member realizes that "Men are that they might have joy."

The Welfare Plan gives each quorum a stimulus to account for the economic, cultural, and spiritual welfare of each member. It draws them together as a family unit, inspiring each employed member to inquire: "Has my brother full time employment? Does any member of the quorum have sickness in his family?

What might the group do to add to the peace and prosperity of any of its needy members?"

For this reason the goal, "A Project For Every Quorum," was made an objective of the Welfare Program. This not only benefits the quorum members who need assistance, but it fosters a spirit of unity in the group which could not otherwise be developed. The 

Priesthood and its quorum organizations need challenging problems like the Welfare Plan to vitalize and inspire cooperative activity to help individual members and to point out the power of united effort and the path to mutual happiness.

The Quorum and Modern Revelation. At this time of widening fields of social service the Priesthood quorums become ideal agencies for the planning and administration of welfare work. The quorum is entitled to inspiration for its tasks as is each member in his personal labors. The many revelatlons in the Doctrine and Covenants which explain the dignity and function of the Priesthood, assure each brother that he has been called to a high and holy calling; that he is honored by the call to service; and that the inspiration and success of his labor will depend upon his faithfulness and diligence.

Each member, whether poor or economically independent, finds a golden opportunity under the Welfare Program to assist in fulfilling the obligation of man's estate, namely: "To subdue the earth." This

labor involves quorum projects, which tend to consolidate the members into an integrated group, while at the same time they assist in the relief of distress. Such united action, like true charity, benefit the giver as well as the receiver.

The faithful quorum member will hearken to the admonition of the living oracles whose advice and instruction are words of inspiration. Hundreds of acres of land were tilled by quorums immediately after the First Presidency advised that quorums unite on the 

Make-Work Project, such as raising sugar beets, grain or other agricultural crops. (See Improvement Era, June, 1936, page 332.)

The Church authorities have encouraged the unemployed members to seek lucrative employment in addition to what their quorum can help them. The 

First Presidency gave the following wholesome advice at a time when too many people wanted to get something for nothing: "Let every Latter-day Saint who has a farm, farm it, and not try to borrow money to be paid back by the Government. Let every man feel that he is the architect and builder of his own life, and that he proposes to make a success of it by working. 'Six days shalt thou labor and do all thy work,' and rest on the seventh, and do not be willing to labor four or five days and then only half labor. Let every Latter-day Saint give value received for everything he gets, whether it be in work, or whatever he does." (Improvement Era, January, 1937.)

In some regions where this movement is going forward with great success the Priesthood quorums apply the principles of vocational guidance and help adjust their unemployed brethren into regular industry, and the opportunities of the quorum projects where they can be of greatest service. If the movement is to

be eminently successful the work directors must agree with Socrates that: "Not only is he idle who does nothing, but he is also idle who might be better amployed."

1. Tell of projects in other Stakes which have been successful in uniting the quorums and promoting goodwill among the brethren. 

2. Read to the class and discuss a few paragraphs from President Grant's “Message Concerning Make-Work Projects." (Improvement Era, June, 1936.) Discuss the wisdom of such a project. 

3. In the light of the Welfare Program, discuss Robert Burns' lines:

Man's inhumanity to man Makes countless thousands mourn. 

4. Discuss the Church Beautification Program. Note the 

Story of an Indian village. (Improvement Era, October, 1937.) 

5. Consider the wisdom of President Brigham Young's advice: "If I oppress you when I teach you to take care of yourselves, then shall I continue to oppress you. Have I ever taught you, by example or precept, to oppress the hireling in his wages? Never!

Can you justly accuse me of depriving the poor, or the stranger that is cast among us, of the means of obtaining the necessary comforts of life? You cannot. But I may be justly accused of making men, as far as possible, earn their living; of teaching them to supply their own wants, and to accumulate and gather round them wealth and independence by a persevering industry and a constant frugality and care of the temporal blessings God bestows upon them."´ (J. D. 10:249.)

Chapter 32 

THE QUORUM AND THE SOCIAL ORDER 

"Wo unto you rich men, that will not give your substance to the poor, for your riches will canker your souls; and this shall be your lamentation in the day of visitation, and of judgment, and of indignation-The harvest is past, the summer is ended, and my soul is not saved! "Wo unto you poor men, whose hearts are not broken, whose spirits are not contrite, and whose bellies are not satisfied, and whose hands are not stayed from laying hold upon other men's goods, whose eyes are full of greediness, who will not labor with your own hands!" (D. & C. 56:16-18.)

The Quorum and its Opportunities. The Priest hood quorum has a distinct advantage over all other agencies in society in carrying out Welfare work. 

There is no social or economic group in society that is comparable with the Priesthood quorum. 

The service within the quorum is voluntary and without financial remuneration. Its aim is the general welfare of the group, not the personal aggrandizement of the leaders at the expense of the members. 

As a class the quorum has occasion to study the field of welfare work and prepare themselves for the duties at hand. As they employ the knowledge thus obtained they become a service unit in the community. 

In all their activities, faith, rather than fear, must be the motive behind their efforts. Modern revelation, rather than artificial pressures, supplies the impelling force which inspires and directs the work of the quorum.

A realization of the power of the Priesthood and the methods of winning and retaining it (Doc. and Cov. 

121:34-40), makes the quorum a spiritual force for good in the social order.

An editorial in the Improvement Era, May, 1936, reads: "The Priesthood is a great brotherhood, the greatest on earth, dedicated to the accomplishment of the purposes of the Lord with respect to his children on earth. Every Priesthood bearer is needed for this task. Every man who receives the Priesthood accepts the obligation to use his new power to help his brother as himself. 

Within the Priesthood the strong must support the weak, so that all may more perfectly do their assigned tasks, and rise nearer to their divine destiny of joy."

From the Guide for Priesthood Quorums, published in 1930, p. 30, we read: "The effectiveness of a quorum will depend upon the condition of its individual members. Strong members make a strong quorum. 

The first consideration of a quorum must always be with respect to the welfare of the individuals composing it. The strong should be kept strong; the weak should be made strong; the sick should be ministered unto; the hungry must be fed; the unhappy must be made joyous. A quorum of the Priesthood that fails to seek out the needs of its members, and does not offer aid to them, misses its best opportunity to build a strong, united quorum for Priesthood service.'

A Model. The history of the Church serves as a model. The faithful members who are acquainted with the traditions of the past cannot cease to push forward this welfare work which was instituted under divine command. The word of the Lord during the infancy of the Church, "And the storehouse shall be kept by the consecrations of the

Church, and widows and orphans shall be provided for, as also the poor, becomes a fresh revelation.

When we realize that scores of welfare agencies have failed because they lacked the very things which lie within the quorums' grasp, it should be an incentive to enter the welfare work of the Church with as much

enthusiasm and devotion as the devout convert enters the Church. The voice of God established the order in the past; the voice of inspiration has called us to the task of new developments to meet the needs of the present and the future.

We are challenged to push forward until the purposes of the movement have been achieved. There is a spirit in the work which is higher than that of man. 

The highest expectations will be realized if we but follow the admonition of President Brigham Young: "Love mercy, eschew evil, be a savior to yourselves and to your families, and to your fellow beings, just as much as you possibly can... 

Go on until you are perfect, loving our neighbors more than we love ourselves."

The principles upon which the pioneers built may be applied to the changed social and economic conditions, as we strive to promote the general welfare of every member of the Church.

The Quorum and the Problem of Human Welfare. 

From a bulletin issued by the First Presidency, April, 1936, we quote: "Whether we shall now take care of our own Church members in need and how fully, depends wholly and solely upon the faith and works of the individual Church members. If each Church member meets his full duty and grasps his full opportunity for blessings; in so far as individual members fail in their duty and opportunity, by that much will the relief fall short." 

Upon each quorum member rests a double responsibility for the successful application of the principles of the Welfare Plan. The eternal principles of work, thrift, industry, and service are always a part of the social order, yet new solutions must be sought for the problems of the day. Each quorum is charged with the responsibility of developing some project or making

some adjustments whereby the welfare of their group is improved.

For example, many of the thought-provoking problems of human welfare need explanation and interpreting in the light of Gospel solutions. The problem of chronic unemployment as a by-product of industrial, laissez-faire society, needs not only an interpretation but challenges the quorum to find a solution, at least so far as the local quorum is concerned. 

Furthermore, what public services exist in each community, such as state employment service, local health clinics, county agents, agricultural experiment stations, and other Federal or private services, whose resources can be tapped to aid the quorum with its problems of welfare work? Projects in this direction could be developed in most communities, uniting the quorum members in a strong brotherhood, at the same time that the welfare of the group was promoted. 

Moreover, what of the decline of morals, the widespread use of liquor and tobacco, the disregard of the sacred Sabbath and its institutions, juvenile delinquency, drunken drivers, Saturday night dances, small attendance at the meetings sponsored by the Church? 

Surely there must be some imaginative genius in each quorum who can plan a project or suggest a scheme whereby his quorum can concern itself with local problems that relate to welfare.

A Challenge to the Quorum. The field of possibilities for quorum genius to develop new welfare devices along economic and social lines is unlimited. The present maladjustment in the social order challenges every quorum to devise a system whereby the social security and welfare of its members may be promoted. 

There were many members of the Church receiving

relief at the beginning of 1936, but surely there is a way out of the economic wilderness; a path to security and independence. The obligation rests with each quorum member to find that way out, so far as his quorum and community are concerned.

For example, in one community a man who had mastered the art of making ties, should not be left idle because every man and boy in the community was supplied with his product. Could not a system be developed whereby a score of workers could assist him in making a quantity of ties which could be distributed in every region of the Church? 

Are there not cases like this in every Stake where the talents of a few individuals might be put to use for the manufacture of a product which could be offered for exchange in every regional storehouse in the Church?

In the realm of Church Welfare there is no limit. 

He who builds below the stars builds too low. Each member is challenged to seek new products, new and better markets, better plans of sale and distribution.

Beyond the realm of money economy there must be developed a system of exchange which will enable one to barter the products of his leisure time for other necessities that he and his family may need. Distance must be ignored in this attempt to bring permanent security to every member of the Church. The makers of ties or ribbons need not become idle the moment everybody in the local area is supplied with those articles. 

We live in an age of rapid transportation. The luxury of refrigeration cars is not too costly when we consider the broad field of welfare work and the vast realm for an unlimited exchange of commodities. Security may be costly, yet insecurity costs far more.

Labor Exchange. Most quorums could concern themselves with the problem of an exchange of labor. 

A person needing a house painted, a barn built, a cellar made, or his house wired for electricity, or equipped with plumbing fixtures, or even music and art lessons for his children and instruction in domestic art for his wife, should be able to find employment at the Church Welfare Center and work for someone, who in turn pays either by products or labor. Units of work, instead of money, would become the element of exchange. 

Under a well organized plan this would help solve the problem of unemployment as it relieved the distress of those who had been out of work. Work shops could be provided where all people could find employment, yet their remuneration need not be limited to food and clothing.

In this system where units of work become the substitute for money, professional men could use such labor and products and thus give their services to people who otherwise could not afford the medical and cultural service they deserved. At least these suggestions may be considered by each priesthood quorum. 

In your consideration of these matters, other and better projects of similar nature may be suggested to you. 

Under this plan the blight of accepting charity would be removed and professional men would be able materially to reduce their price for service, since their time would be more fully occupied. 

In every ward there are many people who would be glad to work under such a system-people who would not go to the bishop and make their wants known, yet would be grateful to find employment even though at a fraction of the true value of their time. 

Is there not a capable music teacher in every

Stake who would gladly devote his time and talents for the instruction of his associates, being willing to accept commodities and labor in exchange for the same?

Until the depression retreats from the country, are there not artists, instructors, and skilled workers in numerous lines who would gladly accept employment under these conditions than to be unemployed for months at a time?

Would not the surgeon rather receive a few tons of coal or hay, or have his house painted by skilled workmen under this plan, rather than be expected to wait for his pay until normal times returned? Would not an instructor prefer working under such a system than to remain idle?

The horror of unemployment does not end with its physical features of idleness, hunger, and want, but has a serious reaction upon the mind. The active person who suddenly finds himself without work is on the verge of mental distraction as well as physical distress. 

He would certainly rather work than to remain idle. 

Is there not an opportunity for every quorum to make use of the talents in every locality and develop some projects that have great cultural value as well as the commonplace and secondary significance of providing employment.

Could your quorum accomplish anything in this direction, or must we wait until such blessings are thrust upon us from without?

Certain social needs, such as hospitalization, death benefit, recreational and health facilities, could be provided by cooperation on a non-profit service basis. 

In the winter months there will be hundreds of idle men and women who would rather work for a small remuneration than to remain idle. Each quorum is

charged with the responsibility of keeping its members employed. The present plan of each person contributing according to ability and distributing according to necessity, should be supplemented by other plans which will provide work as rapidly as possible and thus raise one's standard of living. The first objective of the Welfare Program has been achieved; we must now concern ourselves with raising the standard of living for those barely subsisting in our communities.

Each quorum must be on the alert to keep its members employed while they have a wealth of leisure time. 

Might some domestic manufacturing be developed in your community, the product of which might be exchanged in Canada, Mexico, or New York?

Moreover, if each employed member would arrange to give one day's labor to one unemployed member of the quorum, a large quorum could soon provide many weeks of labor for its few needy members. In this way an observing and helpful brother might discover special gifts and talents that might help to place the man in some permanent position. Vocational guidance should become a prominent feature with every quorum.

Such a responsibility has long been enjoined upon the Priesthood quorums. In 1930 the Guide for Priesthood Quorums said of this subject: "The Priesthood quorum bears a responsibility for the welfare of its members. The quorum, through properly appointed means, must seek out the needs of each individual member, and attempt to supply the needs that may be discovered. One great purpose of the 

Priesthood quorum is to develop a group of brethren who will care for each other's welfare. No two men have exactly the same needs, or must meet just the same difficulties. Therefore, each brother should be able to count on the support of his brethren who are not afflicted as he is. No quorum of the Priesthood is assuming the full obligation placed upon it by the Lord, which does not sufficiently

extend its activities to help each member in his individual need. The spirit of brotherhood is indispensable for quorum success.' ??

The Priesthood Quorum in Action. Under the 

Welfare Plan many quorums have done much to promote the general welfare of its needy members. The regional unit has taught the quorums to look beyond their own boundaries for a solution to this problem. 

Some quorums have fostered Church Beautification as a means of assisting the unemployed. In some wards projects amounting to $5,000 in value have been completed with an outlay of less than $800 in cash. 

In Salt Lake City a group of quorum members made a study of the occupational capabilities of their unemployed members. When it was learned that many of them were skilled farmers who had left the farm with the hope of improving their condition, it was decided to undertake a farming project. Suitable ground was leased near the city and the unemployed members of the quorum assigned to till the soil and market the products.

In Juab County 3,500 acres were donated as a means of furnishing employment under the new system. Three quorums united in the cultivation of this farm. In that community there were some able-bodied men who were entirely without work. Others had but low incomes; while many of them were struggling to provide for families and meet mortgage payments on land and machinery.

An unemployed member of the group was employed at a nominal salary to be caretaker of the property. He was given a home in which to live and irrigated land where he could raise a good garden. As he needed help he called upon the regular communities which had been appointed to cooperate with him. In

a short time a number were given employment; some in financial distress were relieved; and the entire community was benefited. More than 16,845 bushels of wheat were raised in 1938 on 950 acres of this land, through the voluntary efforts of quorum members. 

In a section where coal is mined another type of project has been developed. Coal miners who were members of the Church and were unemployed during the summer months, caught the spirit of the movement and gave their spare time to the Welfare Plan. As a means of reducing the period of unemployment they leased properties and mined coal as their contribution to the movement.

While the brethren in the coal mining districts were busy in the pits, their urban brethren were busy with manufacturing and canning projects. The city dwellers exchanged their furniture, clothing, canned food, and other fruits of their cooperative toil for the coal their distant brethren had mined during the slack season.

Building projects were sponsored by quorums and by the Church authorities. Scores of old chapels were renovated and many new ones constructed. In western 

Canada a number of Mormon families were transferred by Priesthood authorities from unproductive land to fertile areas where they could be self-supporting. 

In another Stake the depression had left many farmers without money to buy seed for spring planting. 

From the General Committee of the Welfare Plan a loan of $360 was secured for the purpose of purchasing seed potatoes. The cash income from the potato crop amounted to $2,800, in addition to the contribution each farmer made to the Ward Welfare Committee, consisting of one-third of his crop.

When the quorum members become thoroughly imbued with the spirit of the movement the world will have occasion to marvel at the practical aspects of Mormonism.

In Mexico, though no traveling missionaries from abroad were permitted to visit and instruct the local brethren in the establishment and operation of the movement, the members of the Church have united in community projects similar to those in the agricultural regions of the West.

In Canada there have been many successful cooperative enterprises pushed forward under the Church Welfare Plan. In one section a plan of rehabilitation has been carried out. At present many of the brethren are erecting a new chapel. An abandoned roundhouse containing $20,000 worth of materials was purchased for $900; and a crew of twenty-five men salvaged the materials for the building of a meeting-house. The entire membership of a ward united in a worthwhile project of Church Beautification.

The saints of Arizona have sent tons of grapefruit in exchange for canned fruits and vegetables produced in the north.

1. In the kingdom of God why cannot man escape his social responsibility? 

2. How does the human personality justify the belief that God is concerned about individuals? 

3. Why does the moral law in a wholesome society require that the strong support the weak? What practical situations grow out of this doctrine? 

4. How may the Church be justified in dealing with the economic affairs of its members? 

5. What are the values of cooperation, religiously, socially, economically?

6. List the agencies and principles in the Church which stress and promote economic welfare. 

7. Discuss F. W. Robertson's statement that "In the spiritual world a man is measured not by his genius, but by his likeness to God." 

8. Read the Improvement Era, February, 1937, page 70, "The 

Willing Worker and the Needy Worker."

Chapter 33 

THE UNIVERSALITY OF THE WELFARE PLAN 

"The will of God is practicable. It can be worked. If it is the will of God that life shall be, not impoverished, but enriched, then it is ours to find the way out of poverty. For a way out there must be."-Dr. John M. Versteeg. "Go on until we are perfect, loving our neighbor more than we love ourselves."-President Brigham Young.

The Priesthood Must Point the Way. Being beneficiaries of the Priesthood and members of a Church in which the social and temporal welfare of its members have always been cardinal principles, we are obliged to point the way to equality and brotherhood. 

In the words of Dr. Montgomery: "The Gospel does have a message of brotherhood and goodwill for just such times as these. The tree of life does indeed have leaves for the healing of the nations. What is to be said, however, of the church, if having the life-giving message, she shall fail to make it known? The very possession of such a priceless word imposes a trust and constitutes a challenge.' "'* The Priesthood holds the key which can unlock the portal to happiness and establish a brotherhood as perfect as that which existed among the Nephites when "they were not rich and poor... and there could not be a happier people among all the people who had been created by the hand of God." 

Professor Peabody hurls a challenge at the Priesthood quorums when he says of the Christian churches: "We find a gulf of alienation and misinterpretation lying be- *John H. Montgomery, The Social Message of Jesus, p. 154, The Abingdon Press.

tween the social movement and the Christian religion-a gulf so wide and deep as to recall the judgment of Schopenhauer that Christianity in its real attitude toward the world is absolutely remote from the spirit of the modern age.' 

Another challenge is issued by Dr. Charles A. Ellwood: "The social character of Christ's religion is best revealed by its program. It looked to the establishment of a social order in which 

God's will should be done-a kingdom of God which should make of humanity one large family with genuine love and good will among all its members. But this new order was not to be established by force or authority, but by a new life within the individual soul-a life redeemed from sin and brought into harmony with the divine will. "Some time, somewhere, men are bound to discover that the spiritual and social life of mankind is subject to law not less than the physical world. If we blunderingly persist in building our civilization upon the selfish interests of classes or national groups, then we may be sure that it is built upon the sand and that some other civilization than ours will profit by our mistake. "If we do not want some such calamity as this to befall us, then our churches must speedily transform themselves into educational institutions to educate the young and also the adults into Christ's way of life... The church must particularly devote itself to the education of the nobler emotions, the development of sympathy, and love, not limited by the barriers of class, nation, or race."t

Within our grasp is the revealed solution to this perplexing problem. The Priesthood must point the way out of the wilderness of want. 

Priesthood is Universal. Wherever faithful male members of the Church are found the Priesthood is in their midst to direct and magnify their ministry. The 

Gospel which has been borne to every nation, kindred, tongue and people, has the same administrative and departmental machinery in all parts of the world. In 

South Africa, New Zealand, Sweden, Denmark, the 

†Dr. Charles A. Ellwood, Man's Social Destiny, p. 212. The 

Cokesbury Press.

Priesthood quorums and the same organizations study similar lesson outlines, maintain the same activities, and follow the universal plan which the General Authorities have prescribed.

In many countries it is necessary for the missionaries to translate the instructions and lessons sent out from the headquarters of the Church. Climate, color or distance are no barriers in the unifying activities of the Church. Wherever the Priesthood is honored, there will all its appendages, activities, and blessings be manifest. 

Quorum Activity World-wide. In the distant branches of the Church the Priesthood quorums are organized and operated as they are in the organized 

Stakes, though the membership may be limited. Wherever Priesthood quorums and the Relief Society organizations are to be found, one will find the machinery for the Welfare Program. A priesthood quorum in foreign lands has its Personal Welfare committee, the members of which have long been interested in the social and economic welfare of its members. 

It is but a short step from the properly-functioning Priesthood quorum to the successful operation of the Welfare Plan. In every branch and mission of the 

Church the machinery may be set up for the welfare movement. As soon as the need arises or the order is given to enter into the program it can become as universal as the far-flung dominions of the Church. 

In foreign lands, as in the organized Stakes, the members of the Priesthood quorums have long been trained to have a deep and unselfish interest in the welfare of their brethren. In Armenia, as in Salt Lake

City, the various Priesthood quorums, the Relief Society, and other agencies within the Church have long

been concerned about the welfare of every member of the branch. In every country where there are enough members to constitute a branch of the Church the cooperative aims and ideals of the Priesthood quorums have been attempted for many years.

Welfare Principles Universal. The principles of the Welfare Plan are universal. The far-reaching effects of this movement, if its principles are applied in every Latter-day Saint home, may be understood if a careful analysis is made of the counsel given by President J. Reuben Clark, Jr., in a General Conference held in April, 1937. "What may we as a people and as individuals do for ourselves to prepare to meet this oncoming disaster, which God in his wisdom may not turn aside from us? "First, and above and beyond everything else, let us live righteously, fearing God and keeping His commandments, that we may in part claim his blessing as of right, and not as of mercy only. 

Along this way only lies happiness and salvation. For the Lord has said: 

Wherefore, fear not even unto death; for in this world your joy is not full. 

Therefore, care not for the body, neither the life of the body; but care for the soul, and for the life of the soul. 

And seek the face of the Lord always, that in patience ye may possess your souls, and ye shall have eternal life." (D. & C. 101:36-38.) "Let us avoid debt as we would avoid a plague; where we are now in debt let us get out of debt; if not today, then tomorrow. "Let us straitly and strictly live within our incomes, and save a little. "Let every head of every household see to it that he has on hand enough food and clothing, and, where possible, fuel also, for at least a year ahead. You of small means put your money in foodstuffs and wearing apparel, not in stocks and bonds; you of large means will think you know how to care for yourselves, but I may venture to suggest that you do not speculate. Let every head_of every household aim to own his own home, free from mortgage. Let every man who has a garden spot, garden it; every man who owns a farm, farm it. "Let us again clothe ourselves with these proved and sterling

virtues-honesty, truthfulness, chastity, sobriety, temperance, industry and thrift; let us discard all covetousness and greed. 

Commenting on this sound counsel the Los Angeles Times made the following comment in its editorial columns: "Apparently they are not trying to frighten anybody, but the

Mormons are getting ready for the next depression. As a unit they are preparing storehouses filled with food and clothing to 'take care of their own.' "Their leaders say that they have no idea when the next era of bad times will strike, but that it will come. This is not the view of alarmists. It is a sensible precaution against the hazards of the future. "The idea is old, that of laying up for lean years in times of plenty. But ordinary people and ordinary nations do not act upon the obvious common sense of such a program. They live from hand to mouth, with no practical preparation for the perils ahead. "This country at the moment has every outward indication of prosperity. Yet, instead of laying up any substance, it is plunging headlong into deeper debt and assuming profligate obligations. It seemingly has learned nothing from the past. "The Mormons have been noted for their canniness and ability to presage the trend of the times. Like the busy ant, they can be watched profitably. They know what they are doing. Their example will bear following."

It now becomes the responsibility and obligation of every Church member, no matter in what country he may live, to adopt such a course as would put him in a position to meet an economic crisis and to ride the storm successfully until proper adjustments can be made. Latter-day Saints would do well in these days of uncertainty, of economic upheavals and social unrest, to follow the instructions and guidance of the leaders of the Church, who are sustained and upheld as prophets, seers, and revelators. 

Future of the Welfare Plan. Since we shall al ways have the poor with us, the present system of welfare work will be as permanent as the Priesthood

quorums. This movement in its narrow sense is concerned with the relief of poverty, but its ultimate aim is to raise the standard of living for everyone near the borderline of want. 

The First Presidency, in commenting on the future of the Welfare Plan, said: "God's law has always been "Thou shalt love thy neighbor as thyself.' The Church Security Plan will help us immeasurably to fulfill this law. The plan has wrought a great work. It is a work that must be done if we are to be what we are pretending to bebrothers and sisters in the Gospel. "It is my testimony to you that President Grant was inspired to begin this work and this plan, and it stands on an equality with the Relief Society, the Primary, the Sunday School and the M. I. A. 

And furthermore, just as these organizations have grown and developed with the experience and needs of the people, and so are not what they were at the beginning, so will this plan grow and develop, and if the time comes when we do not need it, we will not use it, but until that time comes, this will be a permanent thing. "This world is moving into a new era. I know of no responsible authority who challenges the forecast that within the next few years we shall, in the normal course, suffer a depression far more serious, affecting intimately far greater numbers of people, than the one we are now finishing.'

While methods of dealing with changing problems of the future may be modified, the principles underlying the Welfare Plan, as has been explained in previous chapters, are fundamental doctrines of the Church and will remain the same, just as Priesthood quorums and auxiliary organizations will continue to safeguard the welfare of the members of the Church. The present program is a unifying process by which the relationship of Church units to each other is clearly defined regardless of territorial boundaries. Improved methods of transportation and modern systems of communication have been helpful in cementing the stakes of the Church together into a universal brotherhood although separated by great distances in many cases.

A careful study of the plan will explain the reason why organized wards and stakes are able to function more effectively in the Program than are missions with scattered branch organizations. As rapidly as groups of branch organizations within the missions can be organized as stakes, a stake organization is perfected with resulting efficiency in the Welfare Plan. To just the extent that missions are organized to resemble the organized stakes of Zion, to just that extent may they hope to function successfully in carrying out the provisions of the plan.

This system will help unite the members of the Priesthood quorums, making of each quorum a brotherhood. It will correlate the work of the Priesthood and the Relief Society. It will awaken the quorum to its full responsibility and remind each member that he is his brother's keeper. Quorum responsibility and activity are so quickened and sublimated by this plan that it would be a serious loss if it were ever discontinued even in times of prosperity. 

In the words of President Brigham Young: "I have Zion in my view constantly. We are not going to wait for angels, or for Enoch and his company, to come and build up Zion, but we are going to build it. We will raise our wheat, build our houses, fence our farms, plant our vineyards and orchards, and produce everything that will make our bodies comfortable and happy, and in this manner we intend to build up Zion on the earth and purify it and cleanse it from all pollutions. Let there be an hallowed influence go from us over all things over which we have any power; over the soil we cultivate, over the houses we build, and over everything we possess; and if we cease to hold fellowship with that which is corrupt and establish the Zion of God in our hearts, in our own houses, in our cities, and throughout our country, we shall untimately overcome the earth, for we are the lords of the earth; and, instead of thorns and thistles, every useful plant that is good for the food of man and to beautify and adorn will spring from its bosom." (J. D., 9:284) "We never ought to be without three or five years of provisions on hand." (J. D., 3:196)

Unless the plan of brotherhood can be made permanent and universal we must cease to sing with complacent disregard of the facts, the hymn: "We are not divided; all one body we."

The Church Welfare Plan is not based on an idealistic utopian philosophy. It is not an effort to capture government or economic institutions. It represents a determined effort by God-fearing men and women, directed by revelation, to seek out and apply the best available thought and knowledge to the basic problems of economic and social welfare. The spiritualizing influence of Priesthood responsibility symbolizes the universality of what has been and must increasingly be, this determined effort.

1. Discuss Dr. Finney's assertion: "If the kingdom of God is to come in the earth, it must first come in our hearts. Social salvation can only be realized through individual salvation. The social awakening must be realized by a revival of personal religion." 

2. Do you agree with Dr. Ellwood that "Crisis in life will not cease through human progress, nor will man come to need less the power of self-sacrifice. The world will never cease to need, in other words, clean, high-minded, self-devoted, self-sacrificing human living. Dr. John H. Montgomery insists "In all crises, lack of knowledge and apathy are the twin enemies of progress.' 

If this is true, why is it the duty of the Church to make the 

Welfare Program permanent and universal? 

4. Have a member of the class read the Improvement Era for

May, 1937, and make a report of the itemized contributions of the Welfare Program for the first year of its existence. 

5. On the front page of the New York Times, October 29, 1936, the following appeared: "Mormons soon to open Storehouse for Needy." "Bounteous storehouses filled, in a program to take all unemployed members off relief,' will be opened to the needy Nov. 1, by the Latter-day Saint Church. "The budget system will govern distribution of food, clothing and medical supplies to about 15,000 persons reported eligible.

"A dozen strategically situated storehouses were crammed with preserved food and home-made clothing throughout the summer. 

The Church created work projects and ward-farms, employed receiving certificates redeemable in goods. 'A similar program is planned for next year." 

Point out why it would be unwise for the Church to discontinue the Plan with the return of prosperity.
